2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Executive Summary
Water and sewerage services are essential for the wellbeing of the
Tasmanian community. Customers rely on these services every day, and it is
critical that TasWater’s infrastructure and service delivery continue to meet
the State’s requirements, align with community expectations for social and
environmental outcomes, and provide value for customers. TasWater must
maintain and renew its asset base, deliver infrastructure necessary to meet
future demand, and respond to emerging challenges such as climate change.
The Tasmanian Economic Regulator is responsible for determining maximum
prices and service standards for TasWater’s regulated services, with the aim
of ensuring prices reflect prudent and efficient expenditure while balancing
customers’ interests with TasWater’s financial sustainability.
This 2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final
Report sets out the Economic Regulator’s final decisions on prices, service
standards and revenue allowances for the fifth regulatory period (1 July 2026
to 30 June 2030), under the Water and Sewerage Industry Act 2008 and
associated regulations.

An ambitious proposal and unprecedented public
engagement
TasWater’s proposed Price and Service Plan for the fifth regulatory period
(PSP5) sought approval for the largest expenditure program and price
increase for Tasmania’s regulated water and sewerage services in its history.
TasWater proposed total expenditure of almost $2.8 billion over four
years, including $1.67 billion in capital expenditure and around $1.11 billion
in operating expenditure. This represents a 61 per cent increase in total
expenditure compared to the previous regulatory period.
TasWater’s proposal represented a notional allowable revenue of
approximately $2.3 billion over four years, up from $1.5 billion in the previous
regulatory period. Annual price increases of 11.5 per cent would be
required to support this level of expenditure.
TasWater also proposed spreading cost recovery across two price periods,
with prices rising by 8.8 per cent a year over the next four years and a
minimum price increase of 5.8 per cent a year in the following period. The
Economic Regulator believes this is inconsistent with the legislative
framework and did not accept this approach.

TasWater’s proposal prompted an unprecedented level of engagement. The
Economic Regulator received a record number of submissions from
households, businesses, industry groups, councils and community
organisations. Submissions broadly recognised that TasWater must invest
more to address ageing assets and environmental non-compliance, but
raised strong concerns about affordability, deliverability and whether
TasWater had demonstrated that the full scale and timing of its proposal was
justified.

TasWater’s existing asset base and why it matters for
prices
A central issue in this investigation has been the condition, accuracy and
treatment of TasWater’s existing asset base, which is the large portfolio of
water and sewerage assets that was transferred across from councils. Much
of this infrastructure is decades old and underpins service risks, compliance
challenges and investment needs.
During the investigation, the Economic Regulator identified widespread and
material issues with TasWater’s existing regulated asset base. This included
assets that had been retired, replaced or decommissioned remaining in the
regulated asset base, meaning that customers would have continued to pay
for these assets. The Economic Regulator also identified inconsistent asset
lives and depreciation practices, incomplete or unsupported asset data, and
low levels of asset disposals despite TasWater’s extensive capital investment
program.
Assets in the regulated asset base earn both a return on capital and
regulatory depreciation, which are recovered from customers through prices.
The Economic Regulator has undertaken a substantial program of work in
this investigation to correct and strengthen the integrity of TasWater’s
regulated asset base. Given the scale of the legacy regulated asset base,
extent of the issues and known data limitations, the Economic Regulator has
also recognised that it will not be possible to identify and resolve all of the
issues. To provide a workable and transparent basis for moving forward, the
ii

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Economic Regulator has supported a “line‑in‑the‑sand” approach and a
transition to applying weighted average asset lives for depreciation from the
start of the fifth regulatory period.

Staged investment and a more moderate price increase
TasWater’s proposal sought total notional allowable revenue of $2.25 billion
over the fifth regulatory period. It is the notional allowable revenue figure that
forms the basis of TasWater’s price setting framework. Prices are calculated
by first determining a notional allowable revenue, which is made up of
building blocks including operating costs, regulatory depreciation, a return on
capital and inflation. The notional allowable revenue is then divided by the
forecast demand for services to set the maximum prices TasWater can
charge customers each year.
The Economic Regulator undertook a two-step process to determine
TasWater’s notional allowable revenue. Firstly, the Economic Regulator
made what it believed where necessary corrections to TasWater’s
methodology and calculations. These corrections reduced the total notional
allowable revenue to $2.17 billion.
Following completion of the first step, the Economic Regulator then assessed
the prudency and efficiency of each of TasWater’s proposals (informed by
expert reviews and feedback from consultation). Based on this assessment,
the Economic Regulator has determined a total notional allowable revenue of
$1.95 billion for the fifth regulatory period.
This results in a uniform annual price change of 5.7 per cent, compared to
11.5 per cent under TasWater’s proposal.
Figure E.1: TasWater’s proposed notional allowable revenue, corrected notional
allowable revenue and the Economic Regulator’s notional allowable revenue for the
fifth regulatory period ($m, nominal)

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

iii

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Together, these decisions reflect the Economic Regulator’s approach to
correcting errors and inconsistencies in TasWater’s modelling and treatment
of the regulated asset base and supporting higher levels of investment where
it is justified. These decisions also moderate price impacts where expenditure
is not sufficiently evidenced, deliverable or efficient for recovery in the fifth
regulatory period, and improve the quality and transparency of the
information and processes relied on for future determinations.

What has changed from the Draft Report
While the overall approach set out in the Draft Report has largely been
retained, a number of important decisions have been refined or adjusted in
the Final Report in response to submissions received through the
consultation process.
Compared to the Draft Report, the Final Report results in a higher, but still
moderated, notional allowable revenue ($1.95 billion, compared to
$1.89 billion) and a higher uniform annual price increase (5.7 per cent,
compared to 4.3 per cent), reflecting updated inputs and refinements to
several key building blocks.

Note: While the Economic Regulator’s corrections and decisions have reduced TasWater’s annual
price increase to 5.7 per cent, the treatment of customer connections will enable TasWater to collect
approximately $25 million of revenue from this unregulated service. Likewise, the
Economic Regulator’s decision to extend the timing of TasWater’s proposed capex program over the
sixth regulatory period means that TasWater will be able to collect the associated revenue from
regulated customers over a longer period.

iv

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Final Report includes a number of targeted refinements following
consideration of feedback received from TasWater and other stakeholders.
The key changes include:
❑

a revised approach to TasWater’s existing asset base and regulatory
depreciation, which establishes a clear “line‑in‑the‑sand” at the end of
the fourth regulatory period and applies a consistent existing asset life
of 43.86 years to address data limitations while correcting material
errors and removing unused assets;

❑

updated inflation forecasts and Reserve Bank of Australia data, which
have resulted in a modest increase in TasWater’s operating
expenditure and escalation of the regulated asset base;

❑

updated interest rate forecasts, which have resulted in an increase in
the return on capital TasWater receives;

❑

a smoother capex funding profile to better support the deliverability of
TasWater’s capital investment program; and

❑

a transition towards more usage‑based charges over the regulatory
period, with water and sewerage variable charges increasing faster
than the average annual price increase of 5.7 per cent, and smaller
increases to corresponding fixed charges.

Overall, the changes from the Draft Report to the Final Report reflect the
Economic Regulator’s commitment to responding to evidence and
stakeholder feedback, while maintaining a consistent approach in applying
the regulatory framework.

Operational expenditure: funded, but with stronger efficiency
targets
TasWater proposed operating expenditure of around $1.11 billion, a
28.7 per cent increase compared to the previous allowance. The Economic
Regulator engaged Sapere to review TasWater’s opex and, taking into
account Sapere’s findings and stakeholder concerns about efficiency and
cost control, reduced TasWater’s proposed opex by around $100 million to a
final allowance of $1.02 billion.
Table E.3: TasWater’s proposed opex for PSP5 and approved opex allowance for

Key drivers of this reduction include:
❑

price-neutral corrections (including the treatment of customer
connections and bad and doubtful debts);

❑

increased allocation of overheads to unregulated services;

❑

lower salary escalation; and

❑

a higher level of efficiency over the period.

Capital expenditure: a material increase, but staged for
deliverability and affordability
TasWater proposed a gross capital program of almost $1.7 billion, with a
$100 million efficiency target. The Economic Regulator engaged AtkinsRéalis
to assess TasWater’s capex program. While recognising TasWater’s need to
continue investing to address legacy issues and compliance risks,
AtkinsRéalis identified that TasWater’s business case maturity, justification
for timing, and linkages between investments and customer outcomes
require strengthening.
After considering expert advice and stakeholder submissions, the Economic
Regulator approved a capex allowance of approximately $1.18 billion,
representing a 36.3 per cent increase on the previous period. This decision
reflects the Economic Regulator’s view that it is not prudent or efficient to
attempt to deliver TasWater’s full proposed capital program within a single
four-year regulatory period. The allowance therefore assumes some project
delivery extends into the early years of the next regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator emphasises that the capex allowance used for
setting prices does not constrain TasWater in setting its priorities or
responding to its assessment of the needs of the network. Decisions on the
scale, timing and prioritisation of capital works remain matters for TasWater’s
Board and management, informed by the requirements of its technical
regulators.

Return on capital and inflation: benchmark settings and updated
forecasts
TasWater earns a return on its regulated assets to support financial
sustainability and the ongoing delivery of essential services. The Economic
Regulator reviewed the key parameters used to calculate the weighted
average cost of capital and considers an A‑ credit rating appropriate for the
fifth regulatory period. The Economic Regulator received comments in
submissions on the cost of capital and proposed that the approach to
calculating the weighted average cost of capital be reviewed ahead of the
next price determination investigation.
The inflation forecasts in this report have been updated to reflect the latest
Reserve Bank of Australia forecasts (published in February 2026).
Figure E.2: Contribution of each cost component to the Economic Regulator’s
notional allowable revenue for the fifth regulatory period

A transitional approach to changing pricing structures
TasWater proposed significant changes to the structure of its prices as part
of its PSP5, with the aim of making charges more cost-reflective and to give
its customers more control over their bills. In particular, TasWater proposed
shifting a greater proportion of customer bills to usage-based charges and
reducing fixed charges. This included increases to the variable component of
water prices, the introduction of a new fixed and variable charging structure
for sewerage and a new approach to trade waste charging.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

vii

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator is generally supportive of the direction of these
proposed tariff reforms, recognising that more cost-reflective pricing can
encourage efficient water use and better align charges with underlying cost
drivers. The feedback received through consultation also suggested that
there was significant customer support for more usage-based charging.
However, the Economic Regulator found that TasWater had not yet
demonstrated that its proposed pricing structures were sufficiently supported
by robust cost modelling, transparent methodologies, or comprehensive
analysis of customer impacts. In particular, further work is required to ensure
that fixed and variable components accurately reflect the costs of providing
services, and that reforms do not unfairly disadvantage particular customer
groups, including some residential customers, tenants and non-residential
users.
Given these limitations, the Economic Regulator has not approved
TasWater’s full suite of proposed tariff reforms at this time. Instead, the
Economic Regulator has adopted a transitional approach to prices and
charges for the fifth regulatory period, with the aim of balancing
cost-reflectivity, fairness and customer impacts, while providing TasWater
time to improve its cost modelling and consultation process ahead of future
price determinations.

To address the risk of double-dipping, the Economic Regulator has applied a
specific discount on sewerage services for trade waste customers, to ensure
they are not charged twice for the treatment of the same discharge. This
approach balances cost-reflectivity with fairness and affordability, while
recognising that TasWater’s sewerage cost modelling and discharge data
require further refinement before a more heavily usage-based structure can
be supported.
The Economic Regulator will also require TasWater to maintain clear review,
complaint and dispute mechanisms throughout the transitional period, to
ensure customers can request a review of their sewerage discharge factors
where appropriate.

viii

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator’s decisions reduce the impact of TasWater’s
proposals on customer bills. For an average residential household, the
estimated increase in 2026-27 is around $80, compared with $127 under
TasWater’s proposed expenditure. It would have been $91 under TasWater’s
revenue deferral approach.
For small business customers, the Economic Regulator’s decisions result in
an estimated increase of $172 in 2026-27, compared with $255 under
TasWater’s full expenditure proposals.
These outcomes reflect both the lower overall price increase approved by the
Economic Regulator and the decision to phase changes to tariff structures
more gradually.

made assumptions, including how much water the customer uses; the size of their water connection;
and how many equivalent tenements they were charged for in 2025-26. The Economic Regulator has
assumed that the business has a trade waste agreement in place.

Trade waste charging arrangements
While the Economic Regulator is generally supportive of TasWater’s
proposed new trade waste charging approach, further work is needed before
any changes are implemented. Feedback from large customers indicated
limited awareness and understanding of the proposed changes, and further
work is required to reconcile trade waste charges with the new sewerage
charging framework to avoid double charging.
Accordingly, the Economic Regulator has decided to maintain the existing
trade waste charging arrangements for the fifth regulatory period. TasWater
is expected to undertake further consultation and analysis during the period
so that a revised trade waste charging framework can be properly considered
for the sixth regulatory period.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

ix

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Developer charges
TasWater also proposed significant changes to its approach for calculating
headworks charges, which would have resulted in substantial price
increases. The Economic Regulator did not accept TasWater’s initial
proposal, finding that the changes were not adequately justified and had not
been subject to sufficient consultation.
TasWater provided additional information when it resubmitted its proposed
headworks charges on 13 March 2026. After assessing the revised proposal
and feedback from stakeholders, the Economic Regulator has decided that
headworks charges of $3 025 for water and $1 797 for sewerage for a 20 mm
connection will apply in 2026‑27.

A stronger foundation for the future
Following completion of the 2026 Price Determination Investigation, the
Economic Regulator has agreed to TasWater’s request for a comprehensive
review of its regulatory framework. The Economic Regulator expects to
undertake meaningful engagement with customers and stakeholders
throughout the review, to support consideration of potential improvements
ahead of the next price determination.

Next steps
Following release of the Final Report and Price Determination, TasWater
must submit a revised Price and Service Plan for the Economic Regulator’s
approval. Subject to approval, the prices and service standards set out in
TasWater’s final PSP will commence on 1 July 2026.

Return on capital

5.7

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Issues outside of the scope of the
investigation
A number of submissions on the Draft Report raised matters that are outside
of the Economic Regulator’s statutory role or control, or relate to broader
State Government policy. This section summarises those issues to
acknowledge the feedback received during consultation.
Where appropriate, the Economic Regulator will also advise the Treasurer
and the Minister for Primary Industries and Water of the level of public
interest in these issues, as indicated during public consultation.

Affordability
Almost 50 per cent of submissions raised concerns about affordability,
highlighting cost-of-living pressures and the impacts on households,
businesses and communities. While many submitters acknowledged the
need for sustained investment to address ageing infrastructure,
environmental compliance and growth pressures, they expressed concern
that price increases may exacerbate cost-of-living pressures, particularly for
low-income and vulnerable customers. Consumer advocacy groups
emphasised that water and sewerage services are essential services and
that affordability should remain a central consideration in regulatory decision
making. Several submissions supported the Economic Regulator’s role in
moderating TasWater’s proposed price increases and considered the Draft
Report’s revised price path more balanced than TasWater’s original proposal.
Non-residential stakeholders also raised concerns about flow-on impacts on
business viability, employment and regional activity. Several submissions
emphasised that affordability impacts cannot be addressed through pricing
decisions alone, and pointed to hardship programs, concessions and clear
communication as essential complements.
“TasCOSS recognises TasWater’s need to invest in ageing water and
sewerage infrastructure. However, this need must also be considered in the
current environment of persistently high cost of living pressures due to inflation
and interest rates rising and real wages falling in Tasmania.”1

The broader socio-economic questions about affordability and distribution
policy are primarily matters for the State Government. However, the
Economic Regulator recognises the importance of mitigating the impact of
price increases on customers. Within its remit, the Economic Regulator must
1 Submission from The Tasmanian Council of Social Service Inc, page 4.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

xix

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

balance affordability impacts with TasWater’s financial sustainable and the
need to fund efficient investment to maintain safe and reliable services.
The Economic Regulator’s legislated role is to ensure that water and
sewerage prices are fair and reflective of efficient costs. This role is not to
make services artificially inexpensive, but to set prices consistent with that
would be expected from efficient operations in a competitive market, while
avoiding unnecessary cost burdens on customers.
Although Tasmania generally does not face significant water-sourcing
constraints, this does not necessarily result in lower prices, as the majority of
costs relate to the construction, operation, and maintenance of networks and
supporting infrastructure rather than water supply itself. Tasmania’s small
and dispersed population, together with its topography, can result in higher
unit costs for service provision in some areas, which must be taken into
account when assessing affordability and setting prices.

Tasmanian Government concessions and support arrangements
Local government and community stakeholders emphasised the importance
of effective and accessible concession and hardship support for vulnerable
customers. Some submissions raised perceived gaps in eligibility, such as
renters who bear costs but may not be eligible for support under the current
concession framework. A number of submissions recommended
strengthening existing concession and hardship programs, and/or
establishing additional support mechanisms.
“The Salvation Army recommends that a water and sewerage hardship fund be
established to assist community members who are experiencing hardship to
pay their water and sewerage utility bills.”2

The Economic Regulator recognises the important role that targeted support
measures have in mitigating the impacts of price increases on customers
experiencing financial hardship. While the Economic Regulator notes the
interest in the concession framework that stakeholders have expressed, the
provision, eligibility criteria and funding of concessions are matters for the
State Government and sit outside the Economic Regulator’s statutory
jurisdiction.

Dividend payments to council owners
Many submissions questioned the appropriateness of TasWater continuing to
pay dividends while customers faced material price increases and the
business requires significant capital investment. While generally recognising
that dividend decisions sit outside the Economic Regulator’s remit, submitters
argued that it is reasonable to expect owners to prioritise reinvestment in
2 Submission from The Salvation Army, page 5

xx

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

infrastructure over dividend extraction during this period. A number of
individual and business submissions explicitly called for dividends to be
reduced, suspended or deferred, contending that continued dividend
payments are inconsistent with claims of financial constraint and risk shifting
the costs of past underinvestment disproportionately onto customers.
Dividends were therefore raised as an important contextual issue when
considering the appropriateness of price paths and the allocation of financial
burdens between customers and owners. Some submitters raised concerns
that ongoing dividend payments undermine community confidence in
TasWater’s pricing proposals and the broader reform narrative, particularly
where councils continue to receive financial returns while customers face
rising bills.
“The Regulator’s draft decision provides a fair and equitable return to councils.
It would be inappropriate for customers to face higher bills so that owners may
receive elevated returns when the historic under-investment in essential water
and sewerage assets is precisely what now necessitates sustained catch-up
investment.” 3

“PSP5 highlights a broader question about how infrastructure costs are shared
between current customers, future customers and system owners. Given the
history of ownership and reform, CfGH encourages continued reflection
between TasWater, owner councils and the State Government on dividend
arrangements during this period of sustained capital expenditure.” 4

While the Economic Regulator notes the scale and scope of the feedback
received in relation to dividend payments, dividends are not a matter within
the Economic Regulator’s control. Dividend payments are determined by
TasWater’s Board in line with the requirements of the Water and Sewerage
Corporations Act 2012, and the Economic Regulator has no role in relation to
the administration of that Act. Decisions about whether dividends are paid,
how funds are allocated and the level of any dividend payments are
ultimately matters for TasWater’s Board.

Executive salaries
Three submissions from residential customers raised concerns about the
level of remuneration paid to TasWater’s CEO and Executives in the context
of proposed price increases and cost-of-living pressures. These submitters
questioned how executive remuneration levels could be reconciled with
significant increases in customer bills during a period of heightened financial
stress for households, with some drawing comparisons to the salaries of
senior public office holders and suggesting a disconnect between
management and customer circumstances. Several submitters argued that
3 Submission from the Hon Eric Abetz, Treasurer, page 1.
4 Submission from the Committee for Greater Hobart, page 7.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

xxi

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

executive and senior management remuneration should be considered as
part of TasWater’s overall cost base, and suggested that restraint in
executive pay, or reductions in senior management costs, could contribute to
moderating price increases or easing the burden on customers. In this
context, executive remuneration was raised as an example of potential
efficiency improvements prior to seeking higher prices.
“The levels of executive and senior management salaries provides a clue as to
the reason these service industry providers may not be able to relate to the
current financial pain and adversity being experienced by their customers…”5

The Economic Regulator notes the feedback received regarding the
remuneration of TasWater’s executive leadership and acknowledges that
executive pay is an issue of public interest for a government‑owned provider
of essential services. However, decisions relating to executive remuneration,
including salary and incentive arrangements, sit outside the Economic
Regulator’s statutory remit and are matters for TasWater’s Board and its
shareholders. In this determination, the Economic Regulator does not assess
or approve individual remuneration outcomes. Instead, its role is to assess
TasWater’s proposed operating expenditure at an aggregate level to ensure
it is prudent and efficient for the delivery of regulated services. Executive
remuneration is considered only insofar as it forms part of TasWater’s overall
cost base and does not extend to judgments about the appropriateness of
remuneration levels or governance arrangements.

5 Submission from Monica Antel.

xxii

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Introduction
Role of the Economic Regulator
The independent Tasmanian Economic Regulator is responsible for
regulating TasWater’s:
prices and service standards for drinking water services for all
customers
prices and service standards for sewerage services for all
customers
service standards for trade waste services for all customers and
prices for some customers (generally small to medium-sized
businesses).
The Economic Regulator’s roles and responsibilities in relation to the
regulation of TasWater are contained in the Water and Sewerage Industry
Act 2008 (Industry Act) and associated regulations. This framework requires
the Economic Regulator to protect the long-term interests of customers, while
enabling TasWater to provide efficient and sustainable water and sewerage
services, in a safe and environmentally responsible manner.
The regulatory framework requires the Economic Regulator to consider a
number of matters including:
❑

TasWater’s health, safety and environmental obligations;

❑

the long-term efficiency of TasWater’s infrastructure investment
program, with the aim of keeping the cost of providing water and
sewerage services as low as possible over time;

❑

encouraging the efficient pricing for services;

❑

how quickly prices are changed and how that affects customers;

❑

ensuring that services meet appropriate standards; and

❑

avoiding duplication with TasWater’s other regulators.

The Economic Regulator is also tasked with evaluating and reporting on the
performance of Tasmania’s water and sewerage sector. It publishes the
State of the Industry Report each year, to provide transparency and
accountability for sector outcomes.
Licensing arrangements, including issuing, reviewing and administering
TasWater’s licence, are also the responsibility of the Economic Regulator.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

1

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Purpose of this investigation
The Economic Regulator is required to make a price determination, which
sets the prices for regulated water and sewerage services for the next
regulatory period. On 26 June 2024, the Economic Regulator declared that
the fifth regulatory period would commence on 1 July 2026 and conclude on
30 June 2030.
Under regulation 21(2) of the Water and Sewerage Industry (Pricing and
Related Matters) Regulations 2021 (Pricing Regulations), the Economic
Regulator is required to undertake an investigation prior to making (or
varying) a price determination.
The investigation follows a propose and respond approach, based on the
information provided by TasWater in its proposed PSP.
TasWater submitted its proposed PSP5 to the Economic Regulator on
30 June 2025, setting out its proposed prices, service standards, and
investment programs for the upcoming regulatory period.
This Final Report and the Price Determination are based on the
Economic Regulator’s analysis of TasWater’s proposed PSP5, along with
supplementary material, together with the submissions received during public
consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report.

Role of TasWater’s other industry regulators
Regulatory oversight of the water and sewerage industry in Tasmania
extends beyond the role and functions of the Economic Regulator.
Environmental protection, public health and dam safety are regulated by the
Director of the Environment Protection Authority (EPA), the Director of Public
Health and the Department of Natural Resources and Environment Tasmania
(NRE Tas), acting as Delegate for dam safety.
The Economic Regulator has also consulted with these regulators to
understand their required compliance actions for the fifth regulatory period.
Further details on the Economic Regulator’s engagement with these
regulators is provided in Appendix 4.

Context for the investigation
TasWater’s proposed Price and Service Plan
As required under the Industry Act, TasWater provided its proposed PSP5 to
the Economic Regulator on 30 June 2025. TasWater’s proposals set out in
this plan are ambitious and complex.
2

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater’s proposed PSP5 represents the largest infrastructure program in
TasWater’s history. Covering the period from 1 July 2026 to 30 June 2030,
the proposal includes almost $1.7 billion for treatment plant upgrades, dam
safety improvements and infrastructure renewals. This proposed capital
expenditure program is 94 per cent larger than the total capital expenditure
allowance approved by the Economic Regulator for the fourth regulatory
period of $861.9 million.
In addition, TasWater has also sought a 29 per cent increase to its
operational expenditure. This proposal includes the replacement of four of
TasWater’s core IT systems over four years. When combined with the
proposed capital investment program, this represents a total of approximately
$2.8 billion of expenditure over four years.
An 11.2 per cent price increase each year for the next four years would be
required to support TasWater’s planned expenditure as outlined in its
proposed PSP5. TasWater acknowledged that this is unlikely to be affordable
for its customers. In response, it proposed to defer the recovery of
$113.6 million to the following regulatory period.6 This would embed a
5.4 per cent price increase from 1 July 2030, in addition to any increase that
would be required to support TasWater’s expenditure over the sixth
regulatory period.
Table I.2: Price increases for the fifth and sixth regulatory period, under TasWater’s
PSP5 proposal
Regulatory period

TasWater’s proposed price increase

Fifth regulatory period (1 July 2026 to
30 June 2030)

8.8 per cent per year

Sixth Regulatory Period (from 1 July 2030)

5.4 per cent per year to be added to the
proposed PSP6 increase

On 30 September 2025, TasWater provided the Economic Regulator with an
update to its PSP5 proposal. Adjustments to its proposal equate to annual
price increases of 11.5 per cent. Under TasWater’s proposal to defer the
recovery of some revenue to the sixth regulatory period, this would embed a
5.8 per cent price increase from 1 July 2030.
TasWater has also proposed a number of structural changes to its regulated
prices including:
❑

shifting the balance between fixed and variable water charges;

❑

introducing new charging frameworks for both sewerage and trade
waste services; and

❑

a revised approach for calculating developer charges.

6 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal, page 125.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

3

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The number of changes proposed by TasWater has added a significant level
of complexity to the Economic Regulator’s investigation.

Challenges facing TasWater
TasWater operates in an environment where ageing infrastructure, a
dispersed population and regulatory compliance present ongoing challenges.
Much of Tasmania’s water and sewerage network was inherited from
councils and is decades old and has required significant investment to meet
modern standards.
Achieving environmental compliance across 110 sewage treatment plants
continues to be a challenge for TasWater. TasWater has reported that since
its formation in 2013, the proportion of its Level 2 sewage treatment plants
that are considered to be compliant has increased from 15 per cent to
26 per cent. However, of its Level 1 sewage treatment plants, only
three per cent are considered to be compliant.7
TasWater also continues to face large water losses from its drinking water
system, with almost 22 per cent of its potable water unaccounted for, making
it one of Australia’s highest-loss utilities.8
Like all other water utilities across Australia, TasWater also faces the
emerging and ongoing risks of climate change, and the task of how best to
plan for the management and mitigation of these risks.

The Economic Regulator’s approach to the investigation
Balancing affordability with the need for investment
The Economic Regulator acknowledges that investment is needed to meet
TasWater’s compliance obligations and address legacy challenges
associated with the performance of TasWater’s assets.
The Economic Regulator has sought to strike a careful balance between
affordability for customers and the need for sustained investment in water
and sewerage infrastructure. This is consistent with the approach that has
been taken by the Economic Regulator in previous price determination
investigations.

Expert advice on TasWater’s proposed expenditure
The Economic Regulator engaged expert consultants to help assess
TasWater’s expenditure proposals.

7 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal, page 65.
8 TasWater, Annual Performance Report 2024-25, page 6.

4

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

AtkinsRéalis was engaged to undertake a review of the prudency and
efficiency of TasWater’s proposed capex program. AtkinsRéalis has over
20 years of experience delivering expenditure and efficiency reviews across
Australia and internationally.
Sapere was engaged to undertake a review of the prudency and efficiency of
TasWater’s proposed opex. The Sapere team has expertise in regulatory
economics in the water sector and extensive experience assessing
expenditure forecasts for economic regulators across Australia.

Considering TasWater’s customer engagement results
The Economic Regulator required TasWater to carry out consultation with
customers, stakeholders and interested parties when developing its proposed
PSP5. While TasWater determines its consultation approach, it was required
to consider cost-effectiveness and focus on issues that impact the prices for
regulated services.9
The Economic Regulator recognises that TasWater undertook extensive
customer engagement activities in an effort to collaborate genuinely with its
customers. However, several factors have influenced how much weight the
Economic Regulator has placed on the results of that engagement.
Sapere, in its final report,10 raised concerns that much of the customer
engagement undertaken for TasWater’s proposed PSP5 was not well
focussed and/or suffered from the problem of “uninformed response” bias.
Based on Sapere’s understanding of the time and resources available to
TasWater’s Community Advisory Panel, it does not consider that the Panel
had sufficient information at hand to weigh up the benefits and costs of
TasWater’s proposals, or to make recommendations in relation to such
investments as conducting a large trial for digital meters.11
AtkinsRéalis also identified issues with TasWater’s customer engagement,
finding that it lacked depth and was not used to clearly inform its proposed

regulatory period. AtkinsRéalis also observed that TasWater had not
provided supporting cost‑benefit analysis to demonstrate a clear relationship

Data limitations
The Economic Regulator’s ability to rigorously and comprehensively make
evidence-based assessments of TasWater’s proposals is dependent on the
availability and quality of information provided by TasWater.
As the onus is on TasWater to justify all of its proposals, the Economic
Regulator has adopted a conservative approach in this Final Report for
situations where sufficient information was unavailable. In these instances,
the Economic Regulator has sought to work within the constraints of existing
data. Time and resource constraints have meant that it has not been possible
to seek clarification of minor inconsistencies in TasWater’s proposals or to
continue seeking additional information.

Consultation and submissions
There has been an unprecedented level of engagement and interest in
TasWater’s proposed PSP5 and the Economic Regulator’s investigation.
The Economic Regulator’s Draft Report was published on 2 February 2026
and invited submissions by 13 March 2026.
In total the Economic Regulator received 102 submissions during this
investigation. Of these, 29 submissions were received prior to the release of
the Draft Report, with a further 73 submissions received in response of the
Draft Report. These submissions represent views of a diverse variety of
TasWater customers and stakeholders including industry representative and
local advocacy groups, businesses and industrial customers, councils and
individuals. Submissions were also received from the Environment Protection
Authority and both sides of Government.
The differing views reflected in the submissions reflect the diverse range of
stakeholders which provided comment.

should be translated into current-period customer cost recovery. The material
before the Regulator points instead to a proposal that was overstated in parts,

regulatory period. That is why the final determination should remain tightly
constrained to expenditure that is demonstrably prudent, efficient and genuinely

Part 1: TasWater’s
revenue and
expenditure
allowances

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

9

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

1

Introduction to the notional allowable
revenue

TasWater’s regulated prices are set using a building block approach, where
TasWater’s costs are combined to calculate a notional allowable revenue.
The notional allowable revenue represents a hypothetical level of revenue
that TasWater would generate if it operated efficiently and met its service
obligations.
The cost components of the notional allowable revenue are:
Operating expenditure: The prudent and efficient
expenditure TasWater incurs to provide regulated services. It
includes staffing, energy, maintenance and other day-to-day
expenses.
Regulatory depreciation: This allows TasWater to recover
the cost of long-term infrastructure investments (e.g. pipes
and treatment plants) over their useful lives. This supports
intergenerational equity by allowing customers to pay for
assets as they are used and gradually returns capital to the
business for reinvestment.
Return on capital: This provides TasWater with a financial
return on its regulated assets which are used to deliver its
regulated services. The financial return is calculated using the
weighted average cost of capital.
Inflationary adjustment: As a nominal framework is used,
opex and the value of the assets TasWater uses to deliver
regulated services are indexed to account for inflation to
ensure that costs are recovered in real terms. An inflationary
adjustment is then applied to avoid under or over recovery of
revenue due to inflation volatility and prevents inflation from
being double counted in the notional allowable revenue
calculation.
Prices are calculated by dividing the notional allowable revenue by the
forecast demand for regulated services over the regulatory period. TasWater
produces demand forecasts for each year of the regulatory period, which
estimates the future:

10

❑

number of water and sewerage connections;

❑

water consumption; and

❑

sewerage discharge volumes.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

2

Corrections to TasWater’s proposed
notional allowable revenue

The Economic Regulator has made five corrections which impact on costs
and, ultimately, TasWater’s proposed notional allowable revenue and annual
price increases.
These corrections are necessary adjustments to address inconsistencies in
TasWater’s approach compared to the methodology accepted by the
Economic Regulator in past price determination investigations or to make
necessary corrections in the starting point of TasWater’s assets and cost
base.
The five corrections have the cumulative impact of reducing TasWater’s
proposed notional allowable revenue from approximately $2.25 billion (based
on its 30 September 2025 update) to around $2.17 billion. This reduces its
proposed uniform annual price increase from 11.5 per cent to 10.3 per cent.
The majority of these corrections relate to TasWater’s regulated asset base,
which impact on the notional allowable revenue through the components of
regulatory depreciation and the return on capital. TasWater’s expenditure is
only impacted through the corrections to customer connections services
(correction 5) and the treatment of bad and doubtful debts (correction 4).
These corrections reduce TasWater’s operating expenditure by $27.1 million.

2.1 Correction 1: Match the notional allowable revenue
to regulated revenue
TasWater proposed a new approach to calculating its uniform price change
that differs from the methodology accepted by the Economic Regulator in
previous price investigations. Rather than matching total forecast revenue to
the notional allowable revenue over the period, TasWater compared the net
present value (NPV) of forecast revenue with the NPV of the notional
allowable revenue, using the weighted average cost of capital for new assets
as the discount rate. This approach results in forecast revenue being lower
than the notional allowable revenue in early years and higher in later years,
which, once discounted, produces a higher upfront price path. Over the
four-year regulatory period, the NPV method results in a uniform price
increase that is 0.2 percentage points higher than the approach that matches
annual revenue to the notional allowable revenue. As TasWater did not
explain the rational for changing its methodology and the approach is
inconsistent with the Economic Regulator’s established practice, the draft
decision was not to support the proposal. Instead, a correction was applied to
align total regulated revenue with the notional allowable revenue for the
period.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

11

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

2.1.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater submitted that a key distinction between its proposal and the draft
decision is that the Draft Report does not provide for the time value of
money, which it contends would result in an under-recovery of its building
block revenue requirement. TasWater further asserted that the Economic
Regulator’s draft decision does not align with the practices of other economic
regulators across Australia, or with the pricing principles in the Industry Act.

2.1.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator applies a nominal building block framework to
determine TasWater’s notional allowable revenue. Under this framework, the
time value of money is already accounted for through the inclusion of inflation
in expenditure allowances and through the indexation of the regulated asset
base. By aligning TasWater’s regulated revenue requirement for each year of
the regulatory period to the notional allowable revenue, the Economic
Regulator ensures that TasWater is able to recover its efficient costs on an
appropriate year-by-year basis.
Decision 2.1: To set TasWater’s maximum prices by matching total
regulated revenue directly to its notional allowable revenue for the
fifth regulatory period.

2.2 Correction 2: Regulated assets corrections
TasWater earns a return on the capital invested in infrastructure used to
provide regulated water and sewerage services, through two components of
its notional allowable revenue:
12

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

regulatory depreciation, which spreads the cost of assets over their
useful lives so customers pay as they use them; and

❑

return on capital, which reflects the cost of funding assets through
debt and equity.

Both are calculated using TasWater’s regulated asset base, which is the
value of the assets used to deliver regulated services.
The Economic Regulator identified widespread issues affecting the accuracy
of the regulated asset base in TasWater’s proposed PSP5. These issues
included incorrect asset classifications, unsupported asset values,
inconsistent escalation rates, incomplete asset registers, limited evidence
supporting asset lives, and insufficient asset disposals relative to TasWater’s
growing capital program.
In its Draft Report, the Economic Regulator corrected TasWater’s proposed
regulated asset base by:
❑

removing, as disposals, $205.8 million of assets whose remaining
useful life expired during the third, fourth and fifth regulatory periods;

❑

removing, as disposals, assets at the Macquarie Point Sewage
Treatment Plant, Selfs Point Sewage Treatment Plant and Bryn Estyn
Water Treatment Plant that are no longer being used to deliver
regulated services;

❑

removing, as disposals, $1.95 million of decommissioned and inactive
assets across 2023-24 and 2024-25;

❑

applying June-to-June changes in CPI as the escalation rate,
consistent with the approved PSP4 approach;

❑

removing $60.6 million of double counted capex; and

❑

removing $79.2 million of third-party contributions identified during the
2018 Price Determination Investigation.

Cumulatively, these corrections reduced TasWater’s regulated asset base by
$365.1 million (refer to section 6.2.2), resulting in a $77.4 million reduction in
TasWater’s costs and notional allowable revenue.

2.2.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater accepted three of the six corrections identified by the
Economic Regulator to its regulated asset base. These corrections were:
❑

the application of a consistent June-to-June CPI;

❑

the removal of $60.6 million of double-counted capex; and

❑

the removal of $79.2 million of third-party contributions.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

13

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater did not agree with the three corrections regarding the disposal of
assets from its regulated asset base. TasWater submits that assets should
only be disposed of from its regulated asset base where they have been sold.
TasWater argued that the value of assets that are not sold should be left in its
regulated asset base so the business can, in-principle, recover its full capital
investment where this was deemed to be prudent and efficient.
TasWater submitted that the Economic Regulator’s approach to removing
assets from the regulated asset base is inconsistent with the approaches
taken by other regulators, including the Australian Energy Regulator (AER)
and the Independent Pricing and Regulatory Tribunal (IPART).
TasWater further submits that assets which have reached the end of their
economic life may continue to hold a regulatory value where regulatory
depreciation allowances have been insufficient to fully recover their economic
value. In addition, TasWater contends that disposing of assets on a
line-by-line basis is inconsistent with a net present value (NPV=0) principle
once it has transitioned to a weighted average asset life approach.
TasWater acknowledged issues within the regulated asset base and
accepted that some assets, including those with a remaining regulatory
value, should be disposed of. TasWater proposed a “line-in-the-sand” to
transition from the line-by-line methodology to a weighted average
methodology at the beginning of the fourth regulatory period and argued that
disposals should not occur after this transition.
Details of TasWater’s proposed disposals and the closing values that is
submitted for its regulated asset bases are provided in Chapter 6 of this Final
Report.

2.2.2 Economic Regulator’s decisions
The Economic Regulator agrees with TasWater’s proposal to establish a
“line-in-the-sand” and move to a weighted average approach. However, the
Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s position on the treatment of
asset disposals from the regulated asset base, including TasWater’s reliance
on approaches adopted by other regulators. Under the current regulatory
framework, TasWater applies a line-by-line methodology to asset lives, under
which the removal of individual assets is appropriate.
In reviewing TasWater’s regulated asset bases, the Economic Regulator
identified widespread errors and inconsistencies in asset values,
classifications and supporting information. These issues have developed
over multiple regulatory periods and, due to data limitations, cannot be fully
traced or resolved in every case.
Given these circumstances, the Economic Regulator has adopted a
pragmatic approach and set a “line-in-the-sand” at the end of the fourth
regulatory period rather than at the beginning of the fourth regulatory period
as proposed by TasWater. This approach ensures the opening asset bases

14

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

for the fifth regulatory period are appropriately corrected, while providing
clarity and certainty going forward.
Consistent with this approach, assets have been disposed of on a line-by-line
basis up to the end of the fourth regulatory period. This is consistent with the
approach proposed by TasWater in its March submission. During the fifth
regulatory period, disposals are limited to assets that are forecast to be
decommissioned, replaced or otherwise removed.
The Economic Regulator previously sought to transition TasWater to a
weighted average asset life approach to improve simplicity and transparency.
While this would have largely removed many of the issues identified, the
line-by-line approach was retained for the third and fourth regulatory periods
at TasWater’s request.
As a result of the corrections applied, the Economic Regulator has removed
$189.2 million from the regulated asset bases up to the end of the fourth
regulatory period, and has removed $41.8 million during the fifth regulatory
period. Assets initially assessed as having zero value in the Draft Report, but
later shown to have a regulatory value in the fifth regulatory period, were
retained in the regulated asset base.
Finally, although TasWater agreed with the removal of $79.2 million in
third-party contributions, this adjustment was not reflected in its submission
and has therefore been corrected in the Final Report.
The Economic Regulator’s corrections make a cumulative reduction of
$52.8 million to TasWater’s notional allowable revenue.
Further detailed discussions of the Economic Regulator’s approach are
included in Chapter 6.

2.3 Correction 3: Reduction in depreciation for existing
assets
Section 68(1A) of the Industry Act effectively creates two groups of assets:
1. assets transferred prior to 1 July 2011 are referred to as existing assets;
2. assets purchased or constructed after 1 July 2009 are referred to as new assets.

TasWater’s depreciation rate for existing assets is based on the weighted
average of the remaining life of its existing assets and the written down value
of those assets. The weighted average asset life is intended to represent the
average remaining economic life of the portfolio of the existing assets. From
a regulatory perspective, the weighted average asset life is a pricing and
cost-allocation mechanism that is meant to ensure a transparent and
predictable recovery of capital.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

15

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

In the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator recalculated the weighted
average of the existing assets’ remaining lives due to significant changes in
the number and nature of assets in the regulated asset base. In particular,
the Economic Regulator removed approximately 34 700 assets from the
existing regulated asset base and TasWater proposed to reclassify almost
11 000 assets from its new asset regulated asset base to its existing assets
regulated asset base. The remaining weighted average existing asset life
was calculated as 56.04 years.

2.3.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater submitted that it does not agree with the Draft Report approach for
calculating the weighted-average remaining life of existing assets, arguing
that it materially understates depreciation and departs from accepted
regulatory practice. TasWater contended that the Draft Report relies on
incorrect asset life assumptions and applies a depreciation method that is no
longer straight-line, risking misrepresentation of the current age and condition
of the existing asset base, and that an error occurred in the calculation of the
asset life.

2.3.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator acknowledges that the years between 2015 and
2026 were omitted when calculating the Draft Report existing asset life, with
the original asset lives used without adjusting for the time that had elapsed
between the establishment of the asset registers and the commencement of
the fifth regulatory period. This resulted in a longer weighted average asset
life and therefore, a lower depreciation value, being applied in the
Draft Report.
The Economic Regulator also acknowledges that the weighted average asset
life applied in the Draft Report may not accurately reflect the condition of
TasWater’s existing asset base. As outlined elsewhere in this Final Report,
the Economic Regulator believes the existing regulated asset base contains
widespread errors and inconsistencies, evidenced by TasWater’s
reclassification of $238.5 million in assets from the new to existing asset
base, the Economic Regulator’s removal of over 30 000 assets, and the
identification of a further 96 assets that could not be removed due to time
constraints.
As discussed in more detail in section 9.3.1, the Economic Regulator
assessed four options to determine an appropriate existing asset life. These
four options were:
1. accept TasWater’s proposed existing asset average life which it
submitted in response to the Draft Report of 41.11 years;

16

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

2. accept TasWater’s proposed existing asset average life of 43.86 years
which was submitted in its proposed PSP5 in June 2025;
3. roll-forward TasWater’s asset life at the end of the third regulatory
period to the end of the fourth regulatory period, for an asset life of
46.92 years; or
4. apply the asset life that was used in the last year of the third regulatory
period at the beginning of the fifth regulatory period, for an asset life of
51.9217.
Recognising that all options had limitations, the Economic Regulator
concluded that adopting an asset life of 43.86 years, consistent with the end
of the fourth regulatory period, was the most reasonable approach. This
option aligns with the Economic Regulator’s “line‑in‑the‑sand” approach,
reflects a more contemporary view of the asset base, and avoids applying a
retrospective adjustment.
For further details on how this asset life has been chosen and applied, refer
to section 9.3.1 of this Final Report.

2.4 Correction 4: Treatment of bad and doubtful debts as
foregone revenue
Bad and doubtful debts refer to amounts owed to a business that may not be
recoverable. Bad debts are those considered uncollectable and written off as
a loss, while doubtful debts are those where recovery is uncertain, requiring a
provision to cover potential non-payment. In regulated businesses,
allowances for bad and doubtful debts are often factored into pricing
decisions to ensure customers are not charged for revenue that is unlikely to
be collected.
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed to treat bad and doubtful
debts as an operating cost. The Economic Regulator has instead treated bad
and doubtful debts as foregone revenue. This is discussed further in
section 5.4.12. While this correction has no net impact on TasWater or its
regulated prices, it will reduce TasWater’s opex and its notional allowable
revenue by $1.7 million over the fifth regulatory period. TasWater accepted
the treatment of bad and doubtful debts as foregone revenue in its
submission to the Draft Report.
Decision 2.2: To remove TasWater’s proposed opex allowance for
bad and doubtful debts from the notional allowable revenue and
reduce TasWater’s regulated revenue by the same amount.

17 The third regulatory period was extended by a year due to COVID-19. At the time of the third pricing

investigation, the final year of the third regulatory period was 2020-21.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

17

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

2.5 Correction 5: Removal of unregulated service costs
(customer connections)
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed to include the opex for its
customer connections service in its notional allowable revenue, with revenue
from this service treated as regulated revenue. This differs to the approach
approved by the Economic Regulator for the fourth regulatory period, where
TasWater had anticipated that this work would be carried out by external
contractors dealing directly with customers. However, TasWater was unable
to implement this change and continued to deliver this service over the fourth
regulatory period.
In its review of TasWater’s opex, Sapere considered that it would be simpler
and more accurate to treat the customer connection costs as an unregulated
service, noting that this would remove risks associated with incorrectly
forecasting the costs and revenue.18 The Economic Regulator accepted this
approach and decided to treat customer connections as an unregulated
service. This has reduced TasWater’s notional allowable revenue by
$25.4 million, with regulated revenue reduced by the same amount, resulting
in no net impact on regulated prices. This decision does not affect
TasWater’s ability to continue providing and charging for customer
connections as an unregulated service. TasWater accepted this treatment of
customer connections in its submission to the Draft Report.
Decision 2.3: To treat customer connections as an unregulated
service for the fifth regulatory period and remove the associated
costs from TasWater’s notional allowable revenue and from its
regulated revenue.

18 Sapere Draft Report, page 36

18

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

3

Revenue deferral

TasWater proposed deferring the recovery of $127.5 million in revenue to the
sixth regulatory period.19 Under this proposal, the notional allowable revenue
would be calculated as usual, and then the deferred amount would be
deducted from the total notional allowable revenue, resulting in a reduction to
the price increase for the fifth regulatory period. This deferred amount would
then be added to prices in the sixth regulatory period.
Under TasWater’s proposal, the deferred revenue would be treated as a
separate asset within TasWater’s regulated asset base and be fully
recovered by the end of the sixth regulatory period. Treated in this way,
TasWater would earn a rate of return on this investment over the sixth
regulatory period, with regulated customers being required to pay a total of
$144 million through regulated prices.
The Economic Regulator’s draft decision was that TasWater’s proposed
revenue deferral could not be supported, as it is inconsistent with the
regulatory framework and pricing principles. The Economic Regulator
considered that the proposed deferral would attempt to recover costs outside
the relevant regulatory period, does not align with the pricing principles, and
does not meet the criteria for inclusion in the regulated asset base.
The Industry Act specifies that:
❑

Price relates to a specific regulatory period: Under section 65(9),
an approved price and service plan must relate to a specific
regulatory period. This prohibits the recovery of costs incurred in one
period from being transferred to another period; and

❑

Returns may only be earnt on assets providing a regulated
service: Under section 68(1)(d), the price is to allow for a return only
on assets that are necessary for providing the regulated service to
which the price applies. The proposed deferred revenue does not
meet this criterion.

The pricing principles require that prices be efficient, directly attributable to
the provision of a regulated service and reflect costs. The proposed deferred
revenue does not satisfy these requirements, as it would transfer cost
recovery across regulatory periods and does not relate to an identifiable
asset required to provide a regulated service.
The Economic Regulator also considered that deferring revenue to a future
regulatory period would weaken the link between prices and the costs of
service provision, undermining pricing efficiency and cost attribution. In
addition, the proposed deferral would effectively impose a minimum revenue
outcome for the sixth regulatory period, which is inconsistent with the
19 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal Update, 30 September 2025, page 14.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

19

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Industry Act, as price determinations may only set maximum prices or
maximum revenues and cannot extend beyond the scope of the current price
investigation.
Water and Sewerage Industry Act 2008
66. Price determinations
(3A) Nothing in this section is to be taken to permit a price determination made under this
section to set –
(a) a price, other than a maximum price or maximum rate of increase of price; or
(b) an amount of revenue other than a maximum amount of revenue.

3.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater maintained that a carefully structured revenue
deferral mechanism is consistent with the intent and practical application of
the Industry Act and is necessary to ensure that prices in the fifth regulatory
period best advance the long-term interests of customers. While
acknowledging the Economic Regulator’s concerns about cross-period cost
recovery, TasWater argued that the Industry Act does not explicitly prescribe
the timeframe over which efficient costs must be recovered. TasWater also
submitted that economic regulators routinely establish forward-looking
methodologies outside formal determinations, particularly where doing so is
necessary to support the efficient and sustainable provision of regulated
services, and that the proposed revenue deferral mechanism is consistent
with this practice.
The Shadow Treasurer Mr Dean Winter MP submits that the Tasmanian
Labor Party strongly opposes the proposed “buy now, pay later” approach to
essential services. The Shadow Treasurer urges the Economic Regulator to
abandon the revenue deferral scheme and instead re-scope TasWater’s
capital program to a level that is deliverable and affordable.
“By masking the true cost of its proposal, TasWater is failing to be transparent
with the community about the long-term impact of its $1.7 billion capital
program.” 20

3.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
Having considered the arguments raised in TasWater’s submission, the
Economic Regulator has decided to maintain its draft decision not to accept
TasWater’s proposal to defer recovery of some of its revenue to the sixth
20 Submission from Dean Winter MP, Shadow Treasurer, page 2.

20

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

regulatory period. The Economic Regulator maintains its initial view that the
proposed deferral is inconsistent with the regulatory framework and pricing
principles.
Decision 3.1: To not accept TasWater’s proposal to defer recovery
of some of its revenue to the sixth regulatory period.

Demand forecasts

TasWater’s demand forecasts are used to estimate how many customers will
connect to its network and use its services during the fifth regulatory period.
This information is critical for determining maximum prices because it is used
as the basis for distributing TasWater’s regulated revenue across its
customer base (see Figure 4.1).

The demand for TasWater’s services, such as the number of customers and
the volume of water delivered and wastewater discharged, also impacts
TasWater’s opex. More customers and higher volumes will lead to higher
costs in areas such as power, chemicals, and customer billings and

The Economic Regulator’s assessment focused on the veracity and

❑

compared to actual demand over that period; and

❑

TasWater’s forecasts for the fifth regulatory period.

In assessing the accuracy of TasWater’s demand forecasts for the fourth
regulatory period, the Economic Regulator observes the following:
❑

20 mm equivalent connections exceeded forecasts: TasWater
attributed the consistently higher-than-forecast number of connections
to an increase in building approvals. TasWater also attributed the
significant increase in 2023-24 to some connections being upgraded
from 20 mm to 25 mm, and a data error, which was fixed in 2024-25.22

❑

Actual water use was consistently below forecast: Water usage
was between 0.9 to nine per cent below TasWater’s forecasts.

22 Based on additional information provided by TasWater on 18 September 2025.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

23

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater noted that water usage is inherently variable and influenced
by external factors such as weather, which are difficult to predict.23
❑

Sewerage connections were slightly higher than forecast: The
actual number of sewerage connections was approximately
two per cent higher than forecast for the fourth regulatory period.

❑

Category 0 trade waste customers have been excluded from
trade waste connections: TasWater does not charge these
customers for trade waste because their waste load is similar to, or
less than, a residential customer and accordingly, were excluded from
its trade waste connection numbers.

❑

Miscellaneous fees and charges were lower than forecast: The
number of transactions for miscellaneous fees and charges was up to
10.5 per cent lower than forecast. There were unexplained differences
between TasWater’s current and previous forecasts which limited the
Economic Regulator’s ability to assess the forecasting approach.

4.2 TasWater’s forecast demand for the fifth regulatory
period
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater
developed its proposed demand forecasts
using its regional master plans, which divide
Tasmania’s water and sewerage systems into
nine regions and assess expected demand in
each region over a 50‑year horizon.
TasWater has applied a base‑trend forecasting
approach (see Figure 4.2). Its forecasts are
based on Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS)
census data, as well as planning documents
prepared by local councils and regional
planning forums. TasWater also indicated that
it has validated its forecasts against a range of
independent growth indicators, including
population and economic growth data from the ABS, the Tasmanian
Department of Treasury and Finance, and regional land‑use strategies.24
TasWater’s proposed tariff reforms, including the new fixed and variable
charging approaches for sewerage and trade waste services, were reflected
in TasWater’s demand forecasts. As a result, its forecasts also include
sewerage discharge volumes and trade waste volumes for the first time.

4.2.1 The Economic Regulator’s draft assessment of TasWater’s
forecasts for the fifth regulatory period
The Economic Regulator acknowledged that TasWater made improvements
to its demand forecasting approach over the fourth regulatory period, most
notably through the development of regional master plans, which provide an
overview of TasWater’s longer-term supply and demand considerations.
However, the Economic Regulator’s assessment identified some limitations
with TasWater’s forecasting method and data issues, including:
❑

TasWater’s water and sewer connection growth rates reflect a
high-growth scenario.
TasWater forecast connections to grow by, on average, 0.96 per cent
per year for water connections and 0.88 per cent per year for sewer
connections.26 This aligns with the Treasury Population Projections
“high” growth series which reflects a 0.91 per cent annual growth rate.
However, Treasury states that its “medium” series forecasts annual
growth rate of 0.56 per cent per year is a more likely outcome.27

❑

TasWater applied the same water growth rate for both residential
and non-residential customers.
Residential and non-residential water connection growth was forecast
using residential growth rates, which the Economic Regulator noted
may overstate future demand because non-residential growth is
typically lower.

❑

It is unclear how TasWater forecast its sewerage discharge
volumes.
TasWater established a base sewerage discharge volume of 42.4 GL
in 2025-26 and applied projected water usage growth to create its
forecast but did not explain how it calculated its base volume. 28
The Economic Regulator determined a higher base volume of
47.1 GL, which was consistent with TasWater’s tariff reform model.

❑

TasWater’s water usage forecasts are based on limited historical
data, with no allowance for price elasticity of demand.
Water usage was forecast using a simple average of the previous
four years of annual customer consumption, which TasWater
anticipated will increase gradually across all customer groups over the
fifth regulatory period.29 This approach did not consider the potential
impact of increased variable water charges on customer usage
patterns.

28 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal, page 161.
29 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal, page 161.

The Economic Regulator also noted that TasWater expressed concerns
about the reliability and accuracy of data prior to 2020-21 in its discussions
with staff from the Office of the Tasmanian Economic Regulator. It is
understood that this contributed to the decision to base its forecasts on a
limited four-year data set.

4.2.2 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
Despite the Economic Regulator’s decision to accept most of TasWater’s
demand forecasts, TasWater submitted updated demand forecasts in its
submission to the Draft Report. As noted by TasWater, these updates are
material and reflect the incorporation of 2024-25 actual data, a new
sewerage volume forecasting approach and other adjustments to reflect its
proposed tariff structure (see Table 4.3). TasWater contends that its revisions
have improved the accuracy of its revenue modelling and price estimates.
However, TasWater’s submission does not explain why it has chosen to
update its demand forecasts at this stage in the investigation, given that it
previously submitted its 2024-25 actual data in September 2025 and
proposed not to update its forecasts at that time.
TasWater notes that, relative to its previous forecasts, water consumption is
5.5 per cent lower and water connections are 3.0 per cent lower. For
sewerage services, TasWater forecast sewerage discharge to be
2.7 per cent higher, while sewer connections are 3.9 per cent lower.
TasWater estimates that the revised forecasts would increase prices by
1.6 percentage points relative to the Draft Report price outcomes.
