The Economic Regulator received submissions on TasWater’s demand
forecasts from several stakeholders including the Hon Eric Abetz MP,
Treasurer, local government and an individual. Collectively, submissions
emphasised the importance of forecasts accurately reflecting future growth
and raised concerns about the robustness of TasWater’s forecasts.
The Treasurer noted the Economic Regulator’s finding of material
weaknesses in TasWater’s demand forecasts and emphasised the need for
high-quality, reliable data for sound regulatory decision making. It supports
the Economic Regulator’s expectation that TasWater strengthen its demand
modelling and forecasting methodology and considers this will provide a
more robust basis for future price and investment decisions and broader
framework considerations.
Sorrell Council expressed concerns that TasWater’s forecasting may not
adequately reflect house-driven connection growth in fast-growing areas
such as Sorell. It also noted that underestimating required sewer treatment
and network capacity requirements could delay delivery of new housing
estates.
Mr Wright raised concerns regarding the reliability of key data inputs, such as
2021-22 water usage, and noted a lack of robustness in TasWater’s
forecasts. He suggested that forecast water usage should be adjusted to
reflect the long-term declining trend in consumption and that 2023-24 water
usage should be excluded, as it contained elevated usage.
Mr Wright further highlighted differences between the water usage volume
presented in the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report and those reported in
the State of the Water and Sewerage Industry Reports. He also questioned

28

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

the revenue recovery implications if the Economic Regulator accepted
TasWater’s initial forecasts.

4.2.3 Discussion
TasWater submitted actual demand data for 2024-25 to the Economic
Regulator on 30 September 2025 in its PSP5 Proposal Update. However, in
this update, TasWater explicitly chose to retain its initial demand forecasts,
despite the 2024-25 actual data showing lower connections and water usage
than forecast.
“Proposed approach: To provide the actual water and sewerage connections
and the actual water usage for 2024-25. We are proposing to maintain the
same demand forecast as our 30 June 2025 PSP5 Proposal for the PSP5
period.”30

If TasWater revised its forecasts in its September Update, the proposed price
increase for the fifth regulatory period would have been 13.1 per cent per
year, not 11.5 per cent.
Although TasWater characterises its revised forecasts as material updates in
its submission to the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator has concerns
regarding the volatility in the demand forecasts arising from these revisions.
In particular, the revised forecasts exhibit material increases and decreases
relative to the initial forecasts, indicating instability in projected outcomes.
These concerns are compounded by the identification of modelling errors in
TasWater’s revised forecasts, which underestimates non-residential sewer
connections by 3.2 per cent and has a minor impact on volumetric sewerage
forecasts. The presence of the errors in TasWater’s updated model reduces
confidence in the outputs and would require additional time to establish the
veracity of the revised forecasts.
Stakeholders questioned the robustness of TasWater’s forecasts, including
whether 2023-24 water usage should be excluded on the basis that it may
skew forecast consumption. The available evidence shows that 2023-24
usage reflects actual billed volumes and, while elevated, is not unreasonable
and remains below TasWater’s forecast usage for the fourth regulatory
period.
The Economic Regulator also found no evidence to support claims that total
water consumption is declining, with data instead indicating a slight upward
trend over the past five and 10 years. Accordingly, no adjustment has been
made to reflect declining water usage.
However, the Economic Regulator notes TasWater’s correction to 2021-22
water usage arose from a modelling error and considers it reasonable to
30 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal Update, 30 September, page 10.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

29

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

reflect this correction in the forecasts. That the adjustment of a single data
point reduces forecast water usage by 2.3 per cent suggests a high degree of
volatility in TasWater’s current forecasting approach.
It is further noted that the water usage figures in the Draft Report and the
State of the Water and Sewerage Industry Reports are not directly
comparable, as they are based on different definitions and sources - billed
volumes for regulated customers versus total urban water supplied.
The Economic Regulator further notes that all feedback received on the Draft
Report relates to TasWater’s originally proposed demand forecasts. If the
Economic Regulator were to accept TasWater’s materially new proposal at
this stage of the investigation, it would deny stakeholders the opportunity for
consultation on this proposal. This is particularly relevant given the proposed
changes would shift revenue recovery risk to customers through lower
forecast demand.

Economic Regulator’s decisions
TasWater’s submission to the Draft Report proposes significant changes to
its forecasting approach, which amount to a new proposal with methodology
changes and material price impacts. The Economic Regulator is concerned
that the volatility evident in the revised forecasts, together with identified
modelling errors further exposes weaknesses in TasWater’s forecasting
approach. These issues reduce confidence in the revised forecasts and
additional time would be required to establish the veracity of the revised
forecasts, despite the availability of actual data for 2024-25.
TasWater has indicated that its revised forecasts are generally lower than
those in its original proposal. However, the Economic Regulator notes that,
over the fourth regulatory period, actual water and sewer connections
consistently exceeded forecast levels. While actual water usage was lower
than forecast, TasWater has also acknowledged that usage has not returned
to long-term average levels since 2020-21.31
Given the late stage of the investigation that this information was provided,
the Economic Regulator had limited ability to seek further information or to
undertake additional analysis necessary to ascertain the veracity of
TasWater’s revised forecasts and to test them against other related
proposals, such as opex and capex.
The subdued demand outlook implied by TasWater’s revised forecasts does
not align with the scale and urgency of the expenditure programs proposed
by TasWater. Had TasWater provided these revised forecasts in its initial
proposal, it may have influenced the Economic Regulator’s assessment of
TasWater’s capex and opex proposals.
These outcomes suggest caution in placing reliance on revised forecasts that
introduce additional uncertainty and volatility. The Economic Regulator also
31 Based on additional information provided by TasWater on 30 June 2025.

30

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

considers that this reinforces the need for a comprehensive review of
TasWater’s forecasting approach.
The Economic Regulator also notes that TasWater has had prior
opportunities to provide updated forecasts but has previously advised that it
would maintain its original forecasts. Accepting revised forecasts at this stage
would undermine the consultation process and weaken incentives for
TasWater to submit reliable and robust forecasts in its initial PSP proposal.
The Economic Regulator also considers that a further round of consultation
would be required to provide stakeholders with the opportunity to scrutinise
and comment on TasWater’s proposals before they could be accepted for
this determination.
In light of the uncertainty, volatility and procedural concerns associated with
TasWater’s revised proposal, the Economic Regulator will require TasWater
to use its initial demand forecasts, subject to the correction to water usage
forecasts.

The Economic Regulator outlined in its Draft Report that TasWater had not
provided sufficient information to understand its current forecasting approach
for miscellaneous service transactions, which includes the demand for

32

TasWater uses the growth in water and sewer connections from its demand
forecasts to calculate its headworks charges. However, for the purposes of
calculating third-party contributions, TasWater has assumed lower demand
than included in its general demand forecasts without sufficient justification of
this decision. This lower demand assumption understates the value of
third-party contributions, due to either lower demand or a higher headworks
charge.
On this basis, the Economic Regulator has adjusted the headworks demand
to reflect the demand assumptions used to calculate the headworks charges.

TasWater’s documentation indicates that, although it has started to improve
its demand forecasting capabilities, current methods do not yet align with
industry best practice. The Economic Regulator agrees with this assessment
and has identified several data issues and limitations.
The Economic Regulator requires TasWater to review its approach to
forecasting demand, with consideration to:
❑

the most appropriate segmentation of its customers for forecasting
purposes;

❑

the price elasticity of demand for different services; and

❑

the extent that growth forecasts for industrial customers should be
informed by consultation with those customers.

TasWater is required to submit its revised demand forecast model to the
Economic Regulator 12 months before lodging its next pricing proposal. This
will provide sufficient time for TasWater to implement a more robust
forecasting approach and for the Economic Regulator to review the model
ahead of the pricing investigation for the sixth regulatory period.

approach and provide its revised model to the Economic Regulator
by 30 June 2028.

5

Operating and maintenance expenditure

Operating and maintenance expenditure (opex) refers to the ongoing costs
TasWater incurs to deliver its day-to-day water and sewerage services. Opex
is a fundamental building block in the pricing framework, representing
approximately half of TasWater’s notional allowable revenue. To ensure
customers pay only for efficient and necessary costs, the
Economic Regulator includes in the notional allowable revenue only those
opex amounts that are assessed as prudent and efficient.
Opex is assessed as prudent if:
❑

it could be reasonably expected/required by a business exercising good industry
practice; and

❑

it is consistent with delivering minimum service standards, outputs and/or
obligations over the relevant regulatory period.

Opex is assessed as efficient if it represents the most cost-effective way of providing
services.

TasWater proposed an opex allowance of $1 110.2 million for the fifth
regulatory period. This represents a 28.7 per cent increase compared to the
opex allowance of $862.7 million approved for the fourth regulatory period.
Figure 5.1: Comparison of TasWater’s actual and approved regulated opex for PSP4,
with its forecast regulated opex for PSP5 ($m, nominal) Note

Note: Actual opex for 2025-26 reflects TasWater’s forecast for that year.

The Economic Regulator engaged the expertise of independent consulting
firm Sapere and, after considering its recommendations, the Economic
Regulator’s draft decision was to reduce TasWater’s proposed opex
allowance by $98.5 million, to a total of opex allocation of $1 011.7 million.

34

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Following feedback received on the Draft Report, updated recommendations
from Sapere and its own analysis, the Economic Regulator has determined a
prudent and efficient opex allowance for the fifth regulatory period of
$1 018.8 million. This is compared to TasWater’s proposal and Sapere’s final
recommendation in Figure 5.2.
Figure 5.2: Comparison of the Economic Regulator’s total opex allowance to
TasWater’s proposal and Sapere’s recommendations ($m, nominal)

The key adjustments that the Economic Regulator made to TasWater’s
proposal include:
❑

unregulated services: the Economic Regulator increased the
allocation of costs to unregulated services by consistently applying
TasWater’s overheads cost allocation approach across both regulated
and unregulated services. This resulted in a $27.2 million reduction to
the PSP5 opex allocation.

❑

price neutral corrections: the Economic Regulator treated customer
connections as an unregulated service, removing the associated opex
from the notional allowable revenue and regulated revenue. The
Economic Regulator also treated forecast bad and doubtful debts as
foregone revenue, removing it from the notional allowable revenue
and regulated revenue. Combined, these adjustments result in a
$26.8 million reduction to the PSP5 opex allocation.

❑

salary progression: the Economic Regulator removed TasWater’s
proposed one per cent per annum pay point progression above the
wage increases provided in its most recent Enterprise Agreement.
This reduced the PSP5 opex allocation by $17.2 million.

❑

productivity: the Economic Regulator increased the productivity
factor to 1.5 per cent, consistent with the fourth regulatory period. This
reduced the PSP5 opex allocation by $17.2 million.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

35

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

inflation: the Economic Regulator updated forecast inflation using the
RBA’s latest release. This increased the PSP5 opex allocation by
$9.4 million.

The detailed breakdown of the Economic Regulator’s final decisions by opex
categories (with updated inflation), compared to TasWater’s proposal and the
PSP4 allowance is presented at the end of this Chapter in Table 5.4.

5.1 Approach for assessing TasWater’s opex
Sapere conducted a detailed evaluation of TasWater’s opex, examining the
efficiency and prudency of TasWater’s expenditure, including reviewing:
❑

TasWater’s opex in the fourth regulatory period;

❑

TasWater’s proposed opex for each year of the fifth regulatory period;
and

❑

TasWater’s proposed activity-based costing model used to allocate
opex between regulated and unregulated services.

In forming its decisions, the Economic Regulator considered TasWater’s
justification for the opex in its proposal, along with Sapere’s assessment and
recommendations.
Sapere’s final report of its review of TasWater’s operational expenditure is
provided as an attachment.

5.1.1 Base-Step-Trend approach
TasWater forecast future opex using the Base-Step-Trend methodology,
consistent with the approach endorsed by the Economic Regulator in the
2022 Price Investigation. Under this method, costs are projected from an
efficient base year, adjusted for one-off changes such as new or
discontinued activities (step changes), and escalated using annual trend
factors to reflect movements in prices for materials and services, demand
growth and productivity.
The Economic Regulator and Sapere applied the same Base-Step-Trend
approach to assess the prudency and efficiency of TasWater’s proposed
opex, establishing the base year using TasWater’s actual operating
expenditure for the most recently completed financial year.

5.1.2 Activity-based costing approach
In the 2022 Investigation Final Report, the Economic Regulator required
TasWater to implement an activity-based costing approach during the
fourth regulatory period. Activity‑based costing allocates costs based on the
36

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

specific activities undertaken and the resources used to deliver services. This
improves the accuracy of cost allocation between regulated services (such as
water and sewerage) and unregulated services32, and provides clearer
information on which costs are fixed or variable. The Economic Regulator’s
intent was that TasWater would use activity-based costing to allocate opex
between regulated and unregulated services and to classify costs into fixed
and variable components for its PSP5 proposal.

5.2 Review of TasWater’s opex for the fourth regulatory
period
Sapere’s review found that TasWater’s actual opex for the fourth regulatory
period is forecast to exceed the Economic Regulator’s approved allowance
by $28.7 million, or 3.3 per cent.
A year-by-year comparison is provided in Figure 5.3. To ensure a like‑for‑like
comparison, Sapere aligned TasWater’s actual opex with the approved opex
by excluding employee leave and customer connection costs and applying
the PSP4 approach to allocating unregulated costs.

32 Unregulated services include stormwater collection or use, water recycling or re use, and the supply

Sapere identified the largest contributors to these variances as a $6.1 million
overspend on information systems and a $4.9 million overspend on
chemicals.
Consistent with past practice, the Economic Regulator has not made ex-post
adjustments to TasWater’s opex for the fourth regulatory period. TasWater is
incentivised to manage expenditure within its approved allowance because
any overspend is absorbed by the business and cannot be recovered from
regulated customers. This approach ensures efficiency incentives remain in
place and protects customers from cost overruns.

TasWater based its opex for the first year of the regulatory period on its
2024-25 budget, and made several adjustments:
❑

one-off expenditures: TasWater removed expenditures that are not
expected to re-occur.

❑

leave adjustments: TasWater reported that employee leave expenses
were incorrectly excluded from the base year in previous PSPs and
proposed including its annual and long service leave costs for the fifth
regulatory period. This equates to $51.9 million over four years.

❑

allocation to unregulated services: TasWater used its activity‑based
costing model to allocate costs between regulated and unregulated
services, resulting in an additional $8.4 million of opex being allocated
to unregulated services compared with PSP4, when allocations were
based on the share of regulated and unregulated revenue.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

39

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

customer connection costs: TasWater’s proposal includes an
increase to the regulated cost base to cover the costs it incurs in
providing a new customer with a connection. These costs were
excluded from opex in the fourth regulatory period, on the basis that
TasWater intended that customers would engage and pay contractors
directly for connections. TasWater was unable to implement this
approach during the fourth regulatory period, and it plans to continue
to provide this service and carry out the associated work during the
fifth regulatory period.

❑

bad and doubtful debts: TasWater included its bad and doubtful
debts as opex in its base year for the first time. Bad and doubtful debts
refer to amounts owed by customers that TasWater either does not
expect to recover or may be unlikely to recover.

For most opex categories, TasWater has used the RBA’s forecast inflation
rates33 to escalate its costs each year. This is consistent with the
requirements in the Economic Regulator’s PSP Guideline. Separate cost
escalators have been developed by TasWater for salaries, power, insurance
and strategic initiatives.
TasWater proposed adjustments to some of its specific cost pools where
changes are expected to occur during the fifth regulatory period. These
changes include higher costs from selected capex projects ($11.0 million),
increased ICT expenditure ($24.6 million) and expected savings from the
non‑revenue water program ($4.8 million).

5.4 Economic Regulator’s assessment of TasWater’s
proposed opex and decisions for the fifth regulatory
period
This section outlines the assessment of TasWater’s proposed opex costs and
adjustments. It also provides an overview of the decisions in the Draft Report,
feedback received during consultation and the Economic Regulator’s final
decisions.

5.4.1 TasWater’s use of its 2024-25 budget as the basis for its
proposed opex
Sapere noted in its draft report that TasWater’s use of its 2024-25 budget as
a starting point for base opex was unconventional, particularly as the budget
exceeded actual expenditure for that year. Sapere therefore recommended
using TasWater’s actual 2024-25 opex as the base year, consistent with the
standard application of the Base-Step-Trend approach.34

33 Based on the RBA’s Quarterly Statement on Monetary Policy – May 2025.
34 Sapere Draft Report, page ix.

40

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator agreed, noting that the TasWater’s actual opex
represents more up-to-date information. Accordingly, the Draft Report
included a draft decision to use TasWater’s actual 2024-25 operating
expenditure as the base year.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
While TasWater’s submission accepts the use its actual 2024-25 opex as the
base year, it requests additional funding for materials and services and for
consultancy expenditure. TasWater’s submission also proposes increasing
its facility management base year allocation to $11.7 million, based on its
actual 2024-25 expenditure without making any adjustment for one-off
expenditures. This represents a 22 per cent increase for this cost pool from
its PSP5 proposal, which included a base year allocation of $9.6 million
based on its 2024-25 budget and its activity-based costing model.
A summary of the updated adjustments that are requested in TasWater’s

Economic Regulator’s decision
Materials and services include non-salary costs for maintaining and operating
water and wastewater networks and treatment facilities, and represents
TasWater’s second-largest cost category at around 15 per cent of opex.
Sapere’s final report notes that TasWater’s suggested adjustment relates
specifically to additional funding for Maintenance small plant and equipment.
Sapere considers that adjustments of this nature (at a sub-code level) are not
consistent with the Base-Step-Trend approach, as sub-codes naturally
fluctuate year-to-year. It notes that making upward adjustments for specific
sub‑codes without offsetting over‑expenditure elsewhere would result in
overstated forecasts.35 The Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s
assessment and considers that an uplift for materials and services is not
warranted.
Sapere noted that TasWater has previously reported that its facility
management expenditure was abnormally higher in 2024-25. The Economic
35 Sapere’s Final Report, page 59.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

41

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Regulator does not consider TasWater has provided sufficient information to
demonstrate that the additional costs for unforeseen activities are efficient or
ongoing in nature, particularly since the contract commenced on 1 July 2024.
TasWater advised the Economic Regulator in October 2025 that the variance
between actual and budgeted facility management opex in 2024-25 was due
to a catch-up in compliance activities and is not expected to be ongoing.
The Economic Regulator also does not consider that TasWater has provided
sufficient information to justify the upward adjustment for its consultancy
spend. On this basis, the Economic Regulator maintains its decision from the
Draft Report on TasWater’s base year opex for 2024-25.
Decision 5.1: To use TasWater’s actual 2024-25 operating
expenditure as the starting point for estimating TasWater’s base
year operating expenditure.

5.4.2 Allocation of costs between regulated and unregulated
services
TasWater is required to remove operating costs associated with unregulated
services to ensure that regulated customers do not subsidise those services.
Sapere’s assessment
Sapere reviewed TasWater’s activity-based costing approach and found it to
be broadly reasonable. However, it concluded that a greater share of costs
should be allocated to unregulated activities and recommended the following
adjustments to TasWater’s opex allocation:
❑

reducing the cost allocation to unregulated recycled water services,
where recycled water is the lowest cost solution for managing
effluence from sewage treatment plants;

❑

increasing the cost allocation to Category 3 and 4 trade waste and
tankered waste customers by including salaries and overheads,
consistent with the treatment of other trade waste customers; and

❑

increasing the cost allocation to stormwater services based on the
revenue received by TasWater for this service.

Sapere also identified several limitations in TasWater’s activity-based costing
model, including its complexity and lack of appropriate supporting
documentation, significant fluctuation in the service volumes for irrigation,
domestic sewage and tankered waste (with insufficient historical data to
support them), and the use of a five-times multiplier to Category 3 and 4
trade waste and tankered waste volumes to account for higher chemical and
energy costs (which Sapere did not consider that TasWater has appropriately
justified). While Sapere questioned aspects of these issues, it ultimately did
not recommend specific adjustments in response to them.
42

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Sapere further found that TasWater had not allocated any costs to waysider
or pressure sewer scheme customers.36 TasWater explained that these
customers pay regulated charges and are serviced in a similar way to
regulated customers and should be treated as such. Sapere considered this
approach reasonable, provided that these customers are also included in
demand forecasts and the associated revenue is treated as regulated
revenue. However, TasWater treated revenue from these customers
(approximately $4.5 million over the regulatory period) as unregulated
revenue, and Sapere did not have sufficient information to identify the related
costs in the activity-base costing model.
In addition, Sapere found that where TasWater had proposed step changes
to individual cost pools, it did not allocate a portion of the step changes to
unregulated services. Sapere considered that approach had not been
justified and recommended a global adjustment to allocate a proportion of
controllable opex step changes to unregulated services, based on the
activity-based costing allocations.
Economic Regulator’s draft assessment
The Draft Report accepted Sapere’s recommended adjustments, including
reducing the allocation of opex to recycled water, and increasing the
allocations to unregulated trade waste and tankered waste customers, and
stormwater customers.
The Draft Report also included a draft decision to allocate a proportion of
TasWater’s proposed opex step changes to unregulated services, based on
the activity-based costing allocation for each opex category. In addition,
noting the complexity of TasWater’s activity-based costing model, the Draft
Report required TasWater to prepare a simplified activity-based costing
model ahead of the next Price Determination Investigation.
To prevent cross-subsidisation, the Draft Report required TasWater to treat
the revenue from waysider and pressure sewer customers as regulated
revenue.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater accepted:
❑

the treatment of revenue from waysiders and pressure sewer
customers;

❑

the reduced allocation of costs to unregulated recycled water
services; and

❑

in principle, the requirement to simplify its activity-based costing
model, noting it would continue to refine the model during PSP5, and

36 Waysiders are customers that receive their water services via bulk water infrastructure. Pressure

sewer scheme customers are connected for full sewerage services but are outside of serviced land.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

43

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

work with the Economic Regulator to ensure the model is transparent
and decision-useful.
However, TasWater did not support increasing the cost allocation to
unregulated Category 3 and 4 trade waste and tankered waste services,
arguing that this would require recovering higher costs from industrial trade
waste customers and result in a significant price increase. TasWater instead
proposed allocating costs on an incremental basis.
TasWater also submitted that costs allocated to its unregulated stormwater
services should not necessarily align with the revenue that it receives.
“…we note the revenue we receive from providing unregulated stormwater
services does not perfectly match the operating costs we incur in delivering
these services (nor should it), particularly as the unregulated revenue is
determined under an agreement with Launceston City Council dating back to
2016.”37

In addition, TasWater did not accept the Economic Regulator’s approach to
allocate a proportion of step changes to unregulated services, arguing that
these step changes directly relate to regulated water and sewerage services
and should not be allocated to unregulated services.
Economic Regulator’s decisions
Sapere did not consider TasWater’s arguments regarding the cost allocation
to Category 3 and 4 trade waste and tankered waste services to be
reasonable. Sapere instead considered that it is appropriate to allocate costs
to these services using the same approach to allocating costs for regulated
Category 1 and 2 trade waste customers.38 The Economic Regulator agrees
that a consistent approach should be applied to allocate costs across all of
TasWater’s services.
Sapere’s final report also notes that TasWater’s argument against the
increased cost allocation to stormwater services conflicts with how the
contract fee is determined. It notes that the contract fee was, in effect,
regulated in 2016 and set by arbitration to be based on TasWater’s
reasonable efficient costs to provide the service.39
The Economic Regulator acknowledges that efficiencies may have resulted
in cost savings since 2016, but notes that any cost reduction should have
been incorporated into the 2021 service agreement with Launceston City
Council. While TasWater’s submission reports stormwater revenue that is
22 per cent higher than its cost estimate based on work orders, it has not
explained these efficiencies or sufficiently justified its costings. On this basis,

The Economic Regulator notes that allocating a proportion of opex step
changes to unregulated services ensures a consistent approach to cost
allocation across all of TasWater’s services. Step changes to controllable
opex categories, such as information systems, are likely to benefit all
customers, and therefore allocating a portion of these costs to unregulated
services is appropriate.
The Economic Regulator also notes that step changes which solely affect
regulated customers, such as costs associated with the next regulated
pricing investigation, have not been allocated to unregulated services. On
this basis, the Economic Regulator has decided to maintain the approach
adopted in the Draft Report.

Figure 5.4: Comparison of the allocation of operational expenditure between regulated

5.4.1 Salary costs
Salaries are the largest cost category, accounting for 48 per cent of
TasWater’s proposed opex for the fifth regulatory period. TasWater proposed
escalating its salary costs using the wage increases set out in its 2025-29
Enterprise Agreement40 and the forecast wage price index for 2029-30. It
also applied a one per cent annual pay point progression.
Sapere considered the ongoing pay point escalation to be unreasonable,
noting that while individual employees may progress through pay points, the
average pay point across the organisation should stabilise over time as
senior staff retire and new staff join. Sapere also found that including this
escalation would push TasWater’s annual salary growth well above typical
rates in comparable businesses.
The Economic Regulator agreed with Sapere’s recommendation to use the
Enterprise Agreement and Wage Price Index to escalate salary costs, while
excluding the pay point progression. Accordingly, in the Draft Report the
40 TasWater’s enterprise agreement is in place until 2028-29. For the final year of the fifth regulatory

period, TasWater proposed to use the forecast wage price index based on the RBA’s Quarterly
Statement on Monetary Policy – May 2025.

46

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Economic Regulator made a draft decision to apply TasWater’s proposed
salary cost escalator without the one per cent annual pay point progression.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission argued against the removal of the one per cent pay
point progression from salary escalation, on the basis that pay point
progression is a non‑discretionary requirement of its Enterprise Agreement
and a standard feature of remuneration across utilities and the public sector.
TasWater submitted that, while retirements and new hires may offset some
progression in theory, in practice it experiences a net pay point progression
of around 0.54 per cent per annum, and on this basis proposed a revised pay
point allowance of 0.5 per cent per annum.
Economic Regulator’s decision
Sapere noted that while the average pay point of employees may temporarily
increase, eventually the average pay point should stabilise or even decline as
more experienced people retire and new staff come onboard. It also noted
that the inclusion of a pay point progression increases TasWater’s salary
increases well above average increases across the economy, as represented
by the Wage Point Index. While Sapere acknowledged that TasWater’s
submission is more reasonable than its original proposal, Sapere has not
changed its view on the prudency and efficiency of TasWater’s requested
wage changes.
The Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s assessment and maintains
the decision from the Draft Report on salary cost escalators.
Decision 5.6: To use TasWater’s proposed cost escalator for
salaries, without the pay point progression.

5.4.2 Growth rate for water connections
TasWater has applied its forecast growth rate for water connections to
50 per cent of its controllable opex, consistent with the approach that was
accepted by the Economic Regulator for the fourth regulatory period. Sapere
considered this approach to be reasonable and the Economic Regulator
agrees, noting that the proposed growth factor is consistent with scale
economies.

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Decision 5.7: To apply TasWater’s proposed growth rate for water
connections to 50 per cent of its controllable opex.

5.4.3 Power cost escalator
TasWater’s proposed power cost escalator is based on the results from its
market analysis. Sapere was broadly satisfied with TasWater’s assumptions
but considered it more reasonable to apply the CPI forecast for 2029-30
rather than TasWater’s assumed a 7.5 per cent increase in TasNetworks’
distribution charge for that year. Applying this recommendation reduces the
cost escalator from TasWater’s proposed 2.22 per cent to 2.13 per cent.
The Economic Regulator agreed with Sapere’s recommendation and made
the draft decision to use a cost escalator of 2.13 per cent for power in the
Draft Report.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater accepted the Economic Regulator’s approach to
replace the assumed 7.5 per cent increase in TasNetworks’ distribution
charge with the CPI forecast of 3.22 per cent. This reduced the power cost
escalator to 2.13 per cent. However, TasWater updated its power cost
escalation methodology to apply TasNetworks’ approved price increases for
2025-26 to 2028-29 across all network tariff components, with CPI applied in
2029-30. On this basis, TasWater submitted a revised overall power cost
escalator of 2.98 per cent.
Economic Regulator’s decision
Sapere has reported that it is satisfied that the revised trend forecast is
reasonable, subject to the revised efficiency target being applied (refer to
section 5.4.6). The Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s
recommendation and has accepted TasWater’s revised power cost escalator.
Decision 5.8: To use a cost escalator of 2.98 per cent for power.

5.4.4 Insurance cost escalator
TasWater also based its insurance cost escalator on market analysis,
proposing an increase above forecast inflation. Sapere considered this to be
unreasonable because the rate of change for insurance premiums has been
falling in recent years.

48

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Sapere notes that, from its experience, other water utilities have assumed a
CPI increase. Therefore, Sapere recommended applying inflation as the cost
escalator for insurance costs.
While the Economic Regulator acknowledged the downward trend in
insurance premiums since 2023, it also notes that the insurance market
remains complex and volatile. Given that TasWater currently operates under
a four-year regulatory period, there is a real possibility that insurance
premiums could escalate during this time. As a result, the Economic
Regulator has adopted a cautious approach to ensure that any allowance for
insurance costs reflects this uncertainty.
Decision 5.9: To use TasWater’s proposed cost escalator for
insurance costs.

5.4.5 Strategic initiatives
The strategic initiatives cost category funds the management and delivery of
non‑business‑as‑usual strategic projects that TasWater considers necessary
to support efficient and effective service delivery.
In its proposal, TasWater applied inflation as a cost escalator for strategic
initiative costs but proposed a 68 per cent increase in 2025-26 to account for
low expenditure in 2024-25. This resulted in proposed strategic initiative
expenditure of approximately $5 million per year for the fifth regulatory
period.
Sapere acknowledged TasWater’s explanation that spending on strategic
initiatives was low in 2024-25, due to a longer than expected transition period
to TasWater’s new Enterprise Project Management Office. On this basis,
Sapere recommended increasing the base-year allocation for strategic
initiatives to reflect TasWater’s average expenditure in 2022-23 and 2023-24.
While Sapere agreed that the introduction of an Enterprise Portfolio
Management Office is reasonable, it noted that TasWater did not provide a
cost-benefit analysis to justify its proposed level of spending. Sapere also did
not support treating the 68 per cent increase as a trend change instead of a
step change for the base year. Sapere instead recommended removing
TasWater’s one-off cost escalator for 2025-26 and instead applying CPI.
The Economic Regulator agreed with Sapere’s recommendations and in the
Draft Report required TasWater to apply a base of $3.8 million for strategic
initiatives and use the forecast inflation rate as the cost escalator for all
operating cost categories except salaries, power and insurance.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

49

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater argued that strategic initiative expenditure is essential to deliver
business‑critical improvements and that the Draft Report’s base‑year
allowance of $3.8 million is insufficient. It submitted that the reduced
allowance would constrain its ability to deliver planned transformation
programs and proposed a $1.2 million increase to establish a $5.0 million
base‑year allowance.
Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator agrees that an Enterprise Portfolio Management
Office can provide TasWater with a focus on, and framework for, efficiency
improvement and innovation. However, it also shared Sapere’s view that
TasWater should ensure initiatives are appropriately planned and that any
investment is appropriately justified, using a cost-benefit analysis where
appropriate.
The Economic Regulator does not consider that TasWater has appropriately
justified an increased base year allowance for its strategic initiatives, noting
the inconsistencies between the allocation that TasWater has requested and
its own modelling, as identified by Sapere. On this basis, the Economic
Regulator has decided to maintain the decisions from the Draft Report.
Decision 5.10: To apply a base of $3.8 million for strategic
initiatives operating expenditure, based on TasWater’s average
operating expenditure for this cost pool in 2022-23, 2023-24 and
2024-25.

Decision 5.11: To use the forecast inflation rate as the cost
escalator for all operating cost categories except salaries, power
and insurance.

5.4.6 TasWater’s proposed productivity factor
TasWater proposed a one per cent productivity factor across its controllable
opex for the fifth regulatory period, noting that recent regulatory decisions in
other jurisdictions had approved productivity factors of between 0.5 and
one per cent. While Sapere agreed that TasWater’s proposed productivity
factor, combined with its proposed growth rate, was at the high end of
comparable regulatory decisions, it noted that most utilities do not have a
dedicated productivity funding pool of the same scale as TasWater’s strategic
initiatives opex. On that basis, Sapere recommended an increase in
TasWater’s productivity factor from one per cent to 1.5 per cent.

50

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator agreed with Sapere’s assessment and made the
draft decision to apply a productivity factor of 1.5 per cent.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater did not agree with the Economic Regulator’s
productivity target of 1.5 per cent. It also disagreed that its strategic initiatives
funding is large in comparison with other utilities or should be factored into
the productivity target benchmarking. TasWater cited Hunter Water’s
dedicated Cost Efficiency and Continuous Improvement and Innovation team
as evidence that other utilities have dedicated funding for efficiency
programs.
TasWater submitted that while its strategic initiative opex funds a range of
prudent and efficient projects, only some of these initiatives will contribute to
efficiency gains, with many supporting other legitimate business needs.
TasWater also referred to the most recent Water Services Association of
Australia benchmarking study it participated in for 2025, which it stated
shows its cost performance is “mixed but reasonable”, with performance
broadly around the industry median in many core operational areas.
Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator acknowledges that a 1.5 per cent productivity factor
is more challenging than targets applied to some other utilities. However, it
considers that TasWater is not yet operating at the efficiency frontier and has
scope to achieve efficiency gains, particularly given its strategic initiatives
expenditure represents around 1.6 per cent of total opex and is intended to
deliver such improvements. On this basis, and noting that comparable utilities
with lower productivity targets are already among the most efficient in the
sector, the Economic Regulator has maintained the Draft Report decision to
apply a productivity factor of 1.5 per cent for the fifth regulatory period.
Decision 5.12: To apply a productivity factor of 1.5 per cent for the
fifth regulatory period.

5.4.7 Capex driven opex and non-revenue water
Sapere recommended adjusting the capex driven opex and opex relating to
the non-revenue water program to be consistent with the recommendations
made by AtkinsRéalis following its review of TasWater’s capex, including:
❑

scaling back the non-revenue water program;

❑

deferring some of the expenditure relating to the Launceston Sewer
Transformation Project to the sixth regulatory period;

❑

deferring the Bicheno Recycled Water Scheme project by one year;
and
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

51

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

deferring upgrades to the customer management and the human
resources management system to the sixth regulatory period.

The Economic Regulator did not accept all of AtkinsRéalis’ recommended
adjustments to TasWater’s capex program in the Draft Report. As a result,
the Economic Regulator also did not accept Sapere’s recommended
adjustments for capex driven opex. Instead, the Draft Report included a draft
decision to defer certain opex associated with TasWater’s capital expenditure
projects, consistent with the Economic Regulator’s decision on the capex
program.
The Economic Regulator has maintained this approach for this Final Report.
The capex adjustments are discussed in more detail in Chapter 7.
Decision 5.13: To defer some of TasWater’s proposed operational
expenditure step changes associated with its capital expenditure
projects in accordance with the Economic Regulator’s decisions
relating to TasWater’s capital expenditure allowance for the fifth
regulatory period.

5.4.8 Regulatory costs
The regulatory cost pool covers licence fees and costs incurred though
regulatory inquiries and price investigations. Sapere recommended adjusting
some of costs included in TasWater's cost pool for strategic initiatives which
it considered should be categorised as regulatory costs, and reducing
customer engagement expenditure related to TasWater’s proposed PSP for
the sixth regulatory period, which Sapere considered was not efficient. While
the Economic Regulator noted Sapere’s comments, it considered it
reasonable to provide TasWater with opex to undertake consultation on its
proposed PSP for the sixth regulatory period. Accordingly, the Draft Report
accepted TasWater’s proposed step changes for regulatory costs and
reallocated the costs from the base year opex to a step change.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater submitted that, while the Draft Report accepted its proposed step
changes for regulatory costs, the cost of the PSP5 investigation was
significantly higher than originally forecast, increasing from $1.0 million to
around $2.2 million due to the depth of regulatory scrutiny and the
requirement to fully fund the Economic Regulator’s consultant costs. On this
basis, TasWater proposed increasing the step change for the next price
determination investigation in 2029-30 to $2.2 million to reflect expected
future regulatory costs.

52

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator considers TasWater’s forecast total expenditure for
the current investigation, and its proposal to use this forecast to establish
regulatory costs for the next Price Determination Investigation, to be
reasonable.
However, it notes that the Draft Report included $1.6 million as the total
regulatory step change for the next Price Determination Investigation in
2029-30 instead of $1.0 million. Therefore, the Economic Regulator has
decided to increase this amount to $2.3 million for 2029-30, to reflect the total
step changes for the next Price Determination Investigation.
Decision 5.14: To increase total regulatory cost step changes for
the next investigation for 2029-30 from $1.6 million to $2.3 million,
an increase of $0.7 million.

5.4.9 Customer collections
Sapere noted that TasWater’s proposed step change to its customer
collection cost pool includes a water conservation program that has not been
demonstrated to be of net benefit to customers. Sapere recommended that
this program not be included in TasWater’s opex and recommended that
TasWater undertake research on the value of conserving its water prior to
spending more promoting water conservation. However, the
Economic Regulator considers it reasonable that TasWater be provided with
opex for a water conservation program.
Decision 5.15: To accept TasWater’s proposed step change for its
water smart program in its regulated operational expenditure for
the fifth regulatory period.

5.4.10 Information systems
Sapere recommended adjusting the cybersecurity step change to be
consistent with the expenditure forecast set out in TasWater’s business case,
and deferring upgrades to the customer management system and human
resources management system to the sixth regulatory period. While
acknowledging that TasWater’s proposal to upgrade four major systems
during the fifth regulatory period is ambitious, the Economic Regulator
recognises that there is a genuine need for TasWater to undertake these
upgrades.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

53

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Decision 5.16: To accept TasWater’s proposed step changes for
information systems costs but reallocate some of this operational
expenditure to unregulated services.

5.4.11 Community relations
The Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s finding that TasWater has
increased its spending on community relations significantly during the current
regulatory period, and TasWater has only provided a limited assessment on
the outcomes of its engagement programs. However, the Economic
Regulator considered that it is important for TasWater to continue to engage
with the community during the fifth regulatory period.
Decision 5.17: To accept TasWater’s base year opex of $1.4 million
for community relations, based on TasWater’s actual opex for
2024-25.

5.4.12 Opex relating to bad and doubtful debts
TasWater has included an allowance for bad and doubtful debts in its opex.
Sapere recommended that bad and doubtful debts should not be included as
opex or in the notional allowable revenue, and instead, should be treated as
foregone revenue. This approach involves adjusting both opex and regulated
revenue with no impact on TasWater’s prices. As discussed in section 2.4 of
this Final Report, the Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s
recommendation and has decided to remove the costs associated with bad
and doubtful debts and reduce regulated revenue by the same amount (refer
to decision 2.3). TasWater accepted this approach in its submission to the
Draft Report.

5.4.13 Opex relating to customer connections
TasWater has included the costs of providing its customer connections
service in its opex. Sapere considered it simpler and more appropriate to
treat the customer connection costs as an unregulated service, as this
removes any risk of incorrectly forecasting the costs and revenue associated
with providing this service. As was discussed in section 2.5 of this Final
Report, the Economic Regulator agrees with Sapere’s recommendation and
has decided to treat customer connections as an unregulated service by
removing the associated opex from TasWater’s notional allowable revenue
(refer to decision 2.4).

6

Regulated asset base

Under the regulatory framework, TasWater is permitted to earn a financial
return on the capital it invests in infrastructure used to provide regulated
water and sewerage services. This return is incorporated into TasWater’s
notional allowable revenue through two key building blocks:
❑

regulatory depreciation, which recovers asset costs over their useful
lives; and

❑

return on capital, which reflects the cost of debt and equity financing.

Both are calculated using the regulated asset base, which represents the
regulatory value of TasWater’s infrastructure and changes over time as
assets are added, depreciated or disposed of.
TasWater is required to maintain two separate regulated asset bases, one for
its existing assets and one for its new assets. TasWater’s existing asset
base is made up of assets transferred from Councils prior to 1 July 2011.
TasWater’s new assets are those purchased or constructed since
1 July 2009.
The closing values of TasWater’s proposed regulated asset bases, as
provided in its financial model in its 30 September 2025 update, are shown in
Table 6.1 and in Figure 6.1.

As noted in Chapter 2, the Economic Regulator’s review of TasWater’s
regulated asset base has identified a suite of issues relating to asset values,
classifications and supporting information. Given the scale and complexity of
the asset base, and limitations in the available data and documentation, it is
not possible to identify exhaustively all errors or to determine their specific
origins.
In response, the Economic Regulator has sought to strike a practical
balance, correcting issues where possible, but adopting a pragmatic path
forward that appropriately balances TasWater’s needs with customer
interests. As a result, the Economic Regulator’s final regulated asset base for
TasWater is $559.5 million lower than TasWater’s proposed regulated asset
base at the end of the fifth regulatory period than (see Table 6.2).

This chapter focuses on the value of TasWater’s regulated assets base and
the appropriateness of the assets in it. Information on asset categories and
asset lives is provided in section 9.3 of this Final Report.

6.1 Reclassification of assets
During the fourth regulatory period, TasWater identified a significant number
of assets (10 668) that had been incorrectly classified as new assets instead
of existing assets. The value of these assets was forecast to be
$238.5 million as at the commencement of the fifth regulatory period. Under
TasWater’s proposal, these assets were reallocated from TasWater’s new
asset base to its existing asset base at the commencement of the fifth
regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator requested detailed information for a sample of
approximately 1 200 assets, including source data and invoices, to verify
TasWater’s proposal. TasWater advised that it could not provide this
information because source data was unavailable for most assets, noting that
only limited information was transferred when TasWater was established.
After further discussion with the Economic Regulator, TasWater advised that
these assets were identified for reclassification due to lower than expected
asset lives. TasWater conducted a review of these assets, using regulatory
accounts from Southern Water, Ben Lomond Water and Cradle Mountain
Water and it was able to reconcile legacy asset identification numbers to
confirm these assets should be reclassified.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

59

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator accepts TasWater’s proposal to reclassify these
assets from the start of the third regulatory period as part of its regulated
asset base roll-forward. This decision is unchanged from the Draft Report.
Decision 6.1: To accept TasWater’s proposed asset reclassification
of assets that have been previously incorrectly classified as new
assets rather than existing assets.

6.2 Economic Regulator’s assessment of TasWater’s
asset registers
During the 2022 Price Determination Investigation, the size and complexity of
TasWater’s Excel‑based asset register significantly constrained the
Economic Regulator’s ability to assess the regulated asset base. In
response, the Economic Regulator requested that TasWater provide updated
asset registers to support its assessment for the current investigation.
TasWater provided six asset registers on 12 September 2025, which were
analysed by the Economic Regulator. A further set of 15 asset registers were
provided on 30 September 2025. However, these were not suitable for
analysis as they did not include any new capital expenditure after 2016 and
were only rolled forward to the end of 2021-22. Accordingly, the
Economic Regulator identified asset disposals using the original six asset
registers, which separated TasWater’s new and existing assets into three
categories: water, sewer and other.41
The review of TasWater’s asset registers identified approximately 51 600
assets which the Economic Regulator considered should not be in
TasWater’s regulated asset base. This was on the basis that these assets:
❑

had a negative asset value or zero asset value;

❑

did not have a listed asset life;

❑

had been disposed of in the registers, but not accounted for in
TasWater’s disposals;

❑

had a useful life that expires before 30 June 2030; and/or

❑

were described in the asset registers as being “retired”, “removed” or
“replaced”.

The Economic Regulator noted that the identified assets account for
approximately 18 per cent of TasWater’s total regulated asset base.

41 Land and easements were excluded from the disposal adjustments, due to the nature of these

assets and these asset categories not receiving depreciation.

60

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator also found that TasWater had used inconsistent
inflation rates between its models. To ensure consistency, the
Economic Regulator used June-to-June inflation rates to calculate
TasWater’s asset register roll-forward.42
The Economic Regulator was not able to verify TasWater’s asset registers
during the 2022 Price Determination Investigation. As such, the Draft Report
incorporated asset disposals from the end of the third regulatory period. Any
assets that were due for disposal before 2022-23 were subtracted from
TasWater’s values in the asset registers at the end of 2021-22.

6.3 Disposal of assets from TasWater’s regulated asset
base
The pricing principles in section 68 of the Industry Act make it clear that
TasWater may only receive a return on investment for assets that are
providing a regulated service to customers. To ensure that TasWater does
not receive a financial return on assets that are not being used to deliver
regulated services, TasWater must dispose of assets and remove them from
its regulated asset base. This ensures that its customers are not paying for
these assets through regulated prices.

6.3.1 Economic Regulator’s draft assessment of TasWater’s
proposal asset disposals
Despite proposing a capital expenditure program of almost $1.7 billion to
construct new infrastructure and upgrade and renew existing assets over the
fifth regulatory period, TasWater identified only $19.9 million of assets for
disposal from its regulated asset bases across the fourth and fifth regulatory
periods (Table 6.3).43

The Economic Regulator’s analysis of TasWater’s proposed disposals for the
Draft Report identified several issues. Specifically, $15.5 million of asset
disposals recorded in TasWater’s supplementary information were not
reflected in the $19.9 million of disposals included in its price and revenue
model, and a small number of assets were double‑counted, resulting in
$326 657 of duplicated disposals. These issues were corrected in the
Draft Report, and resulted in total disposals of $35.4 million.
In addition, the Economic Regulator applied the principle that TasWater
should not earn a return on assets that have reached the end of their
economic lives, are no longer used to deliver regulated services, or have
been decommissioned, renewed or replaced. On this basis, the Draft Report
corrected TasWater’s regulated asset base by disposing of $205.8 million of
assets whose useful lives had expired, removing assets at the Macquarie
Point, Selfs Point and Bryn Estyn treatment plants that are no longer being
used to deliver regulated services, and disposing of a further $1.95 million of
decommissioned and inactive assets in 2023‑24 and 2024‑25.
Disposal of assets at decommissioned plants
During the fourth regulatory period, TasWater upgraded the Bryn Estyn
Water Treatment Plant. In the fifth regulatory period, it plans to
decommission the Macquarie Point Sewage Treatment Plant and upgrade
the Selfs Point Sewage Treatment Plant. Despite these significant capital
works, which involve constructing new treatment plants to replace the
existing facilities, TasWater did not include or forecast any asset disposals at
these sites in its PSP5 proposal.
The regulated asset values of the treatment plants at their expected
decommissioning dates are:
❑

62

Macquarie Point Sewage Treatment Plant: $7.65 million (as at
2026-27);

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

Selfs Point Sewage Treatment Plant: $5.94 million (as at 2028-29);
and

❑

Bryn Estyn Water Treatment Plant: $4.01 million (as at 2023-24).

Once decommissioned, these assets will not be used to provide regulated
services and TasWater will be unable to receive a return. Accordingly, the
Economic Regulator made the draft decision to remove these assets from
TasWater’s regulated asset base in the years that the assets are
decommissioned, in line with the values recorded above.
Disposal of decommissioned and inactive assets
In response to a request from the Economic Regulator, TasWater provided a
list of assets that were determined as being decommissioned or made
inactive in its asset management system in 2023-24 and 2024-25. These
assets were also disposed of from TasWater’s existing regulated asset base
in the Draft Report (Table 6.4).

6.3.2 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission
TasWater did not support the Draft Report’s approach to the disposal of
assets from the regulated asset base other than limited disposals associated
with asset sales. TasWater’s core position is that a “line-in-the-sand” should
be established at 1 July 2022 and a weighted average remaining life
depreciation method should be applied from that date. It considers that the
further removal of individual assets beyond this date is inconsistent with the
finance capital maintenance (NPV = 0) principle and risks under-recovery of
previously prudent and efficient investments.
In its submission, TasWater agreed with the asset disposals prior to
1 July 2022 (i.e. prior to its proposed “line-in-the-sand”) and has removed
assets with zero or negative values, no listed asset lives, or assets that were
retired, replaced or removed when establishing the opening regulated asset
base and weighted average asset lives. However, TasWater does not accept
disposals applied after 1 July 2022 (i.e. after its proposed “line-in-the-sand”).

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

63

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Specifically, TasWater does not accept:
❑

the disposal of approximately $205.8 million of assets whose
individual useful lives have expired;

❑

the disposal of decommissioned assets at Macquarie Point, Selfs
Point and Bryn Estyn treatment plants; or

❑

the disposal of $1.95 million of decommissioned or inactive assets in
2023‑24 and 2024‑25.

TasWater submits that, under a weighted average remaining life approach,
individual asset lives are already reflected in the depreciation profile, and
removing assets when their nominal lives end would prevent full recovery of
the capital costs previously allowed into the regulated asset base. TasWater
argues that residual regulatory values for older or decommissioned assets
are largely the consequence of previously approved depreciation settings,
not inefficiency or over‑investment.
TasWater further submits that the Draft Report’s disposal approach is
inconsistent with established regulatory practice, and that comparable
regulators (including IPART and the AER) generally only remove assets
when they are sold, with the reduction offset by sale proceeds, or to address
changes in asset lives through accelerated depreciation.
Under this new approach, TasWater’s submitted disposal values in its
March 2026 submission decreased to $7.55 million across the fourth and fifth
regulatory period (Table 6.5), significantly lower than the disposals value of
$19.9 million in its PSP5 proposal.
TasWater submitted revised closing values for its existing and new regulated
asset bases in response to the Draft Report, with its proposed closing value
at the end of the fifth regulatory period $77.2 million higher than in its original
proposal.
Other submissions
The Water Services Association of Australia described the
Economic Regulator’s adjustments to TasWater’s regulated asset base as
‘novel and unconventional’ in its submission. It considered that these
adjustments, coupled with a number of other decisions in the Draft Report,
have the net effect of placing TasWater’s financial sustainability at risk.
Similar concerns were raised in Sorell Council’s submission, which noted
that, while the adjustments may be defensible in isolation, their combined
effect may dampen revenue allowances below the level required to support
growth‑aligned service delivery.
In contrast, the Dean Winter MP, Shadow Treasurer described TasWater’s
attempt to include assets in its regulated assets base that have already been
decommissioned, retired or replaced as the most concerning revelation in the
Economic Regulator’s Draft Report. It considered that this was not a mere
64

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

accounting error, but rather an attempt by TasWater to recover costs that
don’t actually exist.
“It is unconscionable that a government-owned utility would seek to earn a
financial return and depreciation from infrastructure that is no longer providing
services to customers.” 44

6.3.3 Discussion
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s view of the treatment
of disposals from the regulated asset base, including the approaches taken
by other regulators.
Need for a corrective approach before establishing the opening regulated
asset base
The Economic Regulator notes that under the current regulatory framework,
TasWater is applying a line-by-line approach to its asset lives. Consistent
with this approach, the removal of individual assets is appropriate where
required.
The Economic Regulator has determined to draw a “line-in-the-sand” at the
end of the fourth regulatory period and apply a weighted average approach
from the beginning of the fifth regulatory period. This differs from TasWater’s
proposal to transition to a weighted average approach at the beginning of the
fourth regulatory period.
In order to draw a “line-in-the-sand”, the Economic Regulator has developed
a pragmatic approach to deal with TasWater’s regulated asset bases and
deal with the vast number of issues and inconsistencies with the asset
registers as discussed in section 2.2. This approach will allow the opening
value of the regulated asset bases to be somewhat more reflective of the
condition, age and use of TasWater’s assets.
The Economic Regulator also acknowledges that the mechanics of having a
nominal weighted average cost of capital, with annual indexation applied to
the regulated asset base and a line-by-line approach to asset valuation and
lives may have left some assets with a residual value well beyond their useful
economic lives. However, it is noted that the use of a nominal regulated asset
base and line-by-line asset registers were introduced in the third regulatory
period at TasWater’s request.
In addition, the Economic Regulator considers the large number of assets
with a residual regulatory value beyond their useful lives reflects errors and
inconsistencies in the application of TasWater’s line-by-line asset
management methodology. This is evident with assets that were meant to
expire between 2016 and 2020, where inflation was lower than the
44 Submission from Dean Winter MP, Shadow Treasurer, page 2.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

65

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

depreciation rate, but which nevertheless continued to hold regulatory value
once inflation increased and are still earning a return.
The Economic Regulator’s pragmatic approach to dispose of assets up to the
“line-in-the-sand” is consistent with the Industry Act and TasWater’s
proposal. Under section 68(1)(a)(i) of the Industry Act, regulated prices are to
allow for a return on assets required to provide regulated services. TasWater
should have demonstrated that an asset’s economic life was likely to be
shorter than that reflected in the depreciation allowances and could have
proposed accelerated depreciation for these assets on an ex-ante basis.
Accordingly, it was TasWater’s responsibility to justify accelerated
depreciation in advance, and higher returns are not permitted on an ex-post
basis. Therefore, the Economic Regulator has disposed of assets that reach
the end of their asset life before the “line-in-the-sand” commences.
Furthermore, as the Industry Act does not permit a return on assets that are
not required to deliver regulated services, the Economic Regulator has
removed assets from the regulated asset base that have been replaced,
retired, and/or decommissioned.
Regulatory principle and precedent
IPART’s Asset Disposals Policy Paper states “assets that are no longer
required to provide regulated services to customers are efficiently sold or
otherwise disposed of, so that customers do not continue to have to pay for
such assets”.45 IPART’s regulatory position is based on the principle that
customers should only pay for assets that actually deliver ongoing services
and aims to allocate the risk and residual cost of stranded or disposed assets
to the business and shareholders, rather than to customers.
The AER removes assets from regulated asset bases when they are sold,
scrapped or removed. Where an asset has been disposed of and not sold, it
is removed at its remaining regulatory value. This position is included in the
National Electricity Rules, which require the regulated asset base to be
reduced to reflect the disposal of assets used to provide prescribed
transmission services.46
Furthermore, in Ergon Energy Queensland Pty Ltd v Australian Energy
Regulator,47 the court determined that regulated utilities cannot assume
immutable recovery of their regulated asset base.
Legislative basis
Section 68(1)(a)(i) of the Industry Act is clear that TasWater may only receive
a return on investment for assets that are providing a regulated service to
customers.

45 IPART, IPART’s asset disposal policy – for water businesses, 2018, page 1.
46 National Electricity Rules, Schedule 6A.2
47 Ergon Energy Queensland Pty Ltd v Australian Energy Regulator [2025] FCA 541.

66

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Guidance applied to disposals
Given the extent of inconsistencies identified and the Economic Regulator’s
need to develop a workable way forward, the Economic Regulator has taken
a pragmatic approach to addressing disposals during the fifth regulatory
period.
The Economic Regulator agrees with TasWater’s assessment that assets
should not be disposed of due only to their asset life after the weighted
average approach begins. However, the Industry Act is clear that TasWater
cannot receive a return on assets that are not providing a regulated service.
Therefore, the Economic Regulator has disposed of assets that it knows will
be decommissioned or removed from the regulated asset base during the
fifth regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator has established guidance to TasWater as to how
disposals should be treated for future regulatory periods.

Economic Regulator’s guidance for disposals for TasWater’s
regulated asset base
Guide 1: Functional redundancy determines disposal
TasWater is required to remove assets from its regulated asset base when
they are removed, replaced, retired, decommissioned or abandoned.

Guide 2: Economic Regulator will ensure disposals are removed
Once the “line-in-the-sand” is drawn and the move is made to the weighted
average approach, the Economic Regulator will ensure that those assets
assessed as removed, replaced, retired, decommissioned or abandoned
will be excluded. The Economic Regulator will not further adjust the
regulated asset base.

Guide 3: TasWater should maintain auditable records
TasWater must maintain auditable records when assets are removed,
replaced, retired, disposed or abandoned of to ensure that the requirements
of section 68 of the Industry Act are met.

6.3.4 Economic Regulator’s decisions
TasWater’s disposals
Based on section 68 of the Industry Act, TasWater is not permitted to receive
a return for assets which are not in use. Therefore, the Economic Regulator
has decided to proceed with disposing of the list of assets TasWater
submitted in its 30 September Update.

Further information on the assets that the Economic Regulator has identified
