Disposal of assets at decommissioned plants
TasWater’s submission raised specific concerns about the disposal of assets
at Macquarie Point, Selfs Point and Bryn Estyn treatment plants.
For Macquarie Point sewage treatment plant, TasWater submitted that the
decommissioning resulted from a Tasmanian Government policy decision to
enable redevelopment of the site, formalised through a Memorandum of
Understanding in 2015. TasWater argued that removing the asset from the
regulated asset base would risk under‑recovery of a prudent and efficient
investment made outside its control, and that the asset should remain in the
regulated asset base to allow full capital recovery. However, under the
Economic Regulator principles’ approach and the Industry Act, TasWater
cannot charge customers for assets in its regulated asset base that are not
being used to provide regulated services. Furthermore, the
Economic Regulator notes that the Tasmanian Government provided
TasWater with $96.5 million to fund the decommissioning and demolition of
the plant.
For Selfs Point sewage treatment plant, TasWater stated that the site is
undergoing an expansion and upgrade, and that some existing assets may
be reused as part of the transformation project. On this basis, TasWater
contended that the assets should remain in the regulated asset base to allow
recovery of the full return on capital. The Economic Regulator notes that
under the Industry Act, TasWater cannot earn a return on assets that are not
being used to provide regulated services. The Economic Regulator considers
70

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

that assets temporarily removed from service should not earn a return during
that period but may be included in the regulated asset base again in a future
regulatory period if they are again used to provide regulated services.
For the Bryn Estyn water treatment plant, TasWater submitted that, while a
new facility was constructed adjacent to the existing plant, assets that
remained fit for purpose were retained and integrated into the upgraded
system. TasWater argued that the Economic Regulator incorrectly removed
the full value of the Bryn Estyn plant and that the assets should remain in the
regulated asset base to allow capital recovery. In assessing TasWater’s
proposal, the Economic Regulator identified that TasWater had only disposed
of seven assets out of approximately 950 assets associated with the site.
Following a review of asset descriptions and asset histories, the
Economic Regulator determined that $4.1 million (49 assets) should be
disposed of and identified 171 zero‑value assets, which were also removed
from the regulated asset base. The draft decision therefore required the
disposal of $4.1 million of assets, out of a total $209 million asset base
associated with the Bryn Estyn site.
Due to data limitations, the Economic Regulator did not update the disposal
values as part of the Final Report process, and has maintained the Draft
Report values.
The Economic Regulator’s decision is to maintain the draft decision and
dispose of the identified assets at the Macquarie Point Sewage Treatment
Plant, Selfs Point Sewage Treatment Plant and Bryn Estyn Water Treatment
Plant.
Decision 6.3: To correct TasWater’s regulated asset base by
treating as disposals decommissioned assets at Macquarie Point
Sewage Treatment Plant, Selfs Point Sewage Treatment Plant and
Bryn Estyn Water Treatment Plant.
Disposal of decommissioned and inactive assets
Assets that are inactive or decommissioned are not permitted to earn
regulatory depreciation or a return on capital. While TasWater submitted that
removing these assets from the regulated asset base breaches the NPV = 0
principle and risks under‑recovery of capital investment, the
Economic Regulator considers that this principle does not override the
requirements of section 68(1)(a)(i) of the Industry Act, which restricts returns
to assets used to provide regulated services. Accordingly, the
Economic Regulator’s decision is unchanged from the Draft Report, and it will
proceed with disposing of inactive and decommissioned assets, noting that
any such assets remaining in the regulated asset base would result in
customers paying for assets that are not in use.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

71

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Decision 6.4: To correct TasWater’s regulated asset base by
disposing of $1.95 million of decommissioned and inactive assets,
as determined by TasWater, across 2023-24 and 2024-25.

6.4 Other corrections to the value of assets in
TasWater’s regulated asset base
The Economic Regulator identified further issues that required corrections to
be made to the value of assets in TasWater’s regulated asset base in the
Draft Report. These issues included the application of inconsistent escalation
rates, which affected the accuracy of opening and closing asset values; the
double counting of capital expenditure, resulting in inflated asset values; and
the failure to remove third-party contributions, which should not form part of
the regulated asset base.

6.4.1 Inconsistent approach to inflation
The PSP Guideline requires TasWater to roll forward its regulated asset base
using CPI inflation published by the ABS and to forecast inflation using the
most recent Reserve Bank of Australia Quarterly Statement on Monetary
Policy. For 2024‑25, the Guideline specifies applying March‑to‑March CPI, as
June 2025 data was not available when TasWater submitted its proposed
PSP5 on 30 June 2025.
In its proposal, TasWater forecast inflation using figures from the PSP4
investigation and the May 2025 Quarterly Statement, applying
March‑to‑March CPI for 2020‑21 to 2024‑25 and June‑to‑June CPI for
2018‑19 and 2019‑20. While this approach was inconsistent with escalation
rates approved in PSP4, the Economic Regulator acknowledges that the
wording of the PSP5 Guideline allowed some interpretation.
For the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator corrected TasWater’s
regulated asset base roll‑forward to consistently apply June‑to‑June CPI
movements and updated forecast inflation using the November 2025
Quarterly Statement of Monetary Policy. This increased forecast inflation
from 2.72 per cent in TasWater’s proposal to 2.95 per cent. Escalation rates
are applied annually to ensure the regulated asset base is maintained in real
terms and reflects current price levels. TasWater’s submission supported the
use of June-to-June CPI. On the basis, the Economic Regulator’s decision is
unchanged from the Draft Report.
Decision 6.5: To correct TasWater’s regulated asset base by
applying June-to-June changes in the consumer price index as
the escalation rate, consistent with the approved PSP4 approach.

72

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

6.4.2 Double-counted capex
As part of updating its regulated asset base, TasWater separated its assets
into three groups: assets that existed before 2009, assets added between
2009 and 2015, and capital expenditure from 2016 onwards. During its
assessment for the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator found that
TasWater had mistakenly included some capital expenditure in more than
one of these categories. To correct the error and ensure that TasWater does
not receive a return on the same assets twice, the Draft Report removed the
duplicated capital expenditure, reducing the regulated asset base by
$60.6 million.
In its submission, TasWater acknowledged its error in including the
double-counted capex. On the basis, the Economic Regulator’s decision is
unchanged from the Draft Report.
Decision 6.6: To correct TasWater’s regulated asset base by
removing $60.6 million of double-counted capex.

6.4.3 Third-party contributions
In preparing its proposed Price and Service Plan, TasWater rolled forward its
regulated asset base from 2015-16. However, TasWater did not remove
third-party contributions that the Economic Regulator had previously
identified during the 2018 Price Determination Investigation.
Under the regulatory framework, TasWater cannot earn a return or recover
costs on assets it did not fund, including those paid for through developer
charges, service introduction charges, government grants, or assets gifted to
TasWater. To ensure compliance, these contributions must be deducted from
the regulated asset base.
TasWater’s 30 September 2025 update incorrectly included these
contributions, which totalled $79.2 million. The Economic Regulator has
removed these amounts, consistent with its earlier decision in 2018.
Despite TasWater’s submission accepting the Economic Regulator’s removal
of $79.2 million in third party contributions, the Economic Regulator has
reviewed TasWater’s revised regulated asset base and identified the change
had not been reflected in its submission. The Economic Regulator therefore
has again corrected for the third-party contributions for the Final Report.
Decision 6.7: To correct TasWater’s regulated asset base by
removing $79.2 million of third-party contributions identified
during the 2018 Price Determination Investigation.

73

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

6.5 Review of TasWater’s regulated asset base
The Draft Report set out the Economic Regulator’s concerns regarding the
accuracy of TasWater’s regulated asset base, the number and extent of
issues identified during the investigation and the constraints imposed by
incomplete asset information and the investigation timeframes.
The Economic Regulator considered it likely that a comprehensive audit
would identify additional assets that should be disposed of, noting that
historical factors, such as inconsistent asset record‑keeping by predecessor
councils and reliance on high‑level assessments at TasWater’s formation
have contributed to ongoing uncertainty.
Acknowledging that TasWater’s understanding of its asset base has
improved over time, the Economic Regulator considered the fifth regulatory
period an appropriate opportunity to require an independent audit of
TasWater’s existing and new regulated asset bases, with sufficient time for
TasWater to implement any recommendations before preparing its proposed
PSP for the sixth regulatory period. The Draft Report therefore required the
regulated asset base to be independently audited by 31 December 2027.

6.5.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater accepted a proposed independent audit on its regulated asset
bases in principle, subject to the scope of the audit. TasWater’s considers an
audit should be forward-looking and not retrospective. TasWater’s view is a
retrospective assessment of the regulated asset bases would risk reopening
matters that had already been examined.

6.5.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator agrees with TasWater that any audit of the
regulated asset base should not be retrospective. Consistent with the
”line‑in‑the‑sand” drawn at the end of the fourth regulatory period, the
Economic Regulator has therefore decided not to undertake an independent
audit of TasWater’s regulated asset bases, as a retrospective review would
risk reopening historical issues and undermine the benefits of establishing a
clear starting point.
Instead, the Economic Regulator will require TasWater to report annually on
the assets it has disposed of, including those it has identified as retired,
removed, decommissioned, abandoned and privately owned or otherwise not
in use.

74

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Decision 6.8: To require TasWater to report annually on the assets
it has disposed of from its regulated asset bases or are no longer in
use.
Furthermore, the Economic Regulator notes that the approach to dealing with
TasWater’s existing regulated asset base will be examined as part of the
review of the regulatory framework to ensure it remains fit for purpose for
both customers and TasWater.
Decision 6.9: To settle the approach for dealing with TasWater’s
regulated asset base going forward as part of the regulatory
framework review.

7

Capital expenditure

Capital expenditure (capex) is the money TasWater invests in purchasing,
constructing and installing assets it uses to deliver regulated services. Under
the building block approach, capex is added to the regulated asset base for
new assets.
Although capex is not a specific building block, it is recovered over time
through two cost components:
❑

regulatory depreciation, which spreads the cost of assets over their
useful lives; and

❑

return on capital, which reflects the cost of financing assets through
debt and equity.

The Economic Regulator only accepts capex that is prudent, efficient and
used to provide regulated services. Capex for assets used to provide
unregulated services, such as the supply of water for irrigation, is not
included in TasWater’s regulated asset base.

The capex allowance adopted for price setting purposes does
not constrain TasWater in setting its priorities or responding to
its assessment of the needs of the network. TasWater may
undertake capital investment above the expected level, and
such expenditure may be added to the regulated asset base
where the Economic Regulator later determines it to be
prudent and efficient.

The price determination also does not guarantee that capex will address
compliance priorities. Responsibility for compliance improvement and
enforcement remains with the relevant technical regulators.
TasWater proposed increasing its capex allowance by 82.6 per cent for the
fifth regulatory period reflecting:
❑

total capex of $1 674 million;

❑

an efficiency target of $100 million; and

❑

a net capex allowance of $1 574 million.

To put the scale of TasWater’s investment into perspective, TasWater had
the highest water and sewerage capital spending per property of any major

76

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Australian water utility in 2024-25, for the second consecutive year.49 Its
PSP5 proposal represents a further step-change increase to this level of
investment.
TasWater is also transitioning from its Capital Delivery Office model, which
commenced in July 2019 and used alliance partners to support capital
delivery. Following the expiry of the alliance agreements on 30 June 2025,
the Capital Delivery Office will focus on completing existing works, including
the Macquarie Point sewage pump station, and will not commence new
projects. TasWater has transitioned to a new procurement model based on
seven delivery frameworks, which it states has strengthened internal
capability and improved its capacity to deliver capital projects.
The Economic Regulator has assessed TasWater’s actual capital
expenditure in the current regulatory period and its proposed capital
expenditure for the fifth regulatory period to determine whether the level of
expenditure is prudent and efficient, and whether the proposed program can
be delivered within the regulatory timeframe.
The Economic Regulator has concluded that it would not be prudent and
efficient for TasWater to attempt to deliver its full proposed capital
expenditure program within the four‑year regulatory period, and that some
projects should be deferred to the first two years of the sixth regulatory
period. Accordingly, the Economic Regulator has determined a lower level of
capital expenditure for the fifth regulatory period. This is shown in Figure 7.1
and Table 7.1.

7.1 Approach for assessing TasWater’s capex
To support its assessment, the Economic Regulator engaged AtkinsRéalis as
an independent expert to review TasWater’s:
❑

capital expenditure during the fourth regulatory period; and

❑

proposed capital expenditure during the fifth regulatory period.

The review focused on a representative sample of capex initiatives that
consisted of 10 programs/projects for PSP4 and 23 for PSP5.50
AtkinsRéalis reviewed TasWater’s proposed capex for the fifth regulatory
period to assess whether the proposed projects and programs were prudent
and efficient. The review evaluated major projects against key criteria such
as appropriateness, supporting analysis, efficiency, deliverability, cost,
prioritisation, and strategic alignment. It also considered how expenditure
was classified by cost driver (growth, renewal, improvements, compliance),
reviewed regulated asset base matters such as asset life, and assessed out
of sequence projects.
As well as examining TasWater’s PSP5 proposal and supporting information,
AtkinsRéalis conducted five days of structured interviews with TasWater,
supplemented by online sessions. These interviews explored governance,
prioritisation, costing, deliverability, compliance, and asset management.
In reaching its decisions, the Economic Regulator considered TasWater’s
justification for the proposed expenditure together with AtkinsRéalis’
assessment and recommendations.
AtkinsRéalis’ final report is provided as an attachment.

7.2 AtkinsRéalis’ general findings and recommendations
AtkinsRéalis found that TasWater had proven that it can deliver large
projects. However, it considered that TasWater would benefit from
50 AtkinsRéalis Final Report, page 17.

78

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

strengthening the demonstration and evidencing of its decision-making with
respect to whether and what to spend customers’ money on. While
acknowledging that TasWater undertook customer consultation in developing
its PSP5, AtkinsRéalis observed that this consultation appeared to be
decoupled from the proposed capital program in terms of both priorities and
investment level.51
AtkinsRéalis recommended that TasWater embed benefits thinking across all
decision-making processes, ensuring customer-focused benefits are
identified, tracked and evaluated consistently. It has suggested that these
changes include:
❑

Business cases: best practice is for business cases to be a genuine
review of all potential options and robust justification for the timing and
case for the intervention. They should set out the benefits which can
then be measured.

❑

Decision framework: it is useful to embed cost-benefit analysis or
similar tools into decision-making, especially for investments which are
not mandatory and for which a robust case therefore needs to be
made. Customer research can be used to create a customer value
framework against which all decisions can be measured.

❑

Measures/targets: businesses, and more importantly customers, can
benefit from having a set of customer-centric focussed outcomes as
targets (like minutes of service interruptions rather than mains breaks).
These targets should be informed by customer priorities and linked to
proposed interventions.

❑

Portfolio management/capex variance: where decisions are made
to significantly amend the program and/or to spend more than the
allowance then good practice is for these decisions to be strongly
justified and formally documented; this includes for renewals programs
as well as discrete projects.

AtkinsRéalis also recommended exploring whether a “gated” or separate
approval process for high‑value and/or high‑risk projects could support
ongoing engagement between price determinations and increase confidence
in the planning of complex projects among customers, TasWater, the
Economic Regulator and other stakeholders.
AtkinsRéalis further identified areas for TasWater’s consideration:
❑

For environmental improvement programs where interventions are not
mandatory, strong justification is essential. The issue being addressed
should be clearly defined, and the proposed solution must be shown
as the most cost-effective and cost-beneficial option, meaning
customers are willing to pay for it.

❑

For renewals, best practice is to use forward-looking risk assessments
supported by strong data and to prioritise actions based on

51 AtkinsRéalis Final Report, page 12 and 13.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

79

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

cost-effectiveness for risk reduction. Alternatives to full asset
replacement should also be considered, as these can often deliver
customer benefits more cost-effectively (e.g., calm networks).
Improving losses and leakage starts with understanding them. It would be
useful to develop an economic level of leakage and a least-cost plan for each
major system, as leakage management depends on local factors such as
water supply costs, loss levels, and pressure management feasibility.
Measures should be prioritised based on the highest net customer benefits or
lowest-regret options.

7.3 Capex for fourth regulatory period
For the fourth regulatory period, the Economic Regulator approved a capex
allowance of $861.9 million, down from TasWater’s proposed capex of
$907.3 million.
As part of its review, AtkinsRéalis examined TasWater’s actual capex from
1 July 2022 to 30 June 2025 and its budgeted capex for 2025-26. In real
terms52, TasWater is expected to exceed its PSP4 allowance by
approximately eight per cent, with almost half of total spending driven by
compliance requirements and a further 37 per cent attributable to renewals.53
Expenditure was broadly balanced between water (44 per cent) and
sewerage (38 per cent), with the remainder allocated to investment that
supports both water and sewerage or to other assets.
In total, TasWater is expected to spend approximately $939 million of capex
over the fourth regulatory period. Table 7.2 compares the Economic
Regulator’s approved capex allowance to TasWater’s actual and expected
spend over the period.

regulatory period, including the North-West Water Supply Strategy and the
Ridgeway Dam upgrade.

processes. Based on the explanations provided, it considered that
TasWater’s approach to renewals, particularly water mains, appears to be in
its early stages, with little emphasis on root cause analysis,
cost-effectiveness, cost benefit prioritisation, or alternatives to asset
replacement (such as calm networking or relining).

However, AtkinsRéalis did not consider there was sufficient evidence to
conclude that any PSP4 expenditure was clearly not prudent or not efficient.
It therefore recommended accepting the actual expenditure for 2022-23 to

AtkinsRéalis recommended relates to 2025-26, where it recommended
accepting TasWater’s estimate that $21 million of Selfs Point expenditure be

The Economic Regulator has considered TasWater’s capex and
AtkinsRéalis’ review of TasWater’s PSP4 capital program and accepts
TasWater’s actual expenditure for the period from 2022-23 to 2024-25,
including the deferral of $21 million in expenditure associated with the Selfs
Point Sewer Transformation Project to 2025-26.
As there were no issues raised during consultation on TasWater’s expected
capex for the fourth regulatory period, the Economic Regulator’s final
decision is unchanged from the Draft Report.

$21 million of Selfs Point expenditure be deferred to 2025-26.

7.4 Capex for the fifth regulatory period
As previously noted, TasWater has proposed a capital program of almost
$1.7 billion for the fifth regulatory period.
The proposed increase in expenditure for the fifth regulatory period is driven
by several large projects, with the largest 10 making up over $1 billion, or
61 per cent, of the total capex program. These 10 projects are:
1. Ti-Tree Bend Sewage Treatment Plant Transformation ($356 million);
2. Ridgeway Dam Upgrade ($144 million);
3. Water Efficiency Program ($101 million);
4. Pet Dam Ridgley Safety Upgrade ($97 million);
5. Water Main Renewals ($95 million);
6. Electrical and SCADA Renewals ($87 million);
7. Metering Renewals ($45 million);
8. Hadspen and Carrick to Longford Pipeline ($34 million);
9. Selfs Point Sewer Transformation ($31 million); and
10. Sewer Main Renewals ($30 million).

82

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater’s proposal included a capital efficiency target of $100 million, or
5.9 per cent of its proposed capital program.
TasWater considers its capital program necessary to urgently address
environmental non-compliance and water security risks. In its proposed
PSP5, TasWater stated that as at June 2024 only three per cent of Level 1
and 26 per cent of Level 2 sewage treatment plants were compliant. 55
However, the Draft Report noted that this was inconsistent with the
information reported by the EPA in 2024-25, which stated that most sewage
treatment plants are compliant for much of the time56, with non-compliance
often occurring during events outside TasWater’s control, such as
wet-weather events.
EPA’s Annual Report for 2024-25 (page 21):

TasWater’s proposed capital program reflects a shift in focus from improving
drinking water quality and microbiological compliance to improving its
environmental compliance and water security. In the Draft Report, the
Economic Regulator acknowledged that this shift aligns with an early
agreement between the three regional water corporations and the technical
regulators during the first regulatory period, which prioritised achieving
drinking water and microbiological compliance first, before focusing on
improving environmental compliance.
TasWater submitted that delaying investment would lead to higher long‑term
costs for customers and that affordability concerns should be balanced
against the need for timely investment. In the Draft Report, the
Economic Regulator acknowledged the need to balance these considerations
and noted that the approved capex allowance does not restrict TasWater’s
actual capital spending or prescribe the specific projects it may undertake.

7.4.1 Economic Regulator’s draft assessment of TasWater’s
proposed capital expenditure
AtkinsRéalis’ review found that while TasWater has demonstrated the ability
to deliver large projects, it has not adequately justified why many projects
must be delivered within the fifth regulatory period.
Key concerns included:
❑

weak evidence supporting the timing and necessity of several large
projects (notably TasWater was unable to demonstrate to AtkinsRéalis
that the Ti-Tree Bend sewage treatment upgrade was necessary, the
best solution or that its customers are willing to pay the costs);

❑

limited use of option analysis, cost-benefit assessment, and
alternatives to full asset replacement;

❑

insufficient linkage between customer engagement outcomes and the
scale of proposed investment;

❑

weaknesses in project governance, cost control, and asset
management practices (notably TasWater was not able to
demonstrate knowledge of how much it had spent on specific projects,
including whether projects had resulted in underspends or overspends
compared to their respective budgets and the outcomes that had been
delivered).

As a result, AtkinsRéalis’ draft report identified substantial reductions,
recommending a total capex allowance of $942 million for the fifth regulatory
period, $627 million less than TasWater’s proposal. This recommended
reduction was driven mainly by removing or reducing funding for Ti-Tree
Bend, Ridgeway Dam, the Water Efficiency Program and parts of the
renewals program.
In the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator agreed that TasWater had not
fully demonstrated the prudency of delivering all proposed projects in the fifth
regulatory period. Taking into account AtkinsRéalis’ advice, stakeholder
feedback (both for and against the scale of the program) received prior to the
release of the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report, affordability impacts and
deliverability, the Economic Regulator adopted a more moderate position.
The draft assessment assumed that the timeframes for some projects should
be extended into the sixth regulatory period rather than those projects being
delivered entirely within the fifth regulatory period. Accordingly, a capex
allowance of $1 175 million was used as the basis to calculate the prices for
the fifth regulatory period in the Draft Report. This was:

84

❑

$398 million lower than TasWater’s proposed allowance;

❑

$233 million higher than AtkinsRéalis’ recommendation; and
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

36.3 per cent higher than the PSP4 allowance.

The draft decision assumed that around $223 million of TasWater’s proposed
capex would instead be spent in the first two years of the sixth regulatory
period, reducing customer price impacts while still allowing a significant
increase in investment compared to the allowance approved for the fourth
regulatory period. The Draft Report emphasised that the Economic
Regulator’s final decision on TasWater’s capex allowance will not constrain
TasWater from spending more than the allowance during the fifth regulatory
period.

7.4.2 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission
In its submission, TasWater opposes the draft decision to reduce its
proposed capital expenditure to $1.175 billion by deferring $398 million of
investment to the first half of the sixth regulatory period and retaining its
proposed 5.9 per cent efficiency target. TasWater submits that reinstating its
full PSP5 proposed capital program is necessary to restore compliance,
stabilise asset performance, avoid compounding future costs, and deliver
outcomes in customers’ long-term interests.
TasWater argues that its proposed capital program is largely
non‑discretionary, with more than half of the investment required to meet
mandatory regulatory obligations. It contends that deferring capital
expenditure would not reduce costs or improve efficiency, but would instead
increase the risk of asset failure, prolonged regulatory non‑compliance,
service disruptions, and higher whole‑of‑life costs for customers. According
to TasWater, while the Draft Report acknowledges the need for substantial
investment, it delays resolution of known risks rather than addressing them
within the PSP5 period.
TasWater further submits that the Draft Report outcomes would prevent
delivery of critical compliance and safety projects, significantly delay the
Launceston Sewer Transformation, restrict growth‑enabling infrastructure
needed to support housing and regional development, and materially
underfund statewide renewals essential to maintaining service reliability.
TasWater also disputes the AtkinsRéalis’ recommendations, asserting they
contain material factual and analytical errors, including misinterpreting interim
regulatory limits as full compliance, understating environmental and safety
risks, and applying inappropriate financial assessments to safety‑critical,
long‑lived assets. In addition, TasWater argues that the Draft Report’s
back‑ended delivery profile is inefficient and undermines both delivery
capability and cost efficiencies.
Finally, TasWater does not support applying the 5.9 per cent efficiency target
to a substantially reduced capital program, arguing that the proposed
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

85

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

efficiency savings depend on scale, continuity and sequencing that no longer
exist under the draft decisions set out in the Draft Report.
Environment Protection Authority’s submission
In its submission, the EPA supports TasWater’s proposed capital program,
particularly investment focused on improving effluent quality and reducing
environmental risks at high‑risk wastewater treatment plants. As the
environmental regulator, the EPA notes that its primary concern is that
proposed works align with environmental compliance requirements and
deliver measurable improvements in effluent quality.
The EPA notes that many of TasWater’s sewage treatment plants are ageing
and operating beyond their original design capacities, and that deferring
capital investment increases the risk of plant failure and environmental harm.
It considers that TasWater’s Environmental Risk Assessment and
Wastewater Risk Management Plan adequately identify and prioritise
higher‑risk plants and align well with the EPA’s compliance priorities.
The EPA supports TasWater’s rationalisation strategy, including the
Launceston Sewer Transformation and upgrades to the Ti‑Tree Bend
Sewage Treatment Plant. While Ti‑Tree Bend is currently meeting its effluent
quality limits, the EPA notes that these limits are interim and do not reflect
accepted modern technology or best‑practice environmental management.
The EPA cautions against interpreting interim compliance as evidence that
current treatment performance is environmentally adequate.
“The current effluent quality limits on the Ti-Tree Bend WWTP were imposed on
then-Ben Lomond Water via an Environment Protection Notice (EPN) in 2013.
These limits were (and still are) considered to be “interim” limits, to be revoked
once final effluent quality limits can be determined.” 57

The EPA’s submission also expresses concern that the Draft Report’s
deferral of capital expenditure may be based on a misunderstanding of the
distinction between interim compliance and long‑term environmental
adequacy. Overall, the EPA considers TasWater’s PSP5 proposal
appropriately prioritises higher‑risk plants, supports meaningful
improvements in effluent quality, and is consistent with best‑practice
environmental management and the State Policy on Water Quality
Management.
Other submissions
The Economic Regulator received extensive feedback from a broad range of
stakeholders in relation to TasWater’s capital program. The feedback reflects
an acknowledgement that TasWater faces significant and growing
infrastructure challenges arising from ageing assets, population growth,
environmental compliance obligations and climate‑related pressures. Several
57 Submission from the Environmental Protection Authority, page 2.

86

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

stakeholders, including Infrastructure Partnerships Australia and a number of
local councils58, accepted that a step‑change in investment is unavoidable
and supported the need for sustained capital expenditure to maintain service
quality and manage long‑term risk. These submissions emphasised that
effective economic regulation requires balancing short‑term affordability with
the long‑term interests of customers, including asset sustainability and
environmental protection.
“Infrastructure Partnerships Australia recognises [the Economic Regulator’s]
role in scrutinising what efficient expenditure is in the long-term interest of
consumers, and that there will often be a natural divergence with a utility’s view
of what is required. This regulatory tension is crucial to efficient outcomes. But
this setting is a two-way valve, where the regulator must also protect
consumers’ long-term interests by allowing sufficient revenue to meet
reasonable investment needs that ensure continued service quality.” 59

At the same time, a number of stakeholders raised concerns about the scale,
sequencing and justification of TasWater’s proposed capital program. The
Tasmanian Chamber of Commerce and Industry and Dean Winter MP,
Shadow Treasurer accepted the existence of infrastructure need but
questioned whether TasWater had demonstrated sufficient methodological
rigour, business case maturity and alignment with customer outcomes to
justify recovering the full scale of proposed expenditure within a single
regulatory period. These stakeholders generally supported the Draft Report’s
approach of moderating and deferring parts of the program across multiple
regulatory periods to improve deliverability, manage market capacity and
reduce cost escalation risks.
“The existence of ageing assets, compliance pressures and service
deficiencies may justify continued investment in principle, but it does not follow
that all proposed expenditure is therefore appropriate for current-period
recovery. For that to occur, TasWater must establish sound business cases,
accurate inputs, disciplined sequencing and a methodology that gives
confidence the resulting price path reflects prudent and efficient expenditure
rather than overstated assumptions or premature recovery. In TCCI’s view, the
draft material shows that threshold has not been met in full.” 60

Other stakeholders expressed strong support for wastewater and sewerage
investment, cautioning that deferral of sewerage projects may increase the
risk of pollution, prolong environmental harm and delay improvements in
receiving waterways.

58 Launceston City Council, Glenorchy City Council, Huon Valley Council, Sorell Council, and

59 Submission from Infrastructure Partnership Australia, page 3.

“Several PSP5 projects are directly relevant to the Derwent. Work on the
Cameron Bay STP, one of the highest risk sites, is scheduled to commence
during PSP5, and Prince of Wales Bay STP is programmed to begin upgrades
in 2026. These projects are essential to reducing discharge risk and improving
the health of receiving waterways.” 61

Growth and development impacts were a recurring theme across
submissions. Several stakeholders, including developer groups and local
councils, expressed concern that reductions to the capital program could
constrain the timely delivery of water and sewerage infrastructure needed to
support housing supply and economic development. These submissions
warned that growth‑enabling projects are often most vulnerable to deferral
under reduced capital allowances, potentially exacerbating Tasmania’s
housing challenges by limiting the availability of serviced land and increasing
development costs over time.
“Between 2003 and 2026, the JAC Group developed fifteen residential estates
in Tasmania, and our capacity to plan and invest depends entirely on certainty
in essential services … At the developer forums, it was made clear that any
reduction in capital expenditure would result in growth-driven projects being cut
first.” 62

Views differed on specific project sequencing decisions. Some stakeholders,
including the Treasurer and Property Council of Australia, supported the Draft
Report’s decision to defer or re‑sequence particular large projects on the
basis of risk, compliance, timing and affordability considerations, while others
strongly supported early delivery of those same projects to address
long‑standing environmental, safety and growth pressures in particular
locations. This divergence highlights the contested nature of project‑level
prioritisation where short‑term compliance status, long‑term environmental
adequacy and broader system outcomes intersect.
“The Government supports the Regulator’s conclusion that it is not prudent for
TasWater to attempt delivery of all capital projects within a four year period.
Extending the program across two regulatory periods is sensible and allows for
better sequencing, improved market-capacity management and reduced costescalation risk, particularly given the number of major projects planned
statewide.”63

Overall, stakeholder submissions demonstrate broad agreement on the need
for continued and substantial investment in TasWater’s infrastructure, but
divergent views on the appropriate timing, scale and evidentiary threshold for
recovering that investment from customers in the current regulatory period.

7.4.3 AtkinsRéalis recommendations
In finalising its capital expenditure recommendations, AtkinsRéalis
considered the submissions from TasWater and the EPA. AtkinsRéalis
acknowledged TasWater’s position that its proposed capital program is
64 Submission from Elliott Baxter.
65 Submission from Malcolm Walker.
66 Submission from Gaylene Tuson.
67 Submission from Geoffrey Phillips.
68 Submission from Nigel Beeke.
69 Submission from The Paneltec Group, page 2.

71 Submission from Dean Winter MP, Shadow Treasurer, page 1.
72 Submission from J.A Walker.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

89

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

largely compliance-driven and that deferral of investment may shift
environmental and service risks to future periods. However, AtkinsRéalis
maintained its view that the appropriate test for inclusion in PSP5 is not the
existence of long-term need alone, but whether the timing, scale and
formulation of expenditure are prudent, efficient and deliverable within the
regulatory period.
In response to TasWater’s submission, AtkinsRéalis reviewed and reaffirmed
its key analytical positions. It did not accept that its assessment relied
inappropriately on narrow financial metrics or ignored asset condition and
statutory obligations. Rather, AtkinsRéalis maintained that its
recommendations reflect a holistic assessment of risk, deliverability, cost
certainty and evidence of cost‑effective solutions, and that where projects
were deferred this was principally due to project maturity, feasibility or weak
justification for delivery in PSP5, not because the projects were unnecessary.
For example, AtkinsRéalis reaffirmed that large and complex projects such
as the Ridgeway Dam upgrade and elements of major sewer transformation
programs are expected to proceed but are more realistically delivered over
multiple regulatory periods given their planning status and historic deferrals.
AtkinsRéalis gave further consideration to TasWater’s arguments on
renewals and water efficiency. While recognising TasWater’s concerns about
declining network performance, AtkinsRéalis concluded that the evidence still
did not support the scale of water mains renewals or the full proposed water
efficiency program as the least‑cost means of achieving customer and
system outcomes. It therefore retained recommendations for reduced
allowances in these areas, while explicitly including funding for enabling
actions such as improved monitoring, leakage detection and “calm network”
measures that it considered more likely to deliver cost‑effective benefits and
improve future investment targeting.
“TasWater does not appear to have considered or built into its submission more
cost-effective ways to reduce bursts and leaks (such as calm networks) or to
prioritise customer impact per dollar spent.” 73

The EPA’s submission was considered in relation to the Launceston Sewer
Transformation and Ti‑Tree Bend sewage treatment plant. AtkinsRéalis
acknowledged the EPA’s clarification that compliance with existing effluent
limits reflects interim arrangements rather than accepted modern technology,
and that long‑term upgrades are required to achieve best‑practice
environmental outcomes. Consistent with this, AtkinsRéalis’
recommendations continue to provide for significant wastewater compliance
investment, including upgrades at Ti‑Tree Bend and broader sewerage
projects. However, AtkinsRéalis maintained that the timing and sequencing of
major investments should be aligned with system needs, upstream transfer

73 AtkinsRéalis Final Report, page 11.

staging and project readiness, and that interim compliance status remains
relevant when assessing urgency within PSP5.

7.4.4 Discussion
The Economic Regulator has carefully considered TasWater’s proposed
capital program for the fifth regulatory period, the independent assessment
undertaken by AtkinsRéalis, and the extensive feedback received during
consultation on the Draft Report.
The Economic Regulator accepts that TasWater faces significant and
growing infrastructure challenges arising from ageing assets, environmental
compliance obligations, population growth and climate-related pressures.
There was broad acknowledgement across submissions that a step-change
in investment is unavoidable over time. However, the existence of long-term
infrastructure need does not, of itself, demonstrate that all proposed
expenditure is prudent and efficient and, therefore, should be recovered from
customers within a single regulatory period.
92

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Consistent with the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator remains of the
view that TasWater has not sufficiently demonstrated that it is prudent and
efficient to attempt to deliver its full proposed capital program within the
four‑year regulatory period. The primary concerns relate to the timing,
sequencing and justification of a number of large and complex projects,
rather than the underlying need for investment. In particular, the
Economic Regulator considers that several projects are more appropriately
delivered across multiple regulatory periods to improve deliverability, manage
cost escalation risks and reduce short‑term price impacts for customers.
AtkinsRéalis’ final report reached similar conclusions. While its revised
recommendation increased the overall capex allowance compared to its draft
advice, AtkinsRéalis continued to emphasise weaknesses in project maturity,
optioneering, cost-benefit justification and evidence linking proposed
investment to customer outcomes. The Economic Regulator agrees that
these issues are central to assessing prudency and efficiency, particularly
where significant costs are proposed to be recovered from customers.
The Economic Regulator notes stakeholder views differed on the appropriate
balance between affordability, deliverability and the risks of deferring
investment. Some stakeholders, including TasWater and the EPA, argued
that delaying investment increases the risk of environmental harm,
compliance failure and higher long-term costs. Other stakeholders supported
a more cautious and staged approach, noting concerns about affordability,
the construction market’s capacity and TasWater’s ability to demonstrate that
customers are willing to pay for the full scale of proposed expenditure at this
time.
As emphasised in the Draft Report, the capex allowance determined for price
setting purposes is an expected level of expenditure used to calculate
regulatory depreciation and return on capital. It is not a spending cap and
does not prescribe the specific projects TasWater may undertake. TasWater
may spend above the allowance, with additional expenditure added to the
new assets regulated asset base if it is subsequently demonstrated to be
prudent and efficient. Responsibility for project prioritisation, sequencing and
compliance remains with TasWater, informed by the requirements of
technical regulators.
Some submissions indicated that misunderstandings arose during the
consultation process, particularly in relation to the Economic Regulator’s
position on the Ti-Tree Bend Sewage Treatment Plant upgrade. The
Economic Regulator has sought to clarify through this Final Report that
decisions regarding the prioritisation and sequencing of individual projects
remain matters for TasWater’s Board. The Economic Regulator’s decision
relates only to the prudent and efficient level of capital expenditure for the
fifth regulatory period.

“The EPA understands that the Economic Regulator is proposing deferral of
some expenditure relating to the Launceston Sewer Project…” 74
“On behalf of West Tamar Council, I write to express Council’s disappointment
at your draft 2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation
report, specifically the recommended deferral of TasWater’s capital item
upgrading Ti Tree bend.” 75

The Economic Regulator acknowledges that one limitation of the current
regulatory framework is that TasWater’s Board receives formal feedback on
capital program prudency and efficiency only at four‑yearly intervals through
the price determination process. In its Final Report, AtkinsRéalis suggested
that it may be worth exploring whether a “gated” or separate approval
process could add value for projects that are high‑value and/or high‑risk.
“We recognise that it can be challenging to advance complex projects to a point
of having full confidence in the solution and costs at the time of price reviews.
In some jurisdictions a “gated” or a separate approval process is used to allow
for ongoing engagement and decision-making between price determinations.
We consider it would be worth exploring whether this kind of process would add
value and increase confidence in complex project planning for customers,
TasWater, the Regulator and other stakeholders.”76

As noted above, the Economic Regulator recognises that TasWater’s Board
is responsible for the planning, prioritisation and sequencing of TasWater’s
capital program, while the Economic Regulator’s role is to assess the
prudency and efficiency of capex and determine whether, and to what extent,
it should be funded by TasWater’s regulated customer base. In this context,
the Economic Regulator considers that there may be benefits to more regular
engagement and increased transparency for certain high-value and/or
high-risk projects that TasWater intends to fund through regulated revenue.
The Economic Regulator will give this further consideration as part of its
broader review of TasWater’s regulatory framework.
TasWater also submitted that its proposed capital efficiency target relies on
scale and may not be achievable with a reduced capital program. In this
regard, the Economic Regulator notes AtkinsRéalis’ conclusion that a
5.9 per cent efficiency challenge remains reasonable, given TasWater’s
current level of asset management maturity, and that the efficiency target is
appropriately retained as a percentage rather than a fixed dollar amount.

7.4.5 Economic Regulator’s decision
In the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator adopted a moderate position
between TasWater’s proposed capital program and AtkinsRéalis’ draft
recommendations, approving a total capex allowance of $1 175 million for the
fifth regulatory period. This reflected the Economic Regulator’s view that the
timeframes for some projects should be extended with delivery occurring
across the fifth and sixth regulatory periods, rather than these projects being
completed entirely within the fifth regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator has considered the revised recommendations in
AtkinsRéalis’ Final Report, along with submissions received during
consultation. While AtkinsRéalis’ Final Report recommends a slightly higher
total allowance of $1 181 million, the Economic Regulator does not consider
this difference to be material in the context of the overall program. The
Economic Regulator also notes that key elements of AtkinsRéalis’ revised
recommendations align with the Draft Report assumptions, particularly in
relation to project timing and deliverability.
Having regard to all relevant matters, the Economic Regulator has decided
not to change the total capital expenditure allowance from the Draft Report.
The approved capex allowance for the fifth regulatory period remains at
$1 175 million (nominal), which is:
❑

$394 million lower than TasWater’s proposed post-efficiency capex;

❑

approximately $6.0 million lower than the allowance recommended in
AtkinsRéalis’ Final Report; and

❑

a 36.3 per cent increase, compared to the regulatory allowance that
was approved for the fourth regulatory period.

Decision 7.2: To approve a total capex allowance of $1 175 million
for the fifth regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator considers this allowance appropriately balances the
need for increased investment with affordability, deliverability and the
requirement that expenditure be prudent and efficient. It allows for a
substantial increase in capital investment relative to the fourth regulatory
period, while reducing the risk of inefficient expenditure being recovered
prematurely from customers.
The Economic Regulator accepts TasWater’s submission that the annual
capex profile in the Draft Report may not be realistic or efficient. The
Economic Regulator considers that TasWater may deliver a higher level of
capex in the first years of the regulatory period than allowed for under the
capex provided in the Draft Report. Accordingly, the Economic Regulator has
re-profiled the capex allowance to smooth expenditure across the regulatory
period. This updated profile takes into account practical delivery
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

considerations, allowing TasWater to undertake more preparatory works in
the first two years of the regulatory period.

❑

AtkinsRéalis’ concern that commissioning a new sewage treatment

8

Return on capital

The return on capital building block of the notional allowable revenue is the
cost of financing TasWater’s operations efficiently and sustainably. The
Economic Regulator uses a Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC) to
set a rate of return on capital. The value of capital is based on the value of
TasWater’s regulated asset base.
The WACC combines the cost of equity and the cost of debt, weighted by
the proportion of each form of capital. The cost of equity is calculated using
the risk‑free rate, market risk premium and equity beta. The cost of debt is
calculated using market rates for corporate bonds, based on TasWater’s
assumed credit rating with the addition of debt issuance costs.

8.1 Calculating the Weighted Average Cost of Capital
The Economic Regulator uses a benchmark WACC based on a notional
efficient business, rather than TasWater’s actual capital structure. This
approach incentivises TasWater to finance its operations in an efficient
manner.
The WACC is a forward-looking value and therefore involves predicting future
values for the inputs. The derivation of the values is subject to ongoing
debate between academics, regulators, regulated entities and other
stakeholders.
Regulators operate under different legislative frameworks, which can lead to
variation in WACC parameters across jurisdictions and industries. As noted
by the Essential Services Commission of South Australia, jurisdictions select
rate-of-return methodologies to suit to their own requirements and
circumstances and that there are limitations and risks in making
cross-jurisdictional comparisons.77
Given the inherent uncertainty and often conflicting evidence underpinning
WACC parameters, determining an appropriate rate of return necessarily
involves regulatory judgement and discretion. In this regard, the
Economic Regulator agrees with the Queensland Competition Authority’s
view that there is no single correct approach given the inherent uncertainty in
estimating the rate of return. 78
Consequently, in determining a benchmark WACC, the Economic Regulator
has considered market information and the rate of return methodologies and
parameter values used in other jurisdictions, while having regard to
Tasmania’s regulatory framework and pricing principles.

77 ESCOSA, SA Water, Regulatory Water Determination, June 2020, page 277.
78 QCA, Rate of return review, November 2021, page 2.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

97

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Industry Act requires different treatment for TasWater’s two regulated
asset bases. For existing assets, the rate of return includes a commercial
return on debt and a fixed pre‑tax return of three per cent on equity. For new
assets, the rate of return reflects a commercial, risk‑based return on both
debt and equity.
Therefore, two separate WACCs are calculated for TasWater’s assets:
❑

WACCEXISTING: for assets transferred before 1 July 2011, from the
previous water corporations, with a fixed return on equity of
three per cent and cost of debt; and

❑

WACCNEW: for all assets acquired from 1 July 2011 onwards and used
to provided regulated services.

The WACC formulae are set out in Appendix A7 of this Draft Report.

8.2 Economic Regulator’s assessment of TasWater’s
proposed WACC
The Economic Regulator has accepted TasWater’s proposal to use a
nominal vanilla WACC79 for the fifth regulatory period, which is consistent
with the third and fourth regulatory periods. TasWater’s proposed WACC
parameters for the fifth regulatory period are set out in Table 8.1.

8.2.1 Gearing ratio
The gearing ratio shows the proportion of debt compared to equity in a
company’s capital structure. In simple terms, it indicates how much of the
business is financed through borrowing versus shareholder investment.
Regulators tend to use a standard gearing ratio (predominately 60 per cent)
to ensure that customers do not pay for inefficient financing.
TasWater has proposed to maintain the 60 per cent gearing ratio used for the
fourth regulatory period for the fifth regulatory period. The Economic
Regulator accepts TasWater’s proposal as this ratio has been used in the
past four investigations and there is no evidence to support an alternative
value.
Decision 8.1: To use a gearing ratio of 60 per cent for the fifth
regulatory period.

8.2.2 Total cost of debt
The total cost of debt is the sum of the interest on debt and the cost of
issuing that debt and should reflect, as far as practicable, how an efficient
business manages its debt.

8.2.3 Credit rating
The credit rating assigned to a business is an assessment of a business’s
ability to meet its financial obligations and affects its borrowing costs. A
higher credit rating results in a lower interest rate, all other things being
equal. Economic regulators generally use a credit rating of at least BBB for
setting debt costs, as BBB- is the minimum rating for investment grade
debt.80
In the absence of published credit ratings for Australian water utilities, the
Economic Regulator considered the credit ratings of energy network
businesses as these businesses are considered comparable to water utilities.
Table 13 of the Rate of Return Annual Update 202481 published by the AER
shows that over the four years from 2021 to 2024, 46.3 per cent of
businesses were rated A-, 31.3 per cent BBB+, and 22.4 per cent BBB.82
The Economic Regulator considered that water utilities should generally have
a higher credit rating than an electricity network business as water utilities
tend to have more stable and predictable demand. The regulation of
80 Investment grade debt refers to debt securities that are rated as having a relatively low risk of

default.
81 AER, Rate of return Annual update, December 2024, pages 23-24.
82 Ibid.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

99

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

electricity is more complex and technical changes along with the move to
decarbonise the electricity system increases the risk of stranded assets.
The Economic Regulator also considered international credit ratings. In the
USA, most water companies have a credit rating of A- or higher, reflecting
that water utilities are generally seen as low risk and therefore have a higher
credit rating than the minimum rating for investment grade debt.
UK water utilities tend to have lower credit ratings due to a range of local,
specific issues including high debt and large spending requirements. Despite
this, UK listed water utilities such as United Utilities, Severn Trent and
Anglian Water have maintained credit ratings of BBB+ and BBB. While the
UK circumstances don’t apply to TasWater, this suggests that the benchmark
credit rating for estimating TasWater’s WACC should be higher than BBB+.
Based on the information presented, the Economic Regulator’s draft decision
was to use an A- credit rating to calculate the cost of debt component of the
WACC.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission states that it does not accept the draft decision to
raise the benchmark credit rating from BBB+ to A-. The submission questions
the appropriateness of using USA water companies as comparators, on the
basis that all of the companies selected have average gearing ratios that are
materially lower than the 60 per cent benchmark level of gearing adopted by
the Economic Regulator.
TasWater further notes that no regulators of water businesses in Australia
have adopted a benchmark credit rating as high as A-.
Economic Regulator’s discussion
Credit ratings are external, market-based assessments of default risk. They
reflect a wide range of factors, including:
❑

business risk (stability of demand, monopoly protections);

❑

regulatory risk;

❑

financial risk (gearing, coverage ratios);

❑

governance and ownership; and

❑

extraordinary support (e.g. government backing).

The Economic Regulator has reviewed the debt to capital ratios of the US
comparators used in the Draft Report and, based on the sources cited,
identified the gearing ratios for the companies as set out in Table 8.3.

However, there is not a one-to one relationship between the between gearing
ratios and credit ratings, with rating agencies putting significant weight on
qualitative factors such as business risk, revenue stability, management
quality and capital planning. This is illustrated in the difference credit ratings
for Oklahoma City Water Utilities Trust (OCWUT) and Middlesex Water
Company. OCWUT has a AAA S&P credit rating with a gearing ratio of
around 44 per cent. Middlesex Water Company has a similar gearing ratio at
around 43 per cent but has an A credit rating. The different credit ratings
reflect that OCWUT has:
❑

a broad, diversified services area therefore is less exposed to
economic cycles and has stable revenue;

❑

a well designed capital program that factors in growth, renewals and
supply security;

❑

exceptionally strong financial management and planning; and

❑

very strong management practises.84

Furthermore, the benchmark gearing ratio is a notional construct designed to
estimate the cost of capital for an efficient benchmark business. It does not

83 Eqvista, Debt to Equity Ratio by Industry, March 2 2026.

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

imply that an efficient utility operating with that gearing ratio will necessarily
achieve any particular credit rating.
Credit ratings used by Australian regulators for a benchmark business for
regulated water utilities are in the BBB- to BBB+ range based on the S&P
ratings structure. These are primarily based on the benchmark business
being able to obtain finance on reasonable terms by having, at least, a
minimum investment grade rating. However, based on the underlying
premise that the benchmark business is efficiently operated and financed, the
Economic Regulator maintains that evidence (credit ratings of US and UK
comparators and the credit rating of Australian electricity network businesses
specified in the Draft Report) supports a higher rating than BBB+. Using a
lower credit rating would penalise customers through higher prices and
reward a regulated business for risk that it does not face
Economic Regulator’s decision
Taking all of the available information into account, including TasWater’s
submission, the Economic Regulator remains of the view that a credit rating
of A- appropriately reflects the low business risk, stable demand
characteristics and essential service nature of water and sewerage services.
The Economic Regulator’s final decion is therefore to use an A- credit rating
in calculating the cost of debt for the return on capital.
Decision 8.2: To use an A- credit rating for calculating the cost of
debt for TasWater for the fifth regulatory period.

8.2.4 Calculating the cost of debt
As previously noted, the actual gearing of TasWater is not used in the
WACC, with Australian regulators, including the Economic Regulator, using
some form of standardised approach to determining the cost of debt
component of the WACC.
All Australian regulators use some form of trailing average to calculate the
cost of debt,85 with a common approach being 10 equally weighted tranches
of debt each with a 10-year maturity. The cost of debt is then calculated as
the average of the yield over a 10-year period with each year’s yield
contributing 10 per cent to the final value. Each year, the oldest debt is
removed from the calculation and replaced with new debt at the current rate.
TasWater’s rate of return is set for the duration of the regulatory period. If a
simple 10-year average of interest rates was used, some of the rates would
be outdated by the end of the regulatory period. To address this, the

85 Trailing average is type of backward-looking moving average in which the most recent data is added,

while the oldest data is removed.

102

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Economic Regulator uses a forward-looking cost of debt, which replicates an
annually updated trailing average.
To achieve this outcome, during each price determination investigation, the
Economic Regulator updates the cost of debt each year. For the portion of
debt that will mature during the next regulatory period, the
Economic Regulator uses current interest rates. Consequently, over four
years, 40 per cent of the debt is replaced at current rates. This approach
encourages TasWater to invest efficiently, rather than relying on outdated
debt costs.
The Economic Regulator therefore will calculate the A- rated cost of debt for
the fifth regulatory period as follows:
1. Calculate the average yields of the RBA’s non-financial corporate A
rated bonds with a target tenor of 10 years over the preceding
two-month period.
2. Calculate the simple average of monthly values of the preceding nine,
eight, seven, and six years of yields of the non-financial corporate A
rated bonds with a target tenor of 10 years.
3. Calculate an average of the following:
90 per cent: monthly average bond
yield over the past nine years

+

10 per cent: recent 2-month
average bond yield

80 per cent: monthly average bond
yield over the past eight years

+

20 per cent: recent 2-month
average bond yield

70 per cent: monthly average bond
yield over the past seven years

+

30 per cent: recent 2-month
average bond yield

60 per cent: monthly average bond
yield over the past six years

+

40 per cent: recent 2-month
average bond yield

The Economic Regulator will repeat the above process using BBB rated
bonds to obtain a weighted average of the A rated value (two thirds) and the
BBB rated value (one third).
Decision 8.3: To calculate TasWater’s cost of debt using two thirds
A rated and one third BBB rated RBA corporate bond yields as
provided in the RBA’s statistical Table F3.
