2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

8.2.5 Debt issuance costs
Economic regulators generally include an allowance for debt issuance costs
to compensate a business for costs related to raising or refinancing debt
such as legal fees, underwriting fees, and credit rating fees.
The Economic Regulator included debt issuance costs of eight basis points
(0.08 per cent) in the debt costs for the fourth regulatory period and TasWater
has proposed a debt issuance cost allowance of eight basis points in its
proposed PSP5.
The Economic Regulator notes that the AER has calculated debt issuance
costs of between 7.9386 and 7.9887 basis points based on market data for
AusNet Services and SA Power Networks respectively and applied debt
issuance costs of 888 and 8.589 basis percentage points for the most recent
reviews for AusNet Services and SA Power Networks respectively.
Consequently, based on publicly available evidence the Economic Regulator
accepts TasWater’s proposal to include debt issuance costs of eight basis
points.
Decision 8.4: To include debt issuance costs of eight basis points
in the cost of debt for the fifth regulatory period.

8.2.6 Total cost of debt
Based on the above approach, the Economic Regulator calculated a cost of
debt, excluding debt issuance costs, of 4.60 per cent and a total cost of debt,
including debt issuance costs (0.08 per cent) of 4.68 per cent.

8.2.7 Cost of equity
The cost of equity is the return that investors require as compensation for the
risk of investing in a business. Australian regulators primarily use the capital
asset pricing model to calculate the expected cost of equity. The formula for
this model is included in Appendix A7 and the components used in the
formula are:
❑

the expected required return on equity;

❑

the risk free rate with respect to equity;

86 AER, AusNet Services Distribution Determination 2021-26, April 2021,page 2-26.
87 AER, SA Power Networks Determination 2020-25, June 2020, page 3-14.
88 AER, AusNet Services Transmission Determination 2022-27, April 2021, Attachment 3, page 10.
89 AER, SA Power Networks Electricity Distribution Determination 2025-2030, September 2024,

Attachment 3, page 6.

104

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

the equity beta for the business - the expected returns of the business
relative to returns of the entire market; and

❑

the market risk premium - i.e. the market return (for all businesses)
less the risk free rate.

The capital asset pricing model assumes investors are only rewarded for
risks that affect the whole market (systematic risk), because business
specific risks can be reduced by diversification. On this basis, only systematic
risk matters when calculating the return on equity.

8.2.8 Risk free rate
The risk-free rate refers to a theoretical rate of return on an investment with
no risk and is used to calculate the cost of equity. In Australia, it is a
generally accepted practice to use the return on a government security as a
proxy for the risk-free rate with the yield on 10-year Australian Government
bonds commonly used.
The Economic Regulator has previously used a 40-day trailing average on
10-year Australian Government bond yields for calculating the risk-free rate
in past investigations and TasWater has used this approach in its proposed
PSP5. Therefore, the Economic Regulator will continue to use this approach
for the fifth regulatory period.
Decision 8.5: To use the 40-day average bond yield on 10-year
Australian Government bonds to calculate the cost of equity
component of the weighted average cost of capital.

8.2.9

Market risk premium

The market risk premium is the expected return above the risk-free rate for
investing in the market. As the value is forward looking and not directly
observable, estimating the market risk premium is complex and often
debated among regulators, academics and industry stakeholders.
In 2018 the AER,90 Economic Regulation Authority of Western Australia91
and IPART92 reviewed how the market risk premium should be determined.
All three regulators concluded that there was no consistent view as to the
best method to determine a benchmark market risk premium. The
Economic Regulator’s Draft Report for the fourth regulatory period includes
an overview of the difficulties in determining the market risk premium.

90 AER, Rate of return - Explanator Statement, December 2018, pages 220-275.
91 ERA WA, Gas Rate of Return Guidelines Explanatory Statement, December 2018, pages 29-32.
92 IPART, Review of our WACC method, Final Report, February 2018, Section 5.5.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

105

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The AER’s Rate of Return Annual Update, December 202493 chapter on the
market risk premium includes historical excess returns, dividend models and
surveys. Based on the information provided, the Economic Regulator
considers there is insufficient evidence to support a different value to
six per cent.
The Economic Regulator required TasWater to use a six per cent market risk
premium for the fourth regulatory period and TasWater has used a market
risk premium of six per cent in its WACC calculation for the fifth regulatory
period.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
While TasWater’s submission notes that it accepts the Economic Regulator’s
draft decision to maintain a market risk premium of six per cent, it contends
that there is compelling evidence that the market risk premium has increased
to 6.3 per cent.
Economic Regulator’s decision
TasWater’s submission states “In the final decision for PSP4, the
[Economic Regulator] approved an estimate of 6.0 per cent, that being the
estimate derived by the AER exclusively using the arithmetic average of
historical excess returns from 1988 to 2017, assuming an imputation credit
utilisation rate of zero.”94
The Economic Regulator’s final decision for the fourth regulatory period
supported statements by the AER and the Economic Regulation Authority of
Westen Australia that the benchmark market risk premium estimate is based
on consideration of a number of factors and that six per cent is an
appropriate value.
The Economic Regulator maintains this view and reiterates the sentiments of
various regulators, banks and valuation practitioners that there is no single
definitive method to determine the market rate premium and the value should
be determined with reference to multiple sources which are informative but
not determinative.
As the market risk premium is forward looking, contemporary regulatory
practice places less weight on historical evidence and greater weight on
forward looking market indicators, such as prevailing market rates.
Therefore, in determining the appropriate market risk premium for the fifth
regulatory period, the Economic Regulator also had regard to recent
forward-looking market evidence, including the Reserve Bank of Australia’s
analysis of implied equity risk premia published in its Statements on
Monetary Policy. This analysis indicates that implied equity risk premia have

93 AER, Rate of Return Annual Update, December 2024, pages 17-19.
94 Submission from TasWater, page 105.

106

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

trended downward in recent periods, reflecting changes in market pricing,
interest rate expectations and risk appetite.
This evidence does not support the proposition that a higher market risk
premium is currently required. Rather, it is consistent with the view that a
six per cent market risk premium remains reasonable and appropriate in the
prevailing market environment.

Decision 8.6: To accept TasWater’s proposal to maintain a market
risk premium of six per cent.

8.2.10 Equity beta
The equity beta measures the risk (volatility) of the returns from a class of
businesses compared to the financial market as a whole and is not intended
to reflect the risk of any one individual business.
Given the lack of Australian listed water utilities, regulators tend to look to
comparable businesses in Australia and overseas.
As they are generally regulated monopoly utilities, electricity network
businesses provide a reasonable Australian benchmark. The AER’s Rate of
Return Annual Update 202495 provides evidence of equity beta estimates
clustered around a value of between 0.5 and 0.6.
The Economic Regulator also considers it reasonable to conclude that water
utilities should have a lower equity beta than energy transmission and
distribution businesses, as they are considered to have lower systemic risk
for the following reasons:
❑

water has very low price elasticity because it is essential for health
and basic needs, so consumption changes little when prices rise.
Electricity is more elastic since users can reduce usage through
efficiency measures or switching off appliances; and

❑

water utilities face lower risk from decarbonisation compared to
electricity businesses, which are exposed to stranded asset risks,
energy source substitution, technological shifts and policy changes.

According to Professor Johnstone (one of the Eligible Experts selected by the
AER for the 2026 Rate of Return Instruments review), the equity beta and
cost of capital should depend on the size and variability of a firm’s cashflows
relative to market returns. A regulated monopoly with inelastic demand has
stable and predictable cash flows, making it a non-cyclical industry. As such,

95 AER, Rate of Return Annual Update 2024, December 2024, page 15.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

107

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

it is viewed as a lower-risk investment and is expected to have a relatively
low beta. 96,97
Due to the lack of suitable Australian comparators, Australian regulators
often rely on international comparisons to determine a base beta value, then
apply judgement to arrive at a final value. While using international
comparators is potentially the only viable option for determining an equity
beta, there are a number of issues that need to be addressed. These issues
include: 98
❑

normalising the international data for Australian conditions;

❑

reviewing the process for de-levering and re-levering beta to reduce
bias in equity beta estimates; and

❑

adjusting for the differences in the market indices of international
comparators and Australia.

Choosing suitable comparators is challenging. To create a large sample,
regulators often include companies that differ significantly from a water utility
like TasWater. For example, UK’s Severn Trent has business services and
renewable energy operations, and most US comparators are diversified
across multiple industries. However, the Economic Regulator has found no
evidence that other regulators adjust beta calculations to account for
businesses with substantial non-water activities.
Judgment is essential when considering consultants’ reports because the
equity beta is not fixed, rather it depends on statistical methods and
assumptions. Decisions about the estimation technique, time period, data
frequency, and adjustments, as well as factors such as gearing and tax
assumptions, can significantly change the result.
TasWater proposed an equity beta of 0.65. The Economic Regulator
considered reducing the equity beta below this level, but on balance has
made a draft decision to retain an equity beta of 0.65 for the fifth regulatory
period and to review the equity beta prior to the sixth regulatory period.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission notes that while it accepts the draft decision to
maintain an equity beta estimate of 0.65, there is very compelling empirical
evidence to support an equity beta estimate of 0.9. This is based on a
sample of water comparators used in recent decisions by the Queensland
Competition Authority and the Independent Pricing and Regulatory Tribunal.
TasWater’s submission argued against comparing its equity beta against the
beta estimates for energy entities and adjusting those estimates based on
erroneous assumptions about the relative risk of energy and water networks.

96 D. Johnstone, Regulated Rate of Return: Beta and its Drivers, November 2025.
97 AER, 2026 Rate of Return Instrument review - Eligible Expert’s joint report, November 2025.
98 AER, 2026 Rate of Return Instrument review - Eligible Expert’s joint report, 24 November 2025.

108

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasCOSS’ submission recommends that the Economic Regulator adopt a
lower value equity beta at 0.5 per cent. This is based on the reasonability of
the argument that water utilities should have a lower equity beta than energy
transmission and distribution businesses. TasCOSS also noted that it
supported a recent submission to the AER seeking to reduce the equity beta
for regulated electricity networks from 0.6 to 0.5, arguing that it better
reflected an upward bias to network providers rather than outcomes for
consumers.
The Treasurer’s submission acknowledges the Economic Regulator’s
challenging task in setting a stable and predictable return on capital.
However, it notes that the return must be proportionate to TasWater’s actual
risk profile, with TasWater operating as a statutory monopoly with stable,
regulated revenues, predictable demand and no competitive entry risk. On
this basis, the submission argues that there is merit in re‑assessing the draft
equity beta, to ensure customers do not over‑pay for risks TasWater does not
bear while still supporting ongoing investment and financial sustainability.
Economic Regulator’s decision
TasWater’s assertion that empirical evidence supports an equity beta of at
least 0.9 is based solely on the betas of a sample of international companies
which TasWater provided as comparators.
As discussed in the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator considers that
international comparisons are not appropriate for Australian regulated
businesses due to fundamental differences in capital market conditions,
regulatory frameworks, industry structures, and the way systematic risk is
priced across jurisdictions. For example, Companhia de Saneamento Básico
do Espírito Santo operates in an emerging‑market regulatory and economic
environment, exposing it to substantially higher non‑diversifiable sovereign,
political, regulatory and macroeconomic risks than those faced by business in
Australia. These risks include regulatory intervention, political influence,
inflation volatility and broader capital‑market risk, all of which increase
systematic risk and therefore required returns.
Therefore, without applying adjustments for differences between jurisdictions,
the Economic Regulator maintains that these differences materially limit the
relevance and reliability of international beta comparisons.
In addition, a number of the companies cited are not suitable comparators.
For example, Pure Cycle Corp, which is an unregulated company involved in
land development, water rights monetisation, and real estate linked growth,
rather than providing regulated essential water and wastewater services.
Consequently, its revenue is affected by property development cycles,
population growth assumptions, and water asset sales, which expose it to
higher systematic risk compared a regulated essential service.
Having considered the views expressed by stakeholders during the
consultation period, the Economic Regulator has decided to retain an equity
beta of 0.65 for the fifth regulatory period.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

109

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator will review the equity beta prior to the sixth
regulatory period.
Decision 8.7: To retain an equity beta of 0.65 for the fifth regulatory
period and review the equity beta prior to the sixth regulatory
period.

9

Regulatory depreciation

Regulatory depreciation makes up a large part of TasWater’s costs that
factor into its notional allowable revenue. Because TasWater is a
capital-intensive business, depreciation accounts for approximately
25 per cent of its annual notional allowable revenue.
Regulatory depreciation enables TasWater to recover the cost of investing in
assets over their economic life. The amount of regulatory depreciation
depends on:
❑

the depreciation method used;

❑

the economic life of assets;

❑

the rates of depreciation; and

❑

the value of the assets in TasWater’s regulated asset bases.

TasWater does not receive regulatory depreciation on assets which are not
being used to provide regulated services.

9.1 Economic Regulator’s pre-investigation work
As discussed in Chapter 6, the Economic Regulator could not open, or verify
the contents of, TasWater’s asset register during the 2022 Pricing
Investigation. In the absence of any other data, the Economic Regulator was
left with no alternative but to accept the regulatory depreciation proposed by
TasWater for the fourth regulatory period. The Economic Regulator also
noted in the 2022 Investigation Final Report that there was insufficient time
during the investigation to resolve the major issues attributed to regulatory
depreciation.
In response, the Economic Regulator conducted an inquiry, releasing its
Inquiry into the Calculation of Regulatory Depreciation Allowances for
TasWater’s New Assets, Final Report, November 2023 (the
Depreciation Inquiry).
The Economic Regulator found in its Depreciation Inquiry that TasWater’s
data for regulatory depreciation was inaccurate and inconsistent. The issues
identified included:
❑

TasWater received depreciation on certain assets that had reached
the end of their useful lives;

❑

failure to make necessary adjustments in calculating depreciation and
escalation for assets;

❑

significant differences in asset lives between similar assets; and

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

111

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

a reliance on manual input to calculate depreciation on a line-by-line
basis.

TasWater was required to account for the findings from the Depreciation
Inquiry in its proposed PSP5.

9.2 TasWater has proposed that a “line-in-the-sand” be
drawn for regulatory depreciation
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed a new approach to
regulatory depreciation. Key elements of its proposed approach include:
❑

calculating regulatory depreciation in TasWater’s revenue model,
rather than in an asset register on a line-by-line basis;

❑

grouping new assets into 15 smaller asset registers, with respective
asset lives and depreciation rates for each asset category;99

❑

disposing of $13.7 million in assets over the fifth regulatory period;

❑

assigning an asset life of 43.86 years for all existing assets;

❑

reclassifying 10 668 misclassified assets from the new asset to
existing asset regulated asset base; and

❑

calculating depreciation on new capex on an ‘as-incurred’ basis rather
than ‘as-commissioned’.

TasWater’s proposed approach results in a total regulatory depreciation
allowance of $559.8 million over the fifth regulatory period.

9.3 Asset categories and asset lives
All assets in TasWater’s regulated asset base are assigned an economic life,
which determines the level of regulatory depreciation. Asset lives are
therefore critical: setting them too short overstates depreciation and
increases prices for current customers, while setting them too long defers
cost recovery to future customers. In the 2022 Price Determination
Investigation and the Depreciation Inquiry, the Economic Regulator found
that TasWater’s asset lives were significantly shorter than those used in other
jurisdictions and were applied inconsistently and without adequate
justification. To address these issues, TasWater proposed moving from a
line-by-line approach to value-weighted average asset lives.

9.3.1 Asset lives for existing assets
TasWater proposed retaining a single asset life of 43.86 years for all assets
transferred prior to 1 July 2011, consistent with the fourth regulatory period,
99 The 15 asset categories correspond to TasWater’s 15 asset posting groups.

112

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

which would fully depreciate these assets by 2069-70.100 While the
Economic Regulator agreed in principle with applying a common asset life to
existing assets, it did not accept the proposed duration. After removing
disposals from the existing asset base, the Economic Regulator recalculated
an asset life of 56.04 years in the Draft Report (refer to section 6.2).
The Economic Regulator also considered TasWater’s move to fully
depreciate the existing asset base to be an improvement, noting that the
asset base has continued to grow over time. To ensure full depreciation, the
Economic Regulator adopted a ‘wind‑down’ approach to the existing asset
life, rather than recalculating the asset life each year. This would require
TasWater to decrease the asset life of existing assets by one year each year,
until the existing regulated asset base is fully depreciated.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater did not accept the Economic Regulator’s
proposed weighted average life for existing assets. TasWater stated there
was a conceptual inconsistency with the approach to calculating the existing
assets life, and that the original asset lives had been used.
TasWater also argued that calculating the asset life after the
“line-in-the-sand” has been drawn creates a risk of under-recovering its
capital investment. TasWater contends that any weighted average remaining
life for the existing asset regulated asset base should begin at TasWater’s
proposed “line-in-the-sand” of 1 July 2022.
TasWater also disagreed with the Economic Regulator’s ‘winding down’
approach to asset lives, arguing that the remaining life of existing assets
should instead begin from 1 July 2022. In addition, TasWater raised concerns
about how the revised asset lives were implemented in the revenue model,
noting that the flow-on impacts of these changes had not been adequately
considered.
Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator acknowledges an input was omitted in the
Draft Report’s calculation of TasWater’s existing asset life. This led to an
overstated weighted average asset life and understated depreciation. As
discussed in section 2.3 of this Final Report, the Economic Regulator also
recognises that the Draft Report asset life did not accurately reflect the
condition of TasWater’s existing asset base, which contains significant errors
and inconsistencies, including large‑scale asset reclassifications, removals,
and calculation errors.
The Economic Regulator identified multiple concerns with TasWater’s
existing regulated asset base, including insufficient supporting information, a
growing existing asset base despite ageing assets, low levels of disposals
relative to capital expenditure, continued returns on disposed or
100 TasWater’s Supplementary Information N, page 4.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

113

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

decommissioned assets, and widespread depreciation and data calculation
errors. Given these issues, the Economic Regulator accepts that it is not
possible to reliably calculate a weighted average remaining asset life that
reflects actual asset condition.
The Economic Regulator also notes the following material issues:

114

❑

Disposals from the existing regulated asset base have been minimal,
despite a growing capital expenditure program targeted at replacing
ageing infrastructure that was widely understood to be in poor
condition at the time of transfer. Under TasWater’s PSP5 proposal,
only 3 538 disposals from the existing regulated asset base were
identified over the period 2019 to 2030.

❑

In cases where existing assets have been disposed of or
decommissioned, the Economic Regulator has identified instances
where TasWater continued to earn a return on those assets in error
after they were no longer in service.

❑

The Economic Regulator identified widespread calculation and data
errors, including depreciation calculated using the values of unrelated
assets, depreciation continuing beyond the end of assets’ useful
lives, and internal inconsistencies such as asset descriptions that do
not align with recorded acquisition dates.

TasWater’s regulated asset base for its existing assets
Background
❑

The existing asset regulated asset base comprises legacy assets
transferred from councils prior to 1 July 2011.

❑

When the existing assets regulated asset base was established in the
second regulatory period, the Economic Regulator expected it to run
down over time, stating in the Final Report for the second price
determination investigation that “the [regulated asset base] will gradually
decline over time as depreciation and disposals reduce the value of
those assets.”

❑

Instead of declining, the regulated asset base has increased, from an
opening value of $2.3 billion in 2015-16 to a closing value of $2.7 billion
in 2025-26, despite these assets being legacy infrastructure.

❑

At the commencement of the existing regulated asset base, TasWater
had approximately 259 000 existing assets. At the Economic Regulator’s
“line-in-the-sand”, TasWater still had approximately 252 500 existing
assets.

Data quality and asset life concerns
❑

TasWater has confirmed that it does not hold the records used to
establish asset acquisition dates, asset lives and other key attributes.

❑

While the opening value of the existing assets is accepted, there are
unresolved concerns about the lives attributed to those assets.

❑

Over 99 per cent of existing assets have an effective acquisition date of
2009, which is inconsistent with many asset descriptions.

❑

As at the Economic Regulator’s “line-in-the sand” in 2025-26, around 76
per cent of existing assets still had remaining lives exceeding 21 years,
with no evidence to demonstrate these lives are representative of asset
age, condition or remaining service potential.

❑

The Economic Regulator identified a great many inconsistencies and
errors in the calculation and performance of existing assets.

Previous regulatory attempts and limitations
❑

The Economic Regulator sought to address these deficiencies in the
third regulatory period by proposing to move to weighted asset lives, to
improve simplicity and consistency. TasWater did not accept this move.

❑

The Economic Regulator could not open or verify the existing regulated
asset base during the PSP4 investigation.

Regulatory response
❑

Due to data limitations, time and resourcing constraints, not all issues
could be rectified during this Price Determination Investigation.

❑

The Economic Regulator considers the data required to improve the
existing asset information no longer exists and has therefore accepted
TasWater’s proposal to draw a “line-in-the-sand”.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

115

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Based on the above, the Economic Regulator acknowledges that it will not be
possible to identify and resolve all the known issues relating to TasWater’s
regulated asset base. As a result, the Economic Regulator accepts that
calculating a weighted average asset life for TasWater’s existing assets is
unlikely to reflect their actual remaining weighted average asset lives.
Accordingly, the Economic Regulator has identified four alternative
approaches for determining an appropriate remaining average asset life for
the existing assets that are reasonable, justifiable and broadly reflective of
the condition and age of the existing assets base.
These four options are:
1. accept TasWater’s proposed existing asset average life which it
submitted in response to the Draft Report of 41.11 years;
2. accept TasWater’s proposed existing asset average life of 43.86 years
which was submitted in its proposed PSP5 in June 2025;
3. roll-forward TasWater’s asset life at the end of the third regulatory
period to the end of the fourth regulatory period, for an asset life of
46.92 years; or
4. apply the asset life that was used in the last year of the third regulatory
period at the beginning of the fifth regulatory period, for an asset life of
51.92101.
The Economic Regulator has assessed these four options.
Option 1: TasWater’s proposed life of 41.11 years
❑

In its submission to the Draft Report, TasWater recalculated its existing
asset average life based on available data, with its adjustments to the
regulated asset base included.

❑

This calculation does not include all the Economic Regulator’s
adjustments made in the Draft Report. TasWater reasserts that it has
drawn its “line-in-the-sand” at the beginning of the fourth regulatory
period and has not incorporated any adjustments identified by the
Economic Regulator from 2022 onwards.

Option 2: TasWater’s proposed life of 43.86 years
❑

TasWater’s proposed PSP5 included an existing asset average life of
43.86 years, consistent with the average asset life applied in the fourth
regulatory period.

❑

This option aligns with the Economic Regulator’s “line-in-the-sand”,
which it has applied at the end of the fourth regulatory period.

101 The third regulatory period was extended by a year due to COVID-19. At the time of the third pricing

investigation, the final year of the third regulatory period was 2020-21.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

It is noted that the Economic Regulator was unable to verify the
underlying data in TasWater’s asset registers during the fourth

life of 43.86 years in the absence of better evidence.

Option 3: Rolling-forward the third regulatory period to 46.92 years
❑

This option rolls-forward the asset life from 2020-21102 to the
Economic Regulator’s “line-in-the-sand” of 2026-27.

❑

This option reflects the last time the Economic Regulator could
independently verify the existing assets average life calculation, during
the investigation for the third regulatory period. The
Economic Regulator determined an asset life of 51.92 years was
appropriate at 2020-21 based on the information available.

❑

The Economic Regulator notes a significant amount of time has
passed between the third and fifth regulatory period investigations,
and this approach would apply a retrospective view to TasWater’s
existing average asset life.

Option 4: Apply the asset life from 2020-21 of 51.92 years
❑

This option applies the average asset life approved for the end of the
third regulatory period103 of 51.92 years, without rolling the asset life
forward.

❑

This approach also acknowledges that the Economic Regulator was
unable to independently verify TasWater’s assets in the fourth
regulatory period or fully address the inconsistencies and errors that
have been identified during this investigation.

❑

This option would also apply a retrospective view to the existing asset
base.

The Economic Regulator recognises that there is no perfect solution for
setting the average asset life to apply to the existing regulated asset base,
with all of the solutions identified having positives and negatives.
However, on balance, the Economic Regulator has determined that adopting
TasWater’s average asset life from the end of the fourth regulatory period of
43.86 years is the most reasonable option. This option applies a more
contemporary view of the state of the assets in the regulated asset base,
while acknowledging that issues likely remain in relation to those assets. It
also aligns to the Economic Regulator’s approach to draw a “line-in-the-sand”
in relation to TasWater’s treatment of its assets at the end of the fourth
regulatory period.
Subject to the outcomes of the Economic Regulator’s review of TasWater’s
regulatory framework, it is not intended that the existing asset life will be

re-examined in future periods, to maintain the Economic Regulator’s

Decision 9.1: To apply an existing asset life of 43.86 years from the

To ensure consistency with other decisions, the Economic Regulator will
apply this asset life from the beginning of the fifth regulatory period. This
approach ensures internal consistency and that the NPV = 0 principle is
satisfied when the weighted average depreciation approach commences.
The Economic Regulator does not agree with TasWater’s view that ‘winding
down’ the remaining existing asset life is an incorrect approach. In the
absence of sufficient auditable information on the age and condition of the
existing asset base, the Economic Regulator considers that the existing asset
base must be progressively ‘wound down’ for regulatory purposes. However,
to provide regulatory certainty, the Economic Regulator will apply a
consistent asset life in the fifth regulatory period, rather than reducing the
asset life each year, as proposed in the Draft Report.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges the complexity that exists in
TasWater’s revenue model. However, following its assessment of the
proposed changes, the Economic Regulator has determined that
depreciation is applied on a straight-line basis and that the regulated asset
base will fully depreciate.
Decision 9.2: To require TasWater to apply a consistent existing
asset life for the duration of the fifth regulatory period.

metering renewals in categories with longer asset lives than typically applied,
and the treatment of vehicle assets, where heavy vehicles depreciate
differently and are replaced less frequently than policy assumptions suggest.

Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
In its submission, TasWater accepted the Economic Regulator’s approach for
new asset capex lives. However, TasWater did not accept the proposed
asset lives that were presented in the Draft Report. This was due to
TasWater rejecting the Economic Regulator’s treatment of disposals, and its
rationale that the “line-in-the-sand” starts from 1 July 2022.
TasWater recalculated its new asset lives after correcting for the regulated
asset base adjustments that it did accept.105
Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator acknowledges TasWater’s rationale that the new
asset lives should begin when the “line-in-the-sand” is implemented.
However, as part of its broader assessment of the regulated asset base and
regulatory depreciation, the Economic Regulator determined that the
appropriate “line-in-the-sand” is the end of the fourth regulatory period, rather
than the beginning.
Consistent with this approach, the Economic Regulator removed asset
disposals up to the end of the fourth regulatory period. However, it was not
possible to recalculate TasWater’s new asset posting group lives following
these disposals, as TasWater rolled forward its asset base from 2015 and did
not provide line-by-line capex data from 2016 to 2026. As a result, the
Economic Regulator was unable to calculate revised asset lives at the
beginning of its chosen “line-in-the-sand”.
In these circumstances, the Economic Regulator has accepted TasWater’s
proposed new asset lives as at the beginning of the fifth regulatory period,
with minor adjustments to remove third-party contributions included in
TasWater’s calculations. While the Economic Regulator acknowledges that
the approved asset lives may not fully reflect the final composition of the new
asset posting groups following disposals, it considers this outcome to be the
most appropriate regulatory approach given the available data.

9.4 Applying depreciation on an ‘as-incurred’ and
‘as-commissioned’ basis
In the 2018 Investigation Final Report, the Economic Regulator found
TasWater was receiving regulatory depreciation for a significant amount of
time before assets became operational. In response, the Economic Regulator
determined that for any new assets purchased or where the project
commenced after 30 June 2018, TasWater would only receive regulatory
depreciation once an asset has been commissioned, to incentivise
completion of capex projects.
In its PSP5 proposal, TasWater argued that an ‘as commissioned’ approach
was not administratively feasible given the changing scope of projects and
instead proposed calculating depreciation on an ‘as incurred’ basis.
TasWater also raised concerns that adopting an ‘as commissioned’ approach
would adversely affect its financial sustainability, noting that it had not applied
this approach in previous regulatory periods.
The Economic Regulator did not accept TasWater’s proposal, finding it
unsupported by evidence and considered project tracking to be
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

121

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

administratively feasible with minimal impact on financial sustainability. The
Economic Regulator also noted that while depreciation would be calculated
on an ‘as commissioned’ basis, TasWater would continue to receive a return
on capital on new capex as it is incurred.
Accordingly, the Draft Report required TasWater to calculate regulatory
depreciation on an ‘as commissioned’ basis for new assets. No issues were
raised during consultation, and the final decision is unchanged.
Decision 9.4: To require TasWater to calculate depreciation on
capex on an ‘as-commissioned’ basis for the fifth regulatory period.

9.4.1 TasWater’s proposed regulatory depreciation calculation
and allowance
TasWater proposed the following methodology for calculating regulatory
depreciation for existing and new assets.
For existing assets, TasWater proposed calculating regulatory depreciation
within its price and revenue model, rather than maintaining an asset register.
It also proposed splitting the existing assets regulated asset base into
separate water and sewer components, with assets previously classified as
‘other’ allocated between water and sewer using TasWater’s asset allocation
ratio. Under this approach, regulatory depreciation for existing assets would
be calculated by escalating the opening regulated asset base by inflation and
applying the relevant depreciation rate.
Since the third regulatory period, TasWater has calculated regulatory
depreciation for new assets using a line‑by‑line approach, under which
depreciation is calculated at the individual asset level. For the fifth regulatory
period, TasWater proposes to instead calculate depreciation for new assets
acquired or constructed after 1 July 2009 on two components:
❑

asset categories, calculated by escalating the opening regulated
asset base for each category by inflation and applying the relevant
depreciation rate; and

❑

net capex, comprising forecast new capital expenditure and
disposals, with depreciation calculated on the same basis as asset
categories and commencing from the year after the net capex is
incurred.

Based on this approach, TasWater proposal a total regulatory depreciation
allowance of $559.8 million for the fifth regulatory period (Table 9.3).

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

10

Inflationary gain

Inflationary gain is a revenue adjustment designed to prevent TasWater from
being compensated twice for inflation under the current regulatory
framework.
Under the current approach, the regulated asset base is indexed for inflation,
increasing asset values over time. At the same time, the rate of return is
calculated using a WACC that already incorporates forecast inflation. As a
result, inflation is reflected in both the indexed regulated asset base and in
the return on capital.
The inflationary gain adjustment removes this duplication, reducing the
notional allowable revenue so that TasWater is compensated for inflation
only once. Without this adjustment, any change in inflation would ultimately
have double the impact on revenue and subsequently, prices paid by
customers.
This approach was adopted during the investigation for the third regulatory
period, following TasWater’s proposal in its PSP3 that its pre-tax real WACC
(which had been used for the first two regulatory periods) be changed to a
post-tax nominal vanilla WACC. TasWater proposed this approach with the
intention of addressing inconsistencies in the second regulatory period
pricing investigation where the Economic Regulator approved:
❑

real pre-tax WACC and opex; and

❑

nominal capex and prices.

The inflationary gain adjustment is one of three main approaches to account
for inflation in nominal revenue and is widely used in Australia, including by
the AER, Queensland Competition Authority and the Independent
Competition and Regulatory Commission. The alternative approaches are
discussed later in this Chapter.
The inflationary gain adjustment for the fifth regulatory period is
$657.5 million.

10.1 Inflationary gain and depreciation
The AER uses the same approach to account for inflation, but the AER
makes the inflationary gain adjustment through the depreciation component
of its decision.
While this treatment can give the impression that the inflationary gain
adjustment is to offset regulatory depreciation, that is not the case. The link
between the inflationary gain adjustment and depreciation is only through the
indexed regulated asset base.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

125

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The inflationary gain adjustment is calculated from the opening value of the
regulated asset base, with depreciation calculated on the regulated asset
base after it has been escalated by the inflation rate.
By way of example, when inflation increases in Year One:
❑

the amount of depreciation allowance for Year One is higher; and

❑

the opening regulated asset base value for Year Two is increased by
higher inflation, but reduced slightly by higher depreciation.

The interaction between inflationary gain and depreciation is small because
both effects are already reflected in the indexation of the regulated asset
base, through the inflation rate and the depreciation rate respectively.

10.2 Alternative approaches
There are two main alternative approaches to accounting for inflation in a
nominal revenue framework. These are:
❑

Real rate of return approach – this approach combines a real WACC
with an indexed regulated asset base and inflation is only accounted
for once through the indexation of the regulated asset base.

❑

Nominal rate of return approach – this approach combines a nominal
WACC and an unindexed regulated asset base and inflation is only
accounted for once through the rate of return.

Under these two approaches, no inflationary gain adjustment is required to
the notional allowable revenue.
Figure 10.1 shows the difference in revenue paths over 50 years under the
three different approaches, using an example entity with a regulated asset
base opening value of $100 million. The example assumes a constant
inflation rate of three per cent, a depreciation rate of five per cent and a
nominal WACC of eight per cent.

126

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
Figure 10.1: Comparison of revenue paths for an example entity using different WACC
approaches

The revenue paths produced under the Economic Regulator’s approach of
using a nominal WACC with an indexed regulated asset base and the real
rate of return approach are identical, holding all else constant.
The nominal rate of return approach with nominal a WACC and an unindexed
regulated asset base produces a relatively steeper revenue path - this is
because the value of the unindexed regulated asset base will depreciate
much faster than the indexed regulated asset base. This means that
revenues and prices will increase more in the short to medium-term
compared to the other approaches, while in the long-term, it will result in
lower prices.
Despite the difference in revenue paths, the net present values of these
revenue paths are identical over the long-term, with all approaches returning
the initial regulated asset base value.

10.3 TasWater’s submission to the Economic Regulator’s
Draft Report
TasWater’s submission to the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report notes that
a major driver of its financeability problem is the treatment of inflation under
the regulatory framework, particularly the outcomes that it can produce in the
short-term. TasWater’s submission states that “While the Draft Report
outcome may be theoretically net present value neutral over the life of the
asset base, the low depreciation allowance coupled with a relatively large
deduction for forecast inflationary gain, in the current high inflation

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

127

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

environment, gives rise to a significant cash flow problem for the business
that manifests as weak financeability.”106
The Economic Regulator notes that the approach to be used to account for
inflation will be considered as part of the framework review. Refer to
Chapter 24 for further information regarding this review.

Introduction to prices, fees and charges

As discussed in the Introduction, TasWater’s total proposed expenditure over
the fifth regulatory period equates to a price increase of 11.5 per cent per
annum. TasWater has proposed that a price increase of 8.8 per cent be
applied over the period, with some of its revenue recovery deferred to the
sixth regulatory period. The Economic Regulator’s decisions in the
Draft Report resulted in a price increase of 4.3 per cent per annum.
The Economic Regulator has received stakeholder feedback indicating the
hardship that may be felt by many in the face of a price increase.
“The Salvation Army’s experience is that many of the community members
we already support have had to make hard choices about consumption. Even
the proposed 4.3 per cent increase will continue to push important sanitary
practices out of reach, or further cause Tasmanians to choose between
necessities such as food or utilities.”107

This section covers the full range of regulated prices, fees and charges that
TasWater may apply to its customers, as assessed and determined by the
Economic Regulator, including prices for:
❑

water services;

❑

sewerage services;

❑

trade waste services;

❑

developer services; and

❑

miscellaneous services, including charges for network connections
and other service-related fees.

TasWater’s pricing must follow the pricing principles in the Industry Act and
Pricing Regulations. These require prices to recover efficient service costs,
separate fixed and variable charges, reflect the cost of serving different
customers or regions, and provide effective incentives for efficiency and
productivity. Prices must also allow a fair return on assets and, where
reasonable, match the direct cost of providing services to customers.
TasWater proposed to adopt the term ‘access charge’ in place of
‘fixed charge’, and ‘unconnected property access charges’ in place of ‘service
charges’ to enhance clarity for customers. TasWater did not demonstrate that
it had consulted with its customers on the updated terminology prior to
proposing it.

107 Submission from The Salvation Army, page 2.

130

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator accepted this naming convention for the Draft
Report.
TasCOSS provided feedback which indicated that TasWater’s proposed
changes may not improve clarity for customers. Instead, TasCOSS
recommended that TasWater and the Economic Regulator consider adopting
the same, commonly understood terminology that customers are already
familiar with in the energy sector, or retain the existing fixed/variable charges
terminology.
“TasWater and other utilities can improve customer engagement and
comprehension through simple communication. This includes simplification of
language and consistent terminology.” 108

The Economic Regulator recognises the importance of clear and simple
terminology, which is easily understood by customers. For this reason, the
Economic Regulator has reviewed the language that has been used by
customers and stakeholders in their submissions and notes that it generally
aligns with the recommendation by TasCOSS.
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s proposed changes to
the terminology for fixed charges for the fifth regulatory period.
Decision 11.1: To require TasWater to use the naming conventions
of fixed charges, variable charges and unconnected property
service charge, from 1 July 2026 and update its billing system
accordingly.

108 Submission from the Tasmanian Council of Social Service Inc., page 6.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

12

Prices for water services

TasWater’s water bills are structured around two main components: a fixed
charge and a variable charge. The fixed charge is an annual fee that covers
the cost of maintaining water infrastructure and ensuring service availability,
while the variable component is applied to the volume of water supplied to a
customer.
TasWater proposed to increase the variable water charge while reducing the
fixed charge, thereby placing greater emphasis on consumption in
determining customer bills. The proposal aimed to provide customers who
are able to reduce usage with greater control over their bills and to
encourage water conservation. TasWater based the proposal on long‑run
marginal cost estimates for two of its 59 drinking water systems, which
ranged from $1.31 to $1.98 per kL (in 2024-25 dollars). On this basis,
TasWater proposed increasing the variable water charge to $1.81 per kL in
2026-27, representing a 44 per cent increase on the current charge of
$1.26 per kL.
Approaches for calculating the cost of delivering a service:
Short-run marginal cost is the additional cost of supplying one more unit of a service
when some inputs are fixed and cannot be adjusted immediately. It reflects the cost of
meeting extra demand using existing capacity and resources. To fully recover costs for a
service with high fixed costs, a fixed charge is required.
Long-run marginal cost is the extra cost of supplying one more unit of a service when all
inputs can be adjusted over time. It reflects the cost of expanding capacity to meet future
demand, including infrastructure, operations, and maintenance.

Water customers are charged based on the level of service they receive.
Most TasWater customers receive full service, meaning drinking water that
meets quality and pressure standards.
Customers with limited service pay reduced charges, which apply to:
❑

limited water quality: where the water received is not of drinking
water quality; and

❑

limited water supply: where the service is limited due to pressure
and/or flow issues.

TasWater proposed maintaining reduced prices for customers receiving a
limited service, with a 10 per cent reduction on the fixed water charge for
supply (Table 12.3) and/or a 20 per cent reduction on the variable water
charge for quality (Table 12.1). The proposed fixed charge reduction reflects
reduced service, while the variable charge reduction covers lower treatment
costs and the inconvenience of boiling water.

12.1 Economic Regulator’s considerations
12.1.1 Variable water charge
The Economic Regulator acknowledged that while customers value the ability
to manage bills by reducing water consumption, demand for water is
generally price inelastic due to its essential nature and limited substitutes. As
a result, some customers have limited capacity to materially reduce usage.
The Economic Regulator agreed to setting the variable water charge based
on long‑run marginal cost, which aligns with the National Water Initiative
Pricing Principles and practices in other jurisdictions. However, the Economic
Regulator considered that TasWater’s long‑run marginal cost estimates
(derived solely from the Greater Hobart and Greater Launceston systems)
were unlikely to be representative of statewide costs, given Tasmania’s
diverse geography and system characteristics. Given the limited information
available, the Economic Regulator’s draft decision was to adopt the lowest
end of TasWater’s long‑run marginal cost estimates, resulting in a draft
maximum variable water charge of $1.41 per kL for full service customers in
2026-27.
Issues raised during consultation on the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission supports the Economic Regulator’s draft decision to
set the variable water charge at $1.41 per kL for 2026-27 but proposes to
increase the variable water charge at a faster rate over the regulatory period,
to achieve its proposed charge of $2.33 per kL in 2029-30. TasWater argues
that this higher charge is justified by its long-run marginal cost estimates for
its three largest drinking water systems (Greater Hobart, Greater Launceston
and Mersey Central Coast) which together account for 83 per cent of total
water demand in Tasmania.
The Treasurer supports reforms that encourage more efficient price
structures, while carefully managing customer impacts and welcomed the
Economic Regulator’s draft decision to approve a transitional approach to
TasWater’s proposed tariff reforms.
TasCOSS submits that water pricing structures with a high proportion of fixed
charges weaken incentives for efficient water use and can lead to poorer
social, economic and environmental outcomes. It supports a shift toward
more usage-based charging to give customers greater control over their bills,
encourage sustainable water use, and ensure that high-volume discretionary
consumption is not subsidised by other customers. TasCOSS also notes
evidence that price responsiveness varies by income, with low-income
households more likely to respond to price signals over time, and considers
that usage-based pricing is consistent with customer preferences identified
through TasWater’s Deliberative Panel.

134

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Sorell Council supports increasing the variable component of water bills, but
recommends consideration of impacts on households in growth areas or with
constrained incomes.
Feedback was also received from individual residential customers indicating
strong support for increasing the variable component of water charges and
decreasing the fixed charge, to provide greater control over their bills and
stronger incentives to reduce water consumption.
Economic Regulator’s decision
The Industry Act requires that the variable charge for water services is to
reflect the costs that are reasonably attributable to the volume of water
delivered to the property to which the charge relates. The rate of variable
charge may only be greater than the cost of delivering water for a limited
number of prescribed purposes, such as constraints on the amount of water
available or constraints to the capacity of TasWater’s infrastructure.
While the Economic Regulator has concerns about the veracity of
TasWater’s long-run marginal cost estimates. TasWater has not explained or
justified its underlying assumptions used nor demonstrated that the long-run
marginal cost estimates are appropriate for regulatory purposes. It is unclear
whether the modelling adequately accounts for other water uses, such as
leakage, non‑revenue water, or water supplied for irrigation. Further,
TasWater has not provided an explanation for the significant discrepancy in
outcomes between the different methods used to calculate the long-run
marginal cost results for Launceston.
Under section 15 of the Industry Act, the Economic Regulator must have
regard to the impact of the rate of change of prices for customers. On this
basis, and considering stakeholder feedback, a more moderate price path
has been developed that delivers an increased variable pricing component
over time.
Decision 12.1: To set the maximum variable water charge at
$1.41 per kL for 2026-27.
The Economic Regulator has decided to retain its position to support
TasWater’s proposal to maintain a 20 per cent reduction on the variable
water charge for limited quality customers, on the basis that it reflects the
lower treatment costs TasWater incurs providing this service. No
submissions were received in relation to limited quality customers.
Decision 12.2: To set the maximum variable water charge for limited
quality customers at 20 per cent below the charge for full service
customers. The variable water charge for limited quality in 2026-27
is $1.13 per kL.

12.1.2 Fixed water charge
To establish its fixed water charges, TasWater has calculated its total
required water revenue and then subtracted the amount expected to be
recovered from the proposed variable water charge. TasWater has proposed
that the remaining revenue be recovered through reduced fixed water
charges.
The pricing principles require that both the variable water charge and the
fixed water charge reflect the actual costs incurred in delivering water
services. In particular, regulation 14 of the Pricing Regulations requires that
the fixed charge is to reflect the costs borne by TasWater in providing water
services to a particular property.
TasWater has not provided sufficient evidence that its proposed fixed water
charges reflect its fixed costs.
The Economic Regulator considers that the approach taken by TasWater,
and subsequently its proposed fixed water charge, do not necessarily meet
the requirements of the pricing principles.
In the absence of data to support the proposed fixed water charge, the
Economic Regulator has relied on data from TasWater’s 2024-25 regulatory
report to estimate an appropriate fixed charge. This data shows regulated
water and sewerage costs separately and splits the various costs into direct
and indirect.
Decision 12.3: To set the maximum fixed water charge at
$412.92 per 20 mm connection in 2026-27.
The Economic Regulator considers TasWater’s proposal to continue to base
fixed water charges on connection sizes for connected properties is
appropriate, as it reflects potential water demand on TasWater’s
infrastructure and aligns with the pricing principles contained in the Pricing
Regulations.
To align with the Economic Regulator’s requirements for applying the
sewerage fixed charge, the fixed water charge is set using the water meter
connection point and applying the connection multiplier. Where infrastructure
is shared, charges must be divided equally amongst the property owners,
unless stated otherwise in the Sub-metering Policy.

12.1.3 Further work is required for the sixth regulatory period to
understand the cost of delivering water services
Throughout this investigation, TasWater has only been able to provide limited
information and data on its fixed and variable costs of supplying drinking
water across its 59 drinking water systems.
To undertake further rebalancing of its water tariffs in future regulatory
periods, TasWater will need to undertake further analysis and provide more
evidence of its costs of supplying water to its regulated customers.

12.2 Fire service charge
Commercial and industrial customers often have dedicated water service
connections for fire protection purposes. These connections are typically
unmetered and used only in emergencies, such as during a fire event.
Despite their infrequent use, TasWater must maintain sufficient network
capacity to ensure these connections can deliver the required flow and
pressure instantly when needed. This involves ongoing investment in
infrastructure, including larger mains, pumps and storage, to meet potential
peak demand for fire services.
TasWater proposed to maintain the fire service charge at 25 per cent of the
full service fixed water charge, reflecting the infrequent use of this service.
The Economic Regulator accepts TasWater’s proposal and agrees with the
reduced fixed water charge for fire services, acknowledging their rare use
and lower network demand compared to standard connections.
Decision 12.5: To approve the fire service charge at 25 per cent of
the full service fixed water charge. The fire service charge per
20 mm connection in 2026-27 is $103.23.

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

13

Prices for sewerage services

TasWater currently levies sewerage charges using an equivalent
tenements framework, which estimates the relative demand properties place
on the sewerage network compared to an average residential dwelling under
normal dry weather conditions. A typical single residential dwelling is
assigned one equivalent tenement, while non‑residential customers are
allocated multiple equivalent tenements based on assessed demand, with
charges applied on a per‑tenement basis.
Following stakeholder feedback during the 2022 Price Determination
Investigation, the Economic Regulator conducted an Inquiry into Sewerage
Charging and released its Final Report on 22 August 2024. The Report
required TasWater to transition to a two‑part tariff structure comprising fixed
and variable charges for all regulated sewerage customers from the
commencement of the fifth regulatory period. Under this approach, regulated
customers would no longer be charged solely on the basis of equivalent
tenements. Instead, sewerage charges will consist of a fixed charge and a
usage‑based variable charge, calculated by applying a sewerage discharge
factor to metered water consumption and charging a set price per kilolitre of
estimated discharge.
Inquiry into Sewerage Charging Final Report (22 August 2024)109
The Economic Regulator has decided to require TasWater to:
❑

introduce, for the fifth regulatory period, a fixed and variable charge approach for
all regulated sewerage customers (including Category 3 and 4 trade waste
customers);

❑

introduce a single set of fixed and variable charges for all regulated sewerage
customers; and

❑

undertake further analysis and consultation on its proposed discharge factors for
non-residential customers including Category 3 and 4 trade waste customers.

The Economic Regulator also noted that:
❑

subject to TasWater’s further analysis and consultation on its proposed
discharge factors, in the event that Category 3 and 4 trade waste customers face
material changes in their bills due to the implementation of a fixed and variable
charging approach, the Economic Regulator will consider whether a price
transition may be required;

❑

TasWater may want to assess other ways, such as providing information and
advice on water saving measures, to assist customers who receive a
concession; and

❑

consideration will be given to reviewing the provisions that relate to payment
difficulties, payment plans and financial hardship policies in the Customer
Service Code to ensure that sufficient protection is offered to customers who
receive a concession.

109 Inquiry into Sewerage Charging Approach Final Report, 22 August 2024, page 11.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

In the Inquiry into Sewerage Charging Final Report, the Economic Regulator
noted that the majority of Category 3 and 4 trade waste customers were likely
to be worse off under the changed arrangements. This is because they tend
to be large water users, so their bills would increase substantially under the
new model.110 In response, the Economic Regulator required TasWater to
undertake further analysis and consultation on proposed sewerage discharge
factors for non-residential and trade waste customers.
TasWater has advised the Economic Regulator that it did not engage with
these customers specifically on its proposed move to fixed and variable
sewerage charges.111

13.1 TasWater’s proposal for the fifth regulatory period
TasWater proposed a fixed and variable charging approach for sewerage
services, with the key aspects of the proposal being:
❑

variable sewerage charge: a usage charge (Table 13.1) applied to
the estimated volume of sewage that a customer discharges to the
sewer network.;

❑

sewerage discharge factors: a multiplier to establish the amount of
sewage discharged by a customer. The estimated volume is
calculated by multiplying the customer’s metered water volume by a
sewerage discharge factor (Table 13.3);

❑

fixed sewerage charge: based on the size of the customer’s water
connection (Table 13.2);

❑

residential sewerage discharge cap: estimated sewerage discharge
capped at 240 kL per year for residential customers to mitigate the bill
impact on large families; and

❑

review of discharge factors: a user-pays review mechanism for
non-residential customers to apply to TasWater for a review of their
sewerage discharge factors.

13.2 Economic Regulator’s sewerage charging approach
for the Draft Report
The Economic Regulator recognised that there is customer support,
particularly among non-residential customers, to move away from a
sewerage charging approach based on equivalent tenements. However, the
Economic Regulator identified several concerns that indicate that TasWater
has not fully considered the impact of its proposal on customers.
On that basis, the Economic Regulator made the draft decision to introduce a
transitional approach to fixed and variable sewerage charging, with an aim of
minimising negative customer impacts while TasWater undertakes further
work over the next four years to:
❑

verify the appropriateness of its business group categories and refine
them where necessary;

❑

verify the accuracy of its sewerage discharge factors;

❑

establish the fixed and variable costs associated with delivering
sewerage services across Tasmania; and

❑

reconcile its sewerage charging approach with its trade waste
charging approach to ensure there is no double-dipping across the
regulated services.

The Economic Regulator’s draft sewerage charging approach included:

142

❑

A lower variable charge of $0.11 per kL in 2026-27 to reflect the
requirement of the Pricing Regulations that sewerage charges must
reflect only the costs reasonably attributable to providing sewerage
services. The Economic Regulator relied on TasWater’s sewerage cost
analysis to develop this price but acknowledged that other variable
sewerage costs may exist.

❑

Accepting TasWater’s proposal to base the fixed sewerage charge for
connected properties on the size of a customer’s water connection, on
the basis that a larger water connection size is an appropriate proxy
for a larger potential load on the sewerage system.

❑

A sewerage fixed charge of $796.87 for 2026-27.

❑

Introducing a 40-percentage point discount on sewerage discharge
factors for trade waste customers to minimise the risk of
double-dipping between the new volumetric sewerage charging
approach and the trade waste charging arrangements.

❑

Extending TasWater’s proposed sewerage discharge review
mechanism to require it to apply to all customers, at no charge. This
draft decision also required TasWater to ensure that the sewerage
discharge factor review mechanism does not contravene any
provisions of the Customer Service Code.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Furthermore, the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report did not accept
TasWater’s residential sewerage discharge cap, on the basis that it could
result in an ongoing cross-subsidy between customers.
The Economic Regulator arrived at the draft transitional sewerage charging
arrangements to address concerns that indicated that TasWater had not fully
considered the impact of its proposal on customers. These concerns
included:
❑

lack of cost-reflectivity in variable charges;

❑

limited consultation with non-residential customers;

❑

lack of verification of TasWater’s proposed sewerage discharge factors
and business group categories; and

❑

that customers would bear the costs under TasWater’s proposed
sewerage discharge factor review mechanism.

13.3 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
Stakeholders provided a wide range of feedback on the sewerage charging
approach outlined in the Draft Report. While views differed on specific
matters, submissions consistently raised concerns about fairness, whether
charges reflect actual use, affordability, and how the new charges would
work in practice for different customers.

13.3.1 Feedback on how the new sewerage charges will be
applied
Many stakeholders indicated that the proposed sewerage charges are
difficult to understand. In particular, feedback indicated that there is confusion
about how fixed and usage‑based charges would operate together; the
purpose of sewerage discharge factors; and whether fixed charges would
apply per water connection or per sewerage connection. These issues were
more pronounced for properties with multiple connections.
Feedback noted the limited consultation with non-residential customers,
which has contributed to the confusion. Hospitality Tasmania stressed the
need for clear communication before the new charges begin so that
customers understand what to expect. Latrobe Caravan Park advised that
businesses must be able to reliably estimate their future bills so they can set
their own prices with confidence.

13.3.2 Feedback on the proposed sewerage fixed charge
TasWater has proposed applying different fixed sewerage charging
approaches for residential and non-residential customers. Whilst the majority
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

143

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

of residential premises are connected by a 20 mm water connection, this may
not be the case for properties with multiple occupancy, such as apartments.
TasWater proposed charging the equivalent of a 20 mm water connection for
each occupancy, irrespective of the actual meter size at the property.
TasWater submits that there may be some cases where there is a mismatch
between the size of a customer’s connection and the load they place on the
sewer system, with these customers often having a large connection size but
a low discharge factor. On this basis, TasWater has proposed that
non-residential customers with a sewerage discharge factor of 0.1 or less be
charged as if they had a 20 mm connection size rather than their actual water
connection size.
TasWater further stated that its broader tariff reforms are intended to
encourage customers to choose water connection sizes that align more
closely with their actual use. It noted that non‑residential customers could
reduce charges by resizing their connection, where technical standards
allow.
King Island Council and Waratah Wynyard Council raised concerns that
facilities such as sporting grounds, parks and recreational areas often require
large water connections but generate little sewerage. They considered this
could result in unfairly high fixed charges.
Fonterra raised similar concerns, noting that water connection size is not
representative of the sewerage connection at industrial sites. It suggested
that it would be more appropriate to apply the sewerage fixed charge based
on the size of the sewerage outflow pipe.
TasWater reiterated its preference to maintain its initial PSP5 proposal,
where each residential customer would be subject to a minimum sewerage
access charge equal to a 20 mm connection charge, regardless of
connection size. TasWater believes that this will provide equitable outcomes
for all residential customers.

13.3.3 Feedback on the proposed sewerage variable charge
TasWater submitted that the sewerage variable charge proposed in the Draft
Report is below the cost of providing the service. It proposed increasing the
variable charge to $0.54 per kilolitre in 2026-27. This price is based on its
estimated short-run marginal cost, incorporating all sewerage variable costs
into the calculation.
Several submissions questioned the introduction of a usage‑based sewerage
charge for residential customers. The Treasurer’s submission highlighted
increased bill complexity and limited consultation supporting the change.
However, other submissions noted that usage‑based charging may give
customers more control over their bills.

144

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

13.3.4 Feedback on proposed sewerage discharge factors
TasWater proposed refinements to sewerage discharge factor categories, to
better reflect different types of businesses. New categories were included for
caravan parks and mixed‑use businesses, and lower discharge factors were
proposed for sporting facilities. A complete list of TasWater’s updated
sewerage discharge factors is shown in Table 13.3.
TasWater stated that undertaking discharge assessments for every customer
category would be costly and unlikely to significantly improve accuracy
compared to the benchmarked discharge factors proposed. For industrial
customers that generate trade waste, TasWater noted that flow‑monitoring
equipment is already in place and could be used to measure actual sewerage
volumes.
Many stakeholders, including both residential and non-residential customers,
questioned whether the proposed discharge factors accurately reflect actual
use of the sewerage system.
Residential customers
David Myer submitted that neither the Draft Report nor the Inquiry into
Sewerage Charging Final Report demonstrates that the proposed residential
sewerage discharge factor accurately reflects actual residential sewage
volumes. His submission also outlines sewerage charging practices adopted
in other Australian jurisdictions.
Ray Wright supported the move to two-part sewerage pricing but raised
concerns that the assumed residential discharge factor is too high and
insufficiently justified for Tasmanian conditions. He also noted that
TasWater’s sewerage design standards indicate discharge rates of around
82 per cent of the design water allowance for residential customers.
Non-residential customers
The Local Government Association Tasmania supported the revised
sewerage charging approach, noting that the proposed discharge
methodology is reasonable and commonly used where direct measurement
is not available. However, LGAT’s submission highlighted concerns about
implementation and equity, particularly for council owned facilities such as
sporting fields and cemeteries that have high water use but low sewage
discharge. LGAT emphasised the need for clearer discharge categorisation
and refinement to ensure charges are cost reflective and to avoid
disproportionate impacts.
King Island Council expressed concern about the proposed sewer discharge
assumptions applied to sporting facilities, as it is widely recognised across
Australian jurisdictions that only a small proportion of water used at sporting
grounds is discharged to the sewer.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

145

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Simplot and Fonterra submitted there was a failure to adequately categorise
industrial customers, and that it was not clear which sewerage discharge
factor applied to their businesses. Simplot stated that at its two facilities, at
least 95 per cent of incoming water is discharged as trade waste, not as
sewage. Therefore, even with the trade waste 40-percentage point discount
to the sewerage discharge factor, there will still be a significant impact.
Nyrstar’s submits that while TasWater has indicated it does not expect the
sewerage discharge factor of 0.9 to apply to its business, this position has
not been formally confirmed.

13.3.5 Feedback on the proposed residential sewerage discharge
cap
Ray Wright supports the Economic Regulator’s draft decision to reject
TasWater’s proposed cap of 240 kL per year on residential sewerage
discharge volumes, as the cap does not satisfy user pays principles and does
not treat all residential sewerage customers equitably.

13.3.6 Feedback on the proposed sewerage discharge factor
review mechanism
TasWater opposed funding a sewerage discharge factor review mechanism
for all regulated customers. It stated that such a mechanism would impose
additional administrative costs and create uncertainty, particularly if there
were no limits on the frequency or scope of reviews. Further, the residential
sewerage discharge cap was the proposed approach to mitigate any
potential impacts for residential customers.
Nyrstar’s submission raises concerns about reliance on the sewerage
discharge factor review mechanism to ensure the accuracy of sewerage
pricing, and the high level of uncertainty which that could create for some
non-residential customers.
