2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

13.4 Economic Regulator’s decisions
13.4.1 Fixed sewerage charge
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s proposal to apply
different fixed sewerage charging approaches for residential and
non-residential customers. While the Economic Regulator recognises that
sewerage network loads may differ across customer types, both residential
and non-residential customers remain within the same regulated customer
class for sewerage services. In these circumstances, applying different fixed
charging methodologies would be inconsistent with principles of tariff
consistency, transparency, and non-discrimination. A uniform fixed charging
approach within a customer class provides greater pricing stability, reduces
complexity, and ensures that customers with comparable access to the
sewerage network are treated equitably.
The Economic Regulator also considered TasWater’s proposal that
non-residential customers with a sewerage discharge factor of 0.1 or less be
charged based on a 20 mm connection size, rather than their actual water
connection size. While the Economic Regulator understands the intent of this
proposal - namely, to better align fixed sewerage charges with expected
sewerage network loads - it is not satisfied that the proposed threshold would
operate equitably in practice. In particular, the proposal is expected to
primarily capture sports fields, sporting grounds and reserves, which typically
have low sewerage discharges relative to their water use. However, other
non-residential customers that require large water connection sizes for
pressure or operational reasons, but which also impose low sewerage loads
on the network, would not be captured. This creates the risk of inconsistent
outcomes and weakens the cost-reflectivity rationale underpinning the
proposal.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges TasWater’s concerns that inequities
may arise where residential apartment buildings are serviced by water
connections of different sizes, particularly where those buildings contain a
similar number of dwellings. However, the Economic Regulator considers
that TasWater has not demonstrated that such outcomes represent a
material or systematic inequity under the existing charging framework. In
particular, TasWater’s example does not sufficiently establish that customers
in comparable circumstances are treated inconsistently, nor does it quantify
the extent of any alleged inequity.
In the specific example provided by TasWater, an apartment building
containing 10 residential units serviced by a single 32 mm water connection
would be charged as if each unit had a 20 mm connection. The Economic
Regulator notes that applying this approach would result in TasWater
recovering a higher amount of fixed sewerage revenue overall than would
otherwise be recovered under a strictly connection-based methodology. This
outcome would be inconsistent with the intent of fixed charges as a
mechanism for recovering shared network costs on a neutral and
148

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

proportionate basis. Moreover, the example does not demonstrate that the
current approach leads to under-recovery or inappropriate
cross-subsidisation, nor does it justify departing from a consistent charging
framework across customers within the same class.
Therefore, the Economic Regulator has decided to apply fixed sewerage
charges using the water meter connection point and the applicable
connection multiplier. Where infrastructure is shared, charges must be
divided equally amongst the property owners, unless explicitly stated
otherwise in the Sub-Metering Policy.
Decision 13.1: To require TasWater to apply the sewerage fixed
charge based on the water meter connection size, as measured at
the water meter connection point.
The Economic Regulator has decided to set the maximum fixed sewerage
charge at $795.46 per 20 mm connection in 2026-27.
Decision 13.2: To set the maximum fixed sewerage charge at
$795.46 per 20 mm connection in 2026-27.

TasWater’s submission notes that non‑residential customers may apply to

standards, and payment of additional fees. TasWater considers this option
provides flexibility and prevents customers from being locked into historic

Decision 13.3: To require TasWater to report annually on the
number of requests received for resizing of water connections.

13.4.2 Variable sewerage charge
The Economic Regulator has reviewed the information provided by TasWater
to estimate its short-run marginal cost of treating sewage at $0.54. This
information demonstrates the average variable costs for treating sewage,
which is different from the short-run marginal cost. The short-run marginal
cost should, by definition, represent the incremental cost of treating one
additional unit of wastewater, given existing system capacity. Electricity for
pumping and aeration, and treatment chemicals are the primary short-run
costs. Costs such as labour and maintenance are generally fixed in the
short-run, because the requirements of operations, maintenance and
compliance are continuous, regardless of short-term fluctuations in sewage
volumes.
Given these characteristics, the Economic Regulator considers that
TasWater’s estimated cost of $0.54 per kL likely overstates the short-run
marginal cost of sewerage services. In the absence of sufficiently granular
cost data to isolate the truly incremental costs associated with additional
wastewater volumes, the Economic Regulator has exercised regulatory
judgement informed by economic principles, stakeholder submissions and
the need to set a reasonable and proportionate variable charge.
Therefore, the Economic Regulator has set the variable sewerage charge for
2026-27 at $0.22 per kL.
Decision 13.4: To set the maximum variable sewerage charge at
$0.22 per kL in 2026-27.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

13.4.3 Sewerage discharge factors
The Economic Regulator has accepted TasWater’s revised sewerage
discharge factor categories, as they better reflect expected discharge to the
sewerage system.
Decision 13.5: To set the maximum sewerage discharge factors as
shown in Table 13.3.
Noting the concerns raised by stakeholders about the potential for double
charging between trade waste and sewerage services, the Economic
Regulator will retain the 40-percentage point discount for customers who pay
trade waste charges (Category 1, 2A, 2B, 2C, 3 and 4) to reduce the risk of
double charging. This applies to both regulated and unregulated trade waste
customers, except for those which have flow meters capable of measuring
sewage discharges. In limited cases where a revised discharge factor would
result in a negative value, a discharge factor of zero may be applied. In these
instances, costs are expected to be recovered through the fixed sewerage
charge.
Decision 13.6: To apply a 40-percentage point discount to sewerage
discharge factors for both regulated and unregulated trade waste
customers, except for Category 0 trade waste customers, to
minimise the risk of double-dipping.
During consultation the Economic Regulator received feedback from
industrial customers with sewerage flow-monitoring that this data would be
more appropriate than applying sewerage discharge factors to metered
water. TasWater also noted the availability of this data.
Therefore, where industrial customers have appropriate monitoring systems
that separate the sewage volume from the trade waste volume, the Economic
Regulator requires the actual measured sewerage volume be used as the
basis for determining variable sewerage charges, rather than metered water
and sewerage discharge factors.
Decision 13.7: To use sewage only flow metering data, where it
exists, to apply the sewerage variable charge.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

151

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

13.4.4 Residential sewerage discharge cap
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s proposed 240 kilolitre
residential sewerage discharge cap. The proposal was not adequately
justified and would likely result in cross subsidisation and ongoing inequities
between customers.
Decision 13.8: To not apply a residential sewerage discharge cap.

13.4.5 Sewerage discharge factor review mechanism
The Economic Regulator considers that all regulated customers should have
fair access to the sewerage discharge factor review mechanism. Providing
access to such a mechanism supports fairness and cost reflectivity in tariff
outcomes. However, the Economic Regulator acknowledges TasWater’s
concerns about the administrative burden and costs associated with
managing a high volume of reviews.
Accordingly, the Economic Regulator supports the use of clearly defined
thresholds or materiality tests to ensure that the review mechanism is
targeted to cases where adjustments are likely to be meaningful, while
maintaining administrative efficiency and avoiding unnecessary regulatory
burden.
TasWater is required to fully develop and scope the review mechanism and
ensure that it does not contravene any provisions of the Customer Service
Code. TasWater must submit its review mechanism to the Economic
Regulator as part of its final PSP5.
To ensure that customers are fully informed of the existence of the sewerage
discharge review mechanism and their ability to dispute charges, TasWater is
required to refer to its complaints and dispute policy and its sewerage
discharge review mechanism on customer bills.

Decision 13.9: To require TasWater to develop a sewerage
discharge factor review mechanism:

152

❑

that applies to all regulated sewerage customers, at no cost
to the customer;

❑

submit its review mechanism to the Economic Regulator as
part of its final PSP5; and

❑

refer to its sewerage discharge review mechanism and its
complaints and disputes policy on customer bills.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Where a sewerage discharge factor is found to be incorrect, the Economic
Regulator requires TasWater to amend the factor and adjust billing in
accordance with the Customer Service Code.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges stakeholder concerns related to the
residential sewerage discharge factor. Therefore, TasWater is required to
review the residential sewerage discharge factor before the next regulatory
period.
Decision 13.10: To require TasWater to review the residential
sewerage discharge factor prior to the sixth regulatory period.

13.4.6 Customer information
Stakeholders consistently reported confusion about how the new sewerage
charges would operate, including the role of sewerage discharge factors and
the application of fixed charges.
The Economic Regulator requires TasWater to implement clear customer
communications to explain how the new sewerage charging arrangements
operate. Customer bills must also clearly explain the components used to
calculate sewerage charges.
Decision 13.11: To require TasWater to communicate clearly with
customers about how the new fixed and variable sewerage
charging approach works.

13.5 Motor home dump points
A motor home dump point is a facility intended to receive the discharge of
wastewater from any holding tank or similar device installed in a recreational
vehicle. These dump points are usually located in or near caravan parks.
TasWater considers that motor home dump points are equivalent to a full
service sewerage connection. TasWater does not propose imposing a
variable sewerage charge on motor home dump points. Given that there are
no changes that would justify moving away from the past approach, the
Economic Regulator has approved a fixed sewerage charge for this service
as set out in Table 13.6.

13.6 Septic tank effluent disposal schemes
There are various septic tank effluent disposal schemes. Under these
schemes, TasWater receives the liquid waste from customers’ septic tanks
for treatment at one of its sewage treatment plants. Customers participating
in these schemes buy and maintain their own septic tanks and engage a
contractor to pump out the tank and to undertake maintenance, such as
desludging.
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed that properties within
septic tank effluent disposal scheme areas are charged at 70 per cent of a
full service sewerage fixed charge. This proposal reflects current
arrangements, where these customers are charged at a reduced rate of one
equivalent tenement. TasWater does not propose applying a variable
sewerage charge on septic tank effluent disposal schemes.
The Economic Regulator notes that the 30 per cent reduction accounts for
the owner having to engage a contractor to pump and desludge their septic
tank approximately every three years at their own cost. Considering the lower
costs to TasWater of providing this service compared to full-service
customers, the Economic Regulator considers that applying a reduced rate
for this service is appropriate.
However, given TasWater’s move to a fixed and variable charging approach
for sewerage for the fifth regulatory period, it is not clear why the proposed
charge for septic tank effluent disposal schemes is linked to the fixed
sewerage charge rather than applying the variable charge to the amount of
effluent disposed of.
The Economic Regulator considers that while there are likely to be some
fixed costs for TasWater in providing this service (such as maintaining
capacity at its receiving sewage treatment plants), most of its costs are likely
to be incurred in treating the effluent from septic tanks once received.
In the absence of any supporting data on the costs of providing these
services to customers, the Economic Regulator’s decision is to approve
TasWater’s proposal for septic tank effluent disposal schemes.
154

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Decision 13.13: To approve a maximum charge of $556.82 in
2026-27 for septic tank effluent disposal schemes.
The Economic Regulator’s prices for septic tank effluent disposal schemes

14

Unconnected properties in serviced land

Service charges apply to a property owner when the property has access to a
water or sewerage service, even if it is not physically connected to
TasWater’s infrastructure. This is because, when TasWater installs
infrastructure in an area, it designs the network with sufficient capacity to
serve all properties that could connect.
The Economic Regulator released its Inquiry into the Level of TasWater’s
Service Charges, Final Report, December 2023 on TasWater’s service
charges. The Economic Regulator found that the imposition of service
charges is consistent with the Industry Act and the pricing principles. The
Economic Regulator also found that shifting the cost base burden from
unconnected customers to connected customers would be less consistent
with the pricing principles, as it does not reflect the costs of providing
services (TasWater’s infrastructure has been built so that unconnected
properties can be connected within 10 business days on request). The
Economic Regulator also required TasWater to review and calculate fixed
and variable costs related to supplying unconnected properties within
serviced land. However, TasWater did not provide this information when
proposing its fixed charges for the fifth regulatory period.
TasWater proposed that the unconnected property service charge comprise
of:
❑

a charge for water equal to the fixed water charge (full service) if there
is a water main within 30 metres of the property; and

❑

a charge for sewerage equal to 60 per cent of the fixed sewerage
charge if there is a sewerage main within 30 metres of the property.

TasWater’s proposed unconnected property service charges for the fifth
regulatory period are shown in Table 14.1. As TasWater has proposed to
increase its variable charges and decrease its fixed charges, under its
proposal, the unconnected property service charges decrease.

15

Prices for trade waste services

Commercial and industrial activities generate liquid waste streams that differ
significantly in composition and strength from typical residential sewage.
When discharged to the sewerage network, this trade waste places additional
load on TasWater’s infrastructure and treatment processes. To recover the
extra costs associated with managing and treating these higher‑impact waste
streams, TasWater applies trade waste charges.
Following the 2022 Price Determination Investigation, the
Economic Regulator undertook an Inquiry into Trade Waste Charging,
releasing its Final Report in October 2024. The Economic Regulator’s main
conclusion from this Inquiry was that the case for fundamental change to the
trade waste charging arrangements had not been made.
Inquiry into TasWater’s Trade Waste Charging Approach Final Report
(3 October 2024)112
The Economic Regulator decided:
❑

not to support fundamental changes to the trade waste framework for commercial
customers;

❑

to continue to not regulate prices for industrial trade waste customers for the fifth
regulatory period; and

❑

to require TasWater to formalise its review process and promote its availability
before the fifth regulatory period.

In this Inquiry, the Economic Regulator noted that adding more customer
categories to the current risk-based charging approach appears to be a
simple to implement response that would address the issues raised by some
stakeholders.

15.1 TasWater’s current approach to trade waste
charging
TasWater currently uses a risk-based scoring approach to categorise trade
waste customers. This score is based on business activity, substance of
concern, pre-treatment requirements, and trade waste volumes.
Trade waste discharge factors are applied to the metered water volume to
estimate the volume of trade waste discharged by a business, which
influences categorisation. Table 15.1 sets out the current trade waste
categories used by TasWater to determine trade waste charges.

Customers assessed as discharging low volume and
low impact trade waste which poses minimal risk to
the sewerage infrastructure and can be managed
through simple pre-treatment devices such as
screens or tanks with minimal maintenance
requirements.

Customers assessed as discharging trade waste to
the sewerage infrastructure that can be managed

For trade waste categories 1, 2A, 2B and 2C, TasWater charges an annual
consent fee.. Prices for Category 3 and 4 trade waste customers are not
regulated by the Economic Regulator. This is based on the understanding
that prices are agreed between TasWater and the customer through
contracts issued under section 61 of the Industry Act.
Compliant commercial pre-treatment removes about 80 per cent of waste. If
a customer cannot install or maintain pre-treatment, TasWater applies a
non-compliance charge. This charge covers the additional costs associated
with treatment and maintenance.
Non-compliance charges are currently split between:
❑

minor: failure to maintain or install pre-treatment for up to 12 months.
For the applicable category (1, 2A, 2B, 2C), an additional fee equal to
the consent fee applies; and

❑

major: inadequate pre-treatment and failure to upgrade, or discharge
of harmful substances. For the applicable category (2A, 2B, 2C), an
additional fee equal to double the consent fee applies.

Some aged-care and healthcare facilities use disposable paper bedpans
shredded by macerators before discharge into the sewerage network. This
can cause sewer blockages, and therefore TasWater’s current trade waste
charges include a macerator charge per individual bedpan macerator.

15.2 TasWater’s proposed new approach to trade waste
charging
TasWater has proposed significant changes to trade waste charging for the
fifth regulatory period, including:
❑

merging the five commercial categories into one category;

❑

combining Category 3 and 4 customers into a single industrial
category;

❑

creating charging bands based on business types with similar effluent
quality: high biochemical oxygen demand (BOD), low BOD,
automotive, laundry, and other;113

❑

applying cost to serve (fixed) charges based on administrative costs;
and

❑

applying cost to treat (variable) charges based on the estimated mass
of treating BOD, suspended solids, grease, nitrogen, and phosphorus.

Cost to serve fees would be calculated using the average assessment time
multiplied by a corporate charge-out rate. TasWater’s proposed cost to serve
fees are set out in Table 15.2.

15.3 Economic Regulator’s draft trade waste
considerations
The Economic Regulator’s review and analysis identified several areas of
concern with TasWater’s proposed trade waste charging approach.

Lack of review of trade waste discharge factors
TasWater did not review its trade waste discharge factors as part of its
approach to developing new trade waste charges. These factors were
introduced in the second regulatory period for the risk-based model and may
not be appropriate in the context of volumetric charging.
TasWater also did not appear to have reconciled its trade waste discharge
factors with its proposed sewerage discharge factors, creating the risk of
charging overlap. A comparison of Table 15.4 and 13.3 shows that the
discharge factors differ significantly by business classification and activity.

Lack of justification for increased non-compliance charge
Under TasWater’s proposal, the current tiered non-compliance structure
would be replaced with a single charge set at five times the proposed
standard commercial consent fee. TasWater justified this approach on the
basis that compliant pretreatment is approximately 80 per cent effective, with
non-compliant customers required to pay the difference. However, TasWater
did not provide sufficient evidence to support this claim or to directly link the
costs of treating non-compliant trade waste to the proposed amount of the
non-compliance charge.

Lack of customer consultation on proposed trade waste charges
While TasWater undertook widespread consultation on its proposed PSP5,
including Business Customer Briefings in August 2024, the specific proposed
trade waste price structures were not presented or consulted on. Given the
material departure from current arrangements, the Economic Regulator was
concerned that customers may not understand the impact of the proposed
charges.

15.4 Economic Regulator’s draft decisions
The Economic Regulator acknowledged the significant work undertaken by
TasWater to develop its proposed trade waste framework. However, given
the issues identified, the Economic Regulator considered that further work
was required before implementing such fundamental changes. Accordingly,
the draft decision was to retain the current trade waste charging structure for
the fifth regulatory period.
While acknowledging TasWater’s commitment to improving the transparency
and cost‑reflectivity, the Economic Regulator considered that further work
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

163

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

was required to ensure the framework is robust, equitable and consistent with
regulatory objectives. In particular, TasWater must undertake the following
actions before the proposal can be assessed:
❑

conduct comprehensive stakeholder consultation with commercial
trade waste customers and document how feedback has informed the
final approach.

❑

review its trade waste discharge factors alongside sewerage
discharge factors, including alignment of business activity categories,
to address risks of duplication or overlap.

❑

review the application of its approved Trade Waste Policy to
ensure it is consistent with regulatory principles and customer
expectations.

Although trade waste non-compliance charges were approved in the fourth
regulatory period, TasWater did not provide forecasts of non-compliance
events or associated revenue for the fifth regulatory period. As prices are set
by matching allowable revenue to forecast demand, this creates a risk of
over-recovery. On this basis, the Economic Regulator’s draft decision was
not to approve non-compliance trade waste charges for the fifth regulatory
period, subject to reconsideration if sufficient evidence is provided prior to the
Final Report.

15.5 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater expressed disappointment with the Economic Regulator’s decision
to retain the current trade waste pricing framework, arguing that its proposed
approach is more contemporary and transparent. It considers that the
Economic Regulator’s concerns about potential double-dipping arise from a
misunderstanding of the interaction between trade waste charges and
sewerage charges.
TasWater submits that it has undertaken sufficient engagement with trade
waste customers, noting that these customers are an established cohort
subject to regular regulatory oversight and ongoing operational contact. It
reports conducting more than 1 600 site visits, with negligible feedback on
the proposed reforms, and considers this demonstrates that customers are
broadly comfortable with both the existing and proposed arrangements.
While TasWater’s preference is to implement the new methodology from the
start of the fifth regulatory period, it has indicated that commencement could
be deferred to 1 July 2027 to allow for targeted communication, customer
education and further analysis of discharge factors.
TasWater also raised concerns that, in the absence of non-compliance
charges, disconnection would be its only enforcement option for trade waste
breaches. It proposed a stepped escalation framework, applying a
164

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

three-times multiplier for minor non-compliance and a five-times multiplier for
major or persistent breaches. However, TasWater did not provide forecasts
for expected non-compliance events or associated revenue, arguing that the
charges are intended to be preventative and reactive.
Sorell Council expressed support for transition to a fixed and variable trade
waste charging approach. However, it noted that there was a need for further
consultation with high-volume trade waste users and phasing transitional
arrangements to avoid disruption to business growth in developing areas.
Fonterra’s submission noted that TasWater has outlined a complex and
insufficient approach for trade waste charging within the proposed PSP5.
Simplot expressed support for the Economic Regulator’s recommendations
regarding trade waste in the Draft Report and submitted that any future
review should include category 3 and 4 trade waste customers (currently
unregulated).
Simplot also submitted that, in its experience, trade waste charges for
category 3 and 4 trade waste customers were mandated, rather than agreed
by the customer and TasWater, with no regulation or avenue for escalation.
Mondelēz’s submission supported the Economic Regulator’s draft decision
that TasWater’s trade waste proposals were inadequate and should not
proceed in their current form.
“TasWater’s proposed changes to the trade waste pricing mechanism would
have imposed substantial cost increases on industrial users without
corresponding improvement in service, without adequate consultation with
affected parties, and without a credible transition pathway.”114

15.6 Economic Regulator’s decisions
The Draft Report set out clearly the actions required before the Economic
Regulator could consider TasWater’s proposed new approach to trade waste
charging, including comprehensive stakeholder consultation, a review of
trade waste discharge factors, and a review of the application of the
approved Trade Waste Policy. While TasWater’s submission seeks to
address these matters, it does not demonstrate that this work has been
completed to a standard that would allow a new charging framework to be
adequately assessed.
The Economic Regulator understands that, in theory, TasWater’s proposed
charging approach attempts to remove the domestic sewerage equivalent
from the variable trade waste charges. In practice, however, the risk of
double-dipping is entirely dependent on the accuracy of the trade waste
discharge factors that are applied.

114 Submission from Mondelēz International, page 1.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

165

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Draft Report noted that TasWater would need to carry out a review of its
trade waste discharge factors, which should include how they interact with
the sewerage discharge factors, and how they align the business activity
groups to provide clarity for customers. This is not addressed in TasWater’s
submission.
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s interpretation that
limited feedback obtained through compliance inspections indicates broad
customer support, nor does it consider inspections to be an appropriate
consultation mechanism.
The Economic Regulator recognises the important role that genuine
consultation and feedback have in shaping decisions. On that basis, the
Economic Regulator rejects TasWater’s proposal that its new trade waste
charging approach be included in the Final Determination, with a delayed
commencement date.
As TasWater has not adequately addressed the concerns raised in the Draft
Report, the current trade waste charging approach will be maintained for the
fifth regulatory period with the prices to apply included in Table 15.5.

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

16

Tankered waste

Tankered waste refers to trade waste that is accepted directly by TasWater
at its designated sewage treatment plants. While pricing for tankered waste is
unregulated, the service TasWater provides is regulated as trade waste and
included under the definition of sewage in the Industry Act.
TasWater has stated that its current tankered waste charges are based on
charges previously established by councils. This approach accounts for the
variation in tankered waste pricing across sewage treatment plants that
accept trade waste. According to TasWater, it has generally applied CPI
adjustments to the prices since 2017.

16.1 Customer service
During the fourth regulatory period, a tankered waste contractor raised
concerns with the Economic Regulator about tankered waste pricing and the
absence of minimum service standards. The issue related to the receiving
sewage treatment plant being closed at short notice preventing the discharge
of tankered waste and negatively impacting tankered waste contractors.
The Economic Regulator considers it important that TasWater provides
timely notice to contractors about any issues affecting the discharge of
waste, including expected outage timeframes and the availability of
alternative treatment plants.
TasWater has indicated that it is pursuing a Tankered Waste Reform
Strategy over the fifth regulatory period to improve operational efficiency,
environmental outcomes and public trust. To achieve this, TasWater is:
❑

rationalising its receiving sites to optimise resource allocation;

❑

enhancing safety protocols at treatment plants, particularly at
unmanned facilities;

❑

introducing cost-reflective pricing to ensure fair funding and incentivise
best practices; and

❑

investigating advanced technologies, such as anaerobic digestion, to
recover energy and reduce waste volumes.116

To improve transparency around the provision of tankered waste services,
the Economic Regulator made the draft decision to require TasWater to
provide it with updates on the implementation of its Tankered Waste Reform
Strategy during the fifth regulatory period, including changes in receival sites,
any changes to pricing, anticipated price impacts on tankered waste
contractors and any proposed changes to tankered waste contracts.
116 TasWater Wastewater Risk Management Plan PSP5, page 8.

168

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

16.2 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
Two submissions provided feedback in relation to tankered waste.
Sorell Council’s submission notes that it supports enhanced reporting and
transparency and further recommends that reports include performance
metrics, timing, and volumes, to improve customer confidence in reliability.
TasWater’s submission accepts the Economic Regulator’s draft decision and
confirms that it will provide updates regarding the implementation of its
Tankered Waste Strategy during the fifth regulatory period.

16.3 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator has maintained its decision from the Draft Report,
to require TasWater to provide the Economic Regulator with updates on the
implementation of its Tankered Waste Reform Strategy and changes to
tankered waste prices and/or services.
Decision 16.1: To require TasWater to provide it with updates on
the implementation of its Tankered Waste Reform Strategy during
the fifth regulatory period, including changes in receival sites, any
changes to pricing, anticipated price impacts on tankered waste
contractors and any proposed changes to tankered waste
contracts.

17

Prices for development services

Developer charges are cost recovery fees imposed on developers to fund the
expansion or upgrade of water and sewerage infrastructure required to
service new developments.
Parties seeking to make new connections to TasWater’s infrastructure are
subject to developer charges and/or infrastructure contributions to support
network expansion and capacity upgrades.
Consistent with its approach for the fourth regulatory period, TasWater
proposed three major components to developer charges in its proposed
PSP5:
❑

headworks charges: applied to each lot created by a new
development;

❑

works internal to the development: paid for by the developer and
usually gifted to TasWater; and

❑

works external to the development: extensions paid for by the
developer and gifted to TasWater.117

TasWater also proposed abolishing the Bulk Infrastructure Contribution
Charge, which was introduced in the fourth regulatory period, but was not
imposed by TasWater during that period.

17.1 TasWater proposed to change its calculation
method for headworks charges
TasWater proposed that its standard developer charges be renamed to
headworks charges, as this is a term that is widely known and understood
by developers and stakeholders.
TasWater proposed that headworks charges will continue to be imposed on
all developments that increase demand on TasWater’s infrastructure, either
through consumption of excess capacity in bulk water and sewer
infrastructure or by contributing to the need for future network expansion.
TasWater proposed significant changes to its approach for calculating
headworks charges for the fifth regulatory period, including:
❑

Separating the single headworks charge into separate charges for
water and sewerage.

117 Capacity upgrades may be paid for by the developer or costs shared between the developer and

TasWater.

170

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

The application of standard charge for both water and sewerage
based on a 20 mm connection. For properties with larger water
connections, a size-based multiplier would apply. For sewerage, the
same water connection size would apply, with an additional
adjustment using the relevant sewerage discharge factor to reflect the
expected level of discharges to the sewerage network.

❑

extending the recognition of historical capex from four years to
10 years in the calculation of headworks charges.

TasWater’s proposed water and sewerage headworks charges per standard
20 mm connection for the fifth regulatory period are shown in Table 17.1.

17.2 Economic Regulator’s draft decisions
The Economic Regulator’s draft decisions were to:
❑

accept the proposal to apply separate headworks charges for water
and for sewerage;

❑

not accept TasWater’s proposal to apply multipliers to the water and
sewerage headworks charges based on the size of water connections;

❑

require TasWater to recalculate the proposed water headworks charge
and sewerage headworks charge to take into account the net present
value of historical incremental capex for four years (i.e. the fourth
regulatory period);

❑

require TasWater to update its calculation of headworks charges to
incorporate any changes to base assumptions (such as the
depreciation rate, weighted average cost of capital, inflation, fixed and
variable charges, and capital and operational expenditure) that were
reflected in the Draft Report;

❑

to accept TasWater’s proposed approach to works internal and works
external to a new development; and

❑

to require TasWater to provide further information and to justify its
proposed increases in development service miscellaneous fees and
charges.

171

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

17.3 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
The Economic Regulator received a number of submissions in response to
the Draft Report that related to developer charges and development service
miscellaneous fees and charges. Some respondents advised the Economic
Regulator that it is important that headworks charges are transparent and
predictable, allocate costs equitably between existing users and new
developments and do not deter new housing and business development.
However, several submissions alerted the Economic Regulator to the fact
that it had misunderstood the application of the standard headworks charge
during the PSP4 period. The Economic Regulator’s Draft Report implied that
the previous standard charge was applied on a per lot basis, when it was
actually applied on a per equivalent tenement basis. As a result, the
Economic Regulator’s draft decision not to accept TasWater’s proposed
multipliers for headworks charges may mean that large developments on
single lots do not contribute fairly to the cost of water and sewerage services.
Several respondents expressed the view that headworks charges are a
component of the total cost stack of a development and they can influence
whether a project is viable or not.
TasWater’s submission
TasWater submitted that it does not consider the Draft Report’s proposed
approach to developer charges to be in the long-term interests of customers
or the Tasmanian community, with the outcomes reducing certainty for
long-term infrastructure planning and weaking pricing signals. In particular,
TasWater noted that the Draft Report overlooks the fact that a multiplier
already applies to the developer charges, with developer charges currently
multiplied by the number of equivalent tenements that a proposed
development is expected to generate.
TasWater’s submission also addressed the Economic Regulator’s draft
decision to only recognise four years of historical capex in the calculation of
developer charges, and argued that there is no basis to assume that a new
development draws only on assets commissioned during the previous
regulatory period. Instead, TasWater stated that it had assessed the portion
of capex it incurred in servicing growth since 2016 and adjusted it to reflect
the spare capacity used by actual developments since that time, with the
remaining capacity to be recovered from future developments.
TasWater argued that extending the period of historical growth-related capital
costs further enhances cost-reflectivity. A broader time horizon ensures a
more equitable allocation of costs across developments that equally draw on
assets or contribute to the need for investment. Using shorter periods of
historical growth-related capital costs will increase volatility in headworks
charges from one regulatory period to the next.

172

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater’s submission also contends that the cost burden of higher
developer charges is borne by the owners of vacant land and will not impact
on housing prices. Additionally, where headworks charges are set below
cost-reflective levels, the resulting shortfall must be recovered from the
broader customer base through higher recurrent charges than would
otherwise be required. In effect, existing customers would subsidise growth.
Other stakeholders’ submissions
TasCOSS’ submission notes its concern that TasWater’s proposal has the
potential for it to substantially increase the cost of new housing projects that
could result in fewer housing projects being commenced.
The Tasmanian Chamber of Commerce and Industry states that developer
charging reform should support efficiency and investment, not create new
barriers to business expansion and housing supply.
The Committee for Greater Hobart submits that headworks charges are not
simply a technical pricing mechanism, but are a component of the total costs
of development and can influence project feasibility. On this basis, it notes
that headworks methodologies should be transparent and predictable;
allocate costs equitably between existing users and new developments;
reflect actual capacity and investment requirements; and support efficient use
of existing infrastructure networks.
The JAC Group has indicated that it understands and supports TasWater’s
proposal for developer charges and that based on its experience in other
states, the charges proposed are modest.
The submission from J A Walker reflects concerns about the Economic
Regulator’s draft decision not to approve multipliers, with the costs of
required capacity upgrades to TasWater’s treatment plants otherwise being
unfairly passed on to existing connected customers.
Sorell Council submits that recalibrating developer charges without aligning
to actual incremental costs can diminish development feasibility in
high-growth local government areas and slow the housing pipeline. The
Council recommends publishing the methodology used for calculating
charges, to provide developers with confidence to plan and deliver housing
supply.
The Treasurer’s submission notes concerns about the size, transparency and
awareness of TasWater’s proposed changes to developer charges. It also
notes customer concerns regarding development service miscellaneous fees
that are determined on a price on application basis. These concerns relate
to the limited availability of clear classifications, cost structures and defined
connection types which removes transparency and consistency for
customers.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

173

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

17.4 Economic Regulator’s decisions
17.4.1 Separate charges for water and sewerage
The Economic Regulator considers that TasWater’s proposed approach to
applying separate water and sewerage headworks charges will increase
transparency and cost-reflectivity in its pricing. This decision is unchanged
from the Draft Report.
Decision 17.1: To accept the proposal to apply separate headworks
charges for water and for sewerage.

17.4.2 Applying multipliers
The Economic Regulator’s draft decision was to not apply a multiplier based
on the water connection size of a new development on the basis that this
change would substantially increase headworks charges for larger
developments. Stakeholders, including TasWater, raised concerns that the
Economic Regulator’s draft decision would result in substantial commercial
developments not adequately contributing to their load on water and
sewerage systems. As noted, TasWater’s submission also provided further
information on how its current developer charges are imposed, including the
application of a multiplier.
The Draft Report included an illustrative headworks charge of $1.1 million for
a development that required a 250 mm water connection. TasWater advised
that a development requiring a 250 mm water connection would equate to a
minimum of approximately 650 equivalent tenements. Applying a per
equivalent tenement charge using TasWater’s proposed total headworks
charge ($7 048 in 2026-27) would equate to a total headworks charge of
$4.58 million, compared to a $1.1 million under its proposed multiplier
methodology.
The Economic Regulator accepts this correction. Analysis indicates that
headworks charges are likely to be less under the proposed connection-size
multiplier methodology than they would be under a per-equivalent tenement
methodology.
Decision 17.2: To accept TasWater’s proposal to apply multipliers
to the water and sewerage headworks charges based on the size of
water connections.
TasWater also proposed to apply sewerage discharge factors to the reflect
the expected use of the development in the sewerage headworks charge.
Sewerage discharge factors are designed to reflect the potential load a
174

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

customer could place on the sewerage system, based on their amount of
metered water. The application of this discharge factor to the sewerage
headworks charge, which is meant to fund infrastructure upgrades, is not
appropriate.
The Economic Regulator does not accept TasWater’s proposal to apply
discharge factors to calculate sewerage headworks charges.
Decision 17.3: To not accept TasWater’s proposal to apply
discharge factor multipliers to the sewerage headworks charges.

17.4.3 Extending the recognition of historical capex to 10 years
The Economic Regulator’s draft decision was to require TasWater to
recalculate the proposed water and sewerage headworks charges using the
net present value of historical incremental capex for four years (i.e. the fourth
regulatory period), rather than the 10 years TasWater had proposed.
The Economic Regulator accepts the arguments put forward in TasWater’s
submission to its Draft Report and TasWater’s proposal to take into account
the net present value of historical incremental capex for 10 years (2015-16 to
2025-26) for the purposes of calculating the water and sewerage headworks
charges.
Decision 17.4: To accept TasWater’s proposal to take into account
the net present value of historical incremental capex for 10 years
(2015-16 to 2025-26) for the purposes of calculating the water and
the sewerage headworks charges.

17.4.4 Water and sewerage headworks charges
The headworks model was adjusted to reflect the Economic Regulator’s
decisions, including inflation, opex, capex, tariffs, WACC and forecast
demand. Additionally, the Economic Regulator removed any non-growth
related projects from the forecast capex profile.
The Economic Regulator’s water and sewerage headworks charges per
standard 20 mm connection for the fifth regulatory period are shown in
Table 17.2.

17.4.5 Works internal to the development
TasWater’s proposed approach is unchanged from the fourth regulatory
period, whereby reticulation assets (usually water and sewerage mains, but
sometimes reservoirs and pump stations) within the development are built by
the developer and gifted to TasWater.
The costs of works internal to a new development are in addition to any
headworks charges payable.
The Economic Regulator’s decision is unchanged from the Draft Report.
Decision 17.6: To accept TasWater’s proposed approach to works
internal to a new development.

17.4.6 Works external to the development
“Works external” refers to extensions to existing water and sewerage
networks to provide service for a proposed development, or expansions to
increase the capacity of existing infrastructure.
TasWater’s proposed approach is that the developer pays the cost of
extensions required to allow a new development to connect to TasWater’s
existing reticulation networks and gifts these assets to TasWater.
Where there is insufficient system capacity to fully service a new
development (that is, a proposed development would directly cause the need
for an upgrade), the developer also pays the cost of any necessary upgrades
to TasWater’s assets. Where the system is already over capacity, TasWater
and the developer share costs on a pro-rata basis. This approach is
unchanged from the fourth regulatory period.

176

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The cost of works external to a development are in addition to any
headworks charges payable.
The Economic Regulator’s decision is unchanged from the Draft Report.
Decision 17.7: To accept TasWater’s proposed approach to works
external to a new development.

17.4.7 Development service miscellaneous fees and charges
Structure of fees and charges
TasWater proposed to continue applying fees and charges for development
services relating to the costs of managing applications for new developments
and their connection to water and sewerage services.
TasWater proposed amendments to its development service miscellaneous
fees and charges for the fifth regulatory period:
❑

the introduction of a charge for amendments to Certificate of
Certifiable Works and Engineering Design Approval fees and
Re-assessment of expired Engineering Design Approvals;

❑

the introduction of a Connections Applications Administrative Fee,
including loose supply applications (loose supply applications typically
refer to requests for water supply without a standard connection
arrangement); and

❑

removal of the charge relating to the Legacy Certificate of Compliance
for applications made before 1 July 2018.

In addition, TasWater proposed a restructure of its fees in relation to
Certificates of Certifiable Works and Certificate of Compliance and
Engineering Design Approval and Permit to Construct. TasWater indicated
that this change will allow for its development service team to issue a single
invoice related to the service provided, increasing the transparency for
customers while also being administratively simpler for TasWater.
TasWater also proposed to apply different fees depending on whether there
is a development application or not. TasWater has indicated that where there
is a development application in place, two different stages apply, including
planning and detailed design.
Finally, TasWater currently applies a fee for large and complex applications
which involve water or sewer pump stations, reservoirs, treatment plants or
significant work on bulk mains. These fees are currently ‘price on application’
(POA). TasWater proposed to reclassify these services as ‘significant’ with
defined prices.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

177

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator accepted TasWater’s proposed changes to the
structure of its development services miscellaneous fees and charges.
Economic Regulator’s assessment of fees and charges
TasWater proposed large increases to most of its development service
miscellaneous fees and charges. These charges were calculated by
TasWater using an estimate of the average time spent on applications
multiplied by TasWater’s estimated hourly rate. The more complex the
development service application, the more time TasWater staff need to
assess the application and, therefore, the higher the proposed fee.
Based on a comparison with data provided by TasWater during the fourth
regulatory period investigation process, TasWater has substantially
increased the estimated hourly rate and its estimates of the average time
spent on many of the application types. In its Draft Report, the Economic
Regulator required TasWater to provide further information and to justify its
proposed increases.
In response, TasWater provided a calculation of its development services
hourly rate for 2025-26, based on the total direct costs of the development
services team divided by the number of available hours for the team, and a
share of indirect overheads. The Economic Regulator accepts this estimate
for the purposes of setting development service fees, and accepts
TasWater’s estimates of the average amount of time spent on each activity.
The Economic Regulator has determined TasWater’s development service
miscellaneous fees and charges fees based on that data. The Economic
Regulator has indexed the proposed average hourly rate in line with
TasWater’s enterprises wage agreement.
However, there are significant increases in many developer fees and charges
that arise either from an increase in the estimated number of hours
attributable to a task or from the increase in the average hourly rate, or a
combination of both. To moderate the impact on developers that already
have projects in the pipeline, the Economic Regulator has smoothed the
increases by applying a linear transition from the 2025-26 fee (which the
Economic Regulator has estimated in some cases to account for the
proposed fee restructure) to the projected fee in 2029-30.
Several fees are set on a POA basis. The Economic Regulator considers this
method is appropriate given the bespoke nature of the works to which the
fees apply. Noting customer concerns and feedback from stakeholders, the
Economic Regulator will monitor the application of these fees to ensure that
they are cost reflective.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

18

Prices for miscellaneous services

TasWater proposes to continue to charge customers for the costs of its
provision of ad hoc activities associated with water and sewerage services.
As with other regulated charges, miscellaneous fees and charges must be
determined according to the pricing principles and be included in TasWater’s
regulated revenue.
TasWater’s proposed miscellaneous fees and charges for the fifth regulatory
period are set out in Table 18.1.
TasWater proposed two indexation methods for its fees and charges for
miscellaneous services over the fifth regulatory period:
❑

Water metering fees, sundry fees and the security deposit to be
indexed by forecast inflation as they are administrative in nature.

❑

Filling station fees to be indexed by the price increase applied across
the regulatory period.

18.1 Economic Regulator’s draft decisions
As discussed in Chapter 13, the Economic Regulator did not accept
TasWater’s proposal to charge a fee for customer-initiated reviews of
sewerage discharge factors. The Economic Regulator determined that this
review mechanism should be available to all regulated customers at no cost
(refer section 13.4).
The Economic Regulator also noted that some of TasWater’s proposed
miscellaneous fees are to be determined on a price on application basis. This
was considered appropriate provided that the fee is determined on a cost
reflective basis. The Economic Regulator accepted that costs may vary due
to the location of the property, the amount of materials required and the time
taken to complete the job.
The Economic Regulator also accepted TasWater determining fees on a
price on application basis in these circumstances. Customers have the right
to query such fees through TasWater’s complaints handling process and, if
not satisfied with the outcomes from that process, they may raise the issue
with the Ombudsman.
TasWater has notified that it is no longer proposing to reintroduce the
property information plan fee. The Economic Regulator removed this charge
from its fees and charges for miscellaneous services.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

183

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

18.2 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasCOSS raised concerns about the impact of restriction charges and
account administration fees for bounced payments, noting that these costs
often result from circumstances beyond a customer’s control.

18.3 Economic Regulator’s decisions
While recognising the concerns raised by TasCOSS, the Economic Regulator
notes that hardship provisions are available under the Customer Service
Code and, on this basis, considers it appropriate for TasWater to recover its
reasonable costs through these fees.
In considering TasCOSS’ submission, the Economic Regulator also reviewed
the information on TasWater’s website relating to miscellaneous fees,
including its restriction charges. This review identified that while the amount
of the charge is easily available on TasWater’s website, an explanation of
what this charge is and when it would apply was not available. The Economic
Regulator requires TasWater to ensure that explanations of each of these
fees are easily accessible to customers, including the circumstances when
the fees apply.
Decision 18.1: To require TasWater to make available to customers
an accessible explanation of what each of its charges is and the
circumstances in which the charge will apply.
The Economic Regulator considers TasWater’s approach to price indexation
to be reasonable, as the costs underlying the fees that are administrative in
nature are likely to increase by inflation over the period. Likewise, TasWater’s
proposal to index filling station fees by the price increase is consistent with
the approach for setting the prices for other water services. Accordingly, the
Economic Regulator accepts TasWater’s proposed approach.
Decision 18.2: To approve the fees and charges for the
miscellaneous services set out in Table 18.2 of this Final Report.

18.4 Service introduction charges
Service introduction charges are temporary charges imposed on customers
to recover a portion of the cost of constructing water and/or sewerage
infrastructure in areas not previously receiving these services.
TasWater’s Service Introduction Charges Policy states that, when service
introduction proceeds in an area, one-off service introduction charges will be
levied on the owners of properties that will gain access to the new services.
The service introduction charge is to cover the property owner’s share of the
cost of installing, altering or using TasWater’s assets needed to provide
water and/or sewerage services.
TasWater proposes to continue calculating service introduction charges by
estimating the net present value of the infrastructure costs needed to
introduce the service. It then subtracts the present value of the revenue
expected from the 80 per cent of customers in the area who could be
supplied with the service, through ongoing annual water and/or sewerage
charges, in line with its policy threshold. Any third‑party funding contributions
would also be deducted from the net present value calculation. This
approach is unchanged from the fourth regulatory period.
The Economic Regulator’s decision is to accept TasWater’s proposed
approach to continue using the current approach for calculating service
introduction charges.
Decision 18.3: To approve TasWater’s approach on the use and
calculation of service introduction charges.

186

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

19

Bill impacts

TasWater has proposed changes to both its water and sewerage tariff
structures. As discussed previously in Part 2 of this Draft Report, the
Economic Regulator’s draft decisions are to accept a shift towards increasing
the variable component of the tariffs, but not to the degree in TasWater’s
proposal.
The Economic Regulator has selected a range of representative customers
to examine the impact of its draft water and sewerage pricing decisions on
bills. These representative customers have been selected to show a range of
bill outcomes, based on different circumstances such as the level of metered
water use, the number and size of water connections and assumed
discharge into the sewerage network.

19.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
Submissions consistently highlighted concerns about the affordability and
structure of customer bills, with particular emphasis on the high proportion of
fixed water and sewerage charges and the limited ability for customers to
influence their bills through changes in water use. Many residential
submissions, especially low usage, single occupant and low income
households, raised that the majority of their bill was unavoidable, leading to
perceptions that price increases would exacerbate existing cost-of-living
pressures and undermine the effectiveness of usage based pricing.
“I want more control over my bills … it disincentivises me to be careful with my
water usage when 87.7% of my bill is a fixed charge”.118

Business and industry representatives also raised concerns about bill
volatility and the cumulative impact of recent and proposed increases, noting
the importance of predictable and gradual price paths.
COTA Tasmania commented on the Draft Report’s discussion of bill impacts,
emphasising the importance of presenting affordability outcomes in ways that
are readily understood by customers on fixed or low incomes. COTA
highlighted that bill impacts can vary significantly across households and
noted that “there is no meaningful ‘average customer’ where consumption
and impacts are skewed”, encouraging clear communication of how pricing
and investment decisions affect different customer groups, particularly those
more exposed to bill pressure.

118 Submission from Noah Vale.

19.2 Estimated bill impacts on residential customers
The Economic Regulator has selected a range of representative household
types and consumption levels to analyse the impact of the draft decisions on
customer bills. It is assumed that all residential customers have one 20 mm
water connection and that 90 per cent of metered water is discharged to the
sewer.
188

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Figure 19.1 shows representative household bills for water and sewerage in
2026-27, compared to bills in 2025-26. This analysis also includes the
estimated bill outcomes from TasWater’s price proposals, as presented in its
proposed PSP5.119
Figure 19.1: Estimated water and sewerage bills for different types of households in
2026-27, compared with estimated bills for 2025-26 and TasWater’s proposal for
2026-27, assumed annual levels of water consumption ($, nominal)

19.2.1 Estimated bill impacts on residential rental properties
Under the Residential Tenancies Act 1997, an owner of a property may
collect from tenants any money in relation to a water consumption charge.
TasWater’s proposed PSP5 incorrectly states that rental properties may be
subject to a bill of $445 under its proposals.120 This is because it has included
the variable sewerage charge in its bill calculations, which are unable to be
charged to renters under the Residential Tenancies Act.

19.3 Estimated bill impacts on non-residential customers
For non-residential customers there can be considerable variation between
businesses. The variation can relate to having multiple water connections
and different sized water connections, the metered water usage, and the
introduction of sewerage discharge factors. Also, sewerage discharge factors
will vary if a business also pays trade waste charges under the Economic
Regulator’s draft decisions.

119 It is noted that TasWater’s customer impact analysis presented in its proposed PSP5 only includes

its price increase of 8.8 per cent per annum, not the full 11.5 per cent per annum it has effectively
requested.
120 TasWater’s PSP5 Proposal, page 211.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

189

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator’s assessment of bill impacts for non‑residential
customers has been further complicated by limitations in the information
supplied by TasWater. For example, TasWater has not provided a complete
dataset containing all of the key characteristics needed to calculate bill
impacts, such as connection size, metered water use, nature of the business
(for determining the appropriate sewerage discharge factor) or whether that
business is also a trade waste customer.
Without reliable data to calculate bill impacts, the Economic Regulator has
instead modelled estimated water and sewerage bills for a set of “typical”
customer scenarios.
To create these typical customer scenarios, the Economic Regulator has had
to make several assumptions. These include how much water the customer
uses (based on meter readings), the size of their water connection, whether
they are likely to have a trade waste agreement, and how many equivalent
tenements they would be charged under the 2025-26 sewerage charging
approach.

Part 3: Customer
Contract, policies and
service standards

194

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

20

Introduction to the customer contract,
policies and service standards

TasWater’s Customer Contract, supporting policies, and service standards
form a critical component of the regulatory framework governing water and
sewerage services in Tasmania. These documents collectively define the
relationship between TasWater and its customers, ensuring that services are
delivered in a manner that is fair, transparent, and consistent with legislative
requirements. The documents specify rights and obligations, minimum
service levels, and provide mechanisms for accountability, thereby
safeguarding customer interests while supporting TasWater’s operational
objectives.
TasWater submitted a draft Customer Contract, together with draft policies,
processes and proposed service standards, as part of its proposed PSP5.
The Economic Regulator has reviewed the proposed arrangements to ensure
they comply with regulatory requirements and promote positive outcomes for
customers. The assessment also aims to ensure that TasWater maintains
clear, accountable and transparent service standards.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

195

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

21

Service standards

The Economic Regulator is responsible for overseeing the standards of
regulated water and sewerage services, as prescribed in the Customer
Service Code. These service standards establish performance measures and
targets that are used to assess whether TasWater is meeting its
commitments and delivering value to customers. By defining measurable
benchmarks, the Customer Service Code ensures accountability, identifies
performance gaps and drives continuous improvements, so customers
continue to receive services that reflect the value of what they pay for. To
enforce these standards, the Economic Regulator monitors TasWater’s
performance through a formal reporting framework to measure adherence to
approved service levels.
While most standards are set by the Economic Regulator, some
requirements placed on TasWater fall under the responsibility of other
industry regulators. Drinking water quality is regulated by the Director of
Public Health, environmental standards are overseen by the EPA, and dam
safety is managed by the Delegate for Dam Safety Regulation (NRE Tas).
The Economic Regulator’s current approach to regulating water and
sewerage services includes:
❑

establishing the Customer Service Code;

❑

setting minimum service standards within the Customer Service Code;

❑

requiring TasWater to maintain a customer charter; and

❑

implementing a performance reporting framework to monitor
TasWater’s performance against approved minimum service
standards.

21.1 TasWater’s requirements and responsibilities
As part of its proposed Price and Service Plan, TasWater is required to
specify the minimum service standards it intends to apply. For each proposed
standard, TasWater must provide a clear rationale that demonstrates how the
proposal reflects customer consultation outcomes, considers the associated
costs and benefits, and aligns with its capacity to achieve the proposed
minimum service standards.
TasWater is also required to justify any changes to existing service standards
or performance targets. This justification should explain changes in operating
costs, legislative requirements, or other relevant factors influencing the
setting of service standards.

196

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

21.2 TasWater’s proposed service standards
21.2.1 Water service standards
Water losses
TasWater currently measures water losses through two service standards:
unaccounted for water and real losses.
Water losses can be categorised into two types:
❑

real losses: leakage and overflows from mains, service reservoirs and service
connections prior to customer meters; and

❑

apparent losses: unauthorised consumption and customer metering errors.

For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed replacing both with a
single standard that measures the percentage of drinking water lost as
leakage, supported by a $100.6 million leakage reduction program and
additional investment in mains and metering renewals.
Figure 21.1: TasWater’s historical percentage of unaccounted for water, compared to
its approved targets

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

197

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
Figure 21.2: TasWater’s historical real losses, compared to its approved targets for
the fourth regulatory period

Water security
Water restrictions in Tasmania occur for a range of reasons, including
drought, low rainfall, storage limitations, water quality issues and
infrastructure constraints. TasWater proposed a new service standard to
measure the percentage of customers affected by water restrictions due to
water security issues, with targets falling from less than five per cent to less
than three per cent over the fifth regulatory period.
Conserving water
TasWater found that customers support water conservation and proposed a
new service standard based on residential water use.
Response time to attend bursts and leaks
TasWater prioritises its response to bursts and leaks based on their potential
impact, classifying incidents into three priority levels.
Under the Customer Service Code, incidents are classified into three priority levels:
❑

Priority 1: a burst or leak that causes, or has potential to cause, substantial
damage or harm to customers, water quality, flow rate, property or environment.

❑

Priority 2: a burst or a leak that causes, or has the potential to cause, minor
damage or harm to customers, water quality, flow rate, property or environment.

❑

Priority 3: a burst or leak that causes no discernible impact on customers,
property or the environment.

For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed replacing the three
separate response time standards with a single weighted metric.

198

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

Number of water main breaks
Australian water utilities track water main breaks per 100 km of mains to
measure service reliability and network condition. In 2024‑25, TasWater
reported 34.1 breaks per 100km, which is 3.1 breaks above its target of 31.
TasWater proposed reducing this to no more than 16 breaks per 100 km by
the final year of the fifth regulatory period.

Number per 100km of
water main

Unplanned interruptions
Unplanned water interruptions occur when customers suddenly lose access
to water, usually due to bursts or other faults in the network. TasWater
proposed replacing its current measure (unplanned interruptions per
1 000 properties) with a new customer‑focused indicator tracking how many
customers experience more than five interruptions a year.
In 2024-25, TasWater recorded 234 unplanned water supply interruptions per
1 000 properties, significantly exceeding the service standard target of 167.

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater also proposed replacing its two existing measures for restoring
unplanned water interruptions with a single weighted service standard that
combines rectification times for Priority 1, 2 and 3 bursts and leaks.
Planned interruptions
Planned interruptions occur when TasWater carries out scheduled
maintenance, and while counting these interruptions is not useful, the time
taken to restore supply is an important indicator of performance.
TasWater proposed removing the two existing service standards for restoring
planned water supply interruptions.
Microbiological compliance
TasWater proposed introducing a new service standard requiring
100 per cent microbiological compliance for the fifth regulatory period, noting
that safe drinking water is a core customer expectation and that it has
already achieved full compliance since 2019‑20.

21.2.2 Sewerage service indicators
Number of sewer main breaks and chokes
Australian water utilities measure sewer main breaks and chokes per 100 km
of sewer main to assess service quality and network condition. TasWater
proposed to keep this measure and reduce its target to 20 breaks per 100 km
by 2030. Its recent performance, 63 breaks per 100 km in 2024‑25, is well
above its existing target of 39.
TasWater also proposed a new metric tracking customers who experience
more than three sewer interruptions a year.
Response to sewer spills, breaks and chokes
TasWater currently measures how quickly it responds to and contains sewer
spills, breaks and chokes. While it has generally met the containment target,
it has consistently fallen short on responding within one hour during the
fourth regulatory period.
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed slightly higher response
targets and lower containment targets.
Critically notifiable sewage spills
Critically notifiable sewage spills are rare but potentially high‑impact events
defined by the EPA as spills over 10 kL in medium to high‑risk locations
during dry weather. Although TasWater previously included the number of
these spills as a service standard, the measure does not capture response or
containment performance and offers limited insight into customer or
environmental outcomes.
200

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed removing this service
standard, noting that its relevance to customers is low and that it will continue
reporting these events to the EPA as required.
Environmental impact
TasWater’s customer engagement showed strong support for protecting and
improving the environment, which led TasWater to propose new service
standards for reducing nutrient discharges, lowering carbon emissions, and
increasing the reuse of treated effluent.

21.2.3 Customer service indicators
Complaints
Customer complaints provide an important indication of dissatisfaction with
TasWater’s services, and complaints are already categorised under the
national framework by issue type. TasWater previously separated its
complaint service standards into water and sewerage complaints to improve
transparency, and the Economic Regulator agreed this separation
encouraged better performance.
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed recombining them into a
single measure but did not provide a methodology for how it would be
captured or reported. TasWater has also proposed a new service standard
requiring complaints to be responded to within 10 business days, with an
extension allowed by agreement.
TasWater Assist
TasWater’s customer engagement showed strong support for better
assistance for vulnerable customers, and TasWater proposed a new service
standard measuring how effective customers find its hardship support
programs.
TasWater plans to expand TasWater Assist with a $2.4 million investment,
offering tailored case management, financial checks and water‑efficiency
support. Although no baseline data is available, TasWater proposed
ambitious effectiveness targets of more than 80 per cent initially, rising to
85 per cent.
Call resolution and satisfaction
TasWater measures customer satisfaction and first‑contact call resolution
under existing service standards, but satisfaction targets have not been met
during the fourth regulatory period due to inconsistent survey methodologies.
For the fifth regulatory period, TasWater proposed replacing these measures
with a new set of customer experience indicators, but it did not provide the
necessary detail on how they would be defined or reported.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator
