21.3 Economic Regulator’s draft assessment
The Economic Regulator recognised that TasWater’s proposed service
standards for the fifth regulatory period were shaped by insights from its
customer engagement program. TasWater used this to inform the
development of nine key outcomes, to which each of its specific service
standards are aligned.
The Economic Regulator also recognised the importance of ensuring that
service standards reflect the aspects of service that matter most to
customers. However, it also considered several additional factors and applied
five key criteria to assess TasWater’s proposed service standards, which
were that each standard must:
❑

align with the service standards framework;

❑

be informed by actual performance data;

❑

be supported by the capital and operating expenditure programs;

❑

reflect customer preferences; and

❑

be measurable.

In applying these criteria, the Economic Regulator compared TasWater’s
proposals with its performance during the fourth regulatory period, drawing
on information from TasWater’s Annual Performance Reports and the
Bureau of Meteorology’s National Performance Reporting framework.
Overall, the Economic Regulator supported improved performance in areas
where TasWater has historically underperformed, particularly water losses,
network reliability and sewer main performance, as aspects of customer
service. Where TasWater proposed new or expanded service standards, the
Economic Regulator assessed whether the measures are clearly attributable
to TasWater’s actions, can be reliably measured, and provide meaningful
insight into customer outcomes.
In several instances, the Economic Regulator did not support proposed
changes that would reduce transparency, such as consolidating multiple
service standards into weighted metrics. Existing separate service standards
were retained where they provide clearer information on performance across
different service priorities. Similarly, new measures were not supported
where TasWater is not yet able to capture performance data, where
outcomes are largely influenced by external factors (such as weather), or
where the measure reflects customer behaviour rather than service delivery.
The Economic Regulator also did not support including service standards
that duplicate existing National Performance Reporting indicators or that
relate primarily to broader environmental outcomes rather than the quality or
reliability of services provided to customers under the Customer Service
Code.
204

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

In setting draft targets, the Economic Regulator considered TasWater’s
historical performance and the scale of proposed investment programs.
Targets were drafted to ensure they represent achievable improvements over
the fifth regulatory period, while still driving stronger performance and
accountability.
The resulting standards focused on measurable, customer-relevant
outcomes, supported by evidence and aligned with TasWater’s ability to
deliver sustained improvements over the fifth regulatory period.

21.4 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
The Economic Regulator received a number of submissions in response to
the Draft Report that related to the service standards. TasWater’s submission
raised concerns that the Draft Report’s decision to retain PSP4 service
standards does not reflect the level of performance that can be realistically
achieved under the reduced operating and capital expenditure allowances.
TasWater indicated that the Draft Report’s service standard framework
creates a misalignment between required performance and the resources
available to deliver it. TasWater submitted new targets that it believes are
achievable under the Draft Report’s expenditure allowances.
M.J. Eastley raised concerns regarding TasWater’s water losses, noting
limited improvements over the past eight years, and the need for urgent
action on leakage.
R. Wright also highlighted several issues relating to TasWater’s management
of water losses, including:
❑

uncertainty about whether the observed decline in the proportion of
sourced water supplied to customers reflected reduced treatment plant
efficiency or increased losses before treatment;

❑

inconsistent terminology in PSP5 when reporting water loss metrics,
resulting in vagueness about whether figures are based on sourced
water or drinking water produced;

❑

reliance on percentage‑based loss measures, which may not fully
reflect trends in the actual volume of water lost; and

❑

the potential need for an additional performance measure reporting
the annual volume of unmetered drinking water produced.

R. Wright also raised concerns regarding TasWater’s use of per‑person
water consumption values in its PSP5 proposal, suggesting that
per‑connection water use may provide a more accurate and verifiable
reporting basis.
COTA highlighted issues relating to affordability and vulnerable customer
outcomes, noting that monopoly pricing should not create predictable
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

205

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

hardship that is then retrospectively addressed with assistance measures.
COTA also called for clearer reporting on:
❑

the uptake and effectiveness of hardship support programs;

❑

trends in customer arrears and the use of early‑intervention practices;
and

❑

the accessibility of assistance pathways, including ensuring options
beyond digital‑only channels.

The Committee of Greater Hobart’s submission argues that beyond the size
of charges, the timing, certainty and efficiency of approval processes are
critical to whether projects are viable. It notes that delays, unclear
requirements and lengthy approval timeframes increase costs, financing risk
and uncertainty, and that in a small market like Tasmania, time delays can
cause projects to fail altogether. The submission emphasises that timely
advice, predictable processes and clear decision frameworks are just as
important as pricing, and that improving certainty can support development
without undermining infrastructure standards or long‑term cost recovery.
The submission from J.A. Walker notes that while the Draft Report
recognises the Economic Regulator’s role in considering social and
environmental outcomes alongside EPA regulation, there is no reference to
service delivery measures that include environmental performance.
Sorrell Council’s submission: “Council supports draft minimum service
standards and recommend they reflect reliability and responsiveness,
particularly for growth areas where continuity is critical. Council suggests
metrics for water quality, pressure, and sewerage reliability be measurable
and publicly reported.”

21.5 Economic Regulator’s decisions
The Economic Regulator acknowledges M. J. Eastley’s submission
concerning TasWater’s water losses. While these concerns are noted, the
Economic Regulator considers that water loss performance is already
addressed through the existing service standards and TasWater’s required
efficiency improvements for the fifth regulatory period. As a result, no
amendments to the draft decisions are warranted.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges R. Wright’s submission regarding the
interpretation of data, the calculation method for unaccounted for water, and
suggestions for additional performance metrics. The Economic Regulator
considers that most matters relate to data interpretation rather than the
regulatory framework and therefore do not necessitate changes to the draft
decisions (additional information available in section 4.2.3). However,
Mr Wright identified an important wording inconsistency in the customer
service standard for unaccounted for water. The Economic Regulator agrees
that the reference to “total sourced potable water” does not reflect the basis
of the calculation. In response, the customer service standard has been
206

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

updated to “Percentage of unaccounted for water (of total drinking water
produced)” to accurately reflect the metric used.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges COTA’s submission regarding
affordability, hardship support, and the need for stronger transparency
around outcomes for vulnerable customers. While the Economic Regulator
notes the concerns raised about reporting on hardship program uptake and
accessibility of assistance pathways, these matters fall largely outside the
scope of customer service standards, which are intended to measure service
quality and reliability rather than broader policy. However, the Economic
Regulator notes that the new customer service standard “Percentage of
customers who are accessing or have accessed the TasWater Assist
program that agree the program is effective” directly addresses the
effectiveness of hardship assistance and will enable the Economic Regulator
to assess TasWater’s performance in supporting customers experiencing
payment difficulty. As a result, no further changes to the draft decisions are
required.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges TasWater’s submission in response
to the draft service standards. The Economic Regulator notes that TasWater
did not provide additional information to support reinstating the broader suite
of service standards originally proposed in its PSP5. As a result, the
streamlined set of service standards included in the Draft Report has been
retained.
In section 17 of its submission, TasWater outlined a number of reasons why
several of its originally proposed targets were not achievable, drawing on
recent performance, operational capacity and the scale of activity that can
reasonably be delivered within the approved expenditure allowances.
The Economic Regulator considers that many of TasWater’s initial PSP5
targets were overly ambitious, and agrees that, in certain areas, a shift
towards more realistic minimum service targets is appropriate. The
acceptance of revised targets reflects this reassessment of achievability,
rather than any change to TasWater’s approved expenditure allowances.
Having considered the information provided in section 17 of TasWater’s
submission, the Economic Regulator accepts the revised targets for several
service standards. These include a number of response‑time standards,
water main performance indicators, unplanned water supply interruption
measures and selected sewerage and customer service standards. The
Economic Regulator is satisfied that these revised targets reflect a realistic
minimum level of performance.
The Economic Regulator also accepts TasWater’s justification for updating
the customer satisfaction measure to an eleven-point (0-10) scale, noting that
this approach aligns with TasWater’s current customer engagement
practices. On this basis, the Economic Regulator has therefore adopted
TasWater’s updated proposed targets for this measure.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

207

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

In addition, the Economic Regulator has amended two draft service standard
targets to reflect a balanced position between the Draft Report and
TasWater’s submission. These amendments relate to the “Percentage of
unaccounted for water (of total drinking water produced)” and the
“Percentage of drinking water supplied lost as leakage”. The revised targets
take into account TasWater’s submission while maintaining appropriate
performance expectations for customers.
However, the Economic Regulator has not accepted TasWater’s proposed
reductions for two measures. First, the target for “Percentage of planned
water supply interruptions restored within the time nominated to affected
customers” has been retained. TasWater has full control over the notification
timeframes it provides to customers in advance of a planned interruption, and
failure to meet this standard reflects the accuracy of customer
communication rather than the achievability of the standard itself. Second,
the Economic Regulator has not accepted TasWater’s proposed target for
“Complaints responded to within 10 business days”. Section 4.2.2(c) of the
Customer Service Code requires regulated entities to respond to complaints
within 10 business days, unless an extension is applied in accordance with
the Code. The appropriate target for this standard therefore remains at
100 per cent.
Overall, the Economic Regulator considers that the final service standards
strike an appropriate balance between achievability, accountability and
service quality. The adopted targets reflect a more realistic interpretation of
TasWater’s service capability for the fifth regulatory period. The final
amendments to the standards are summarised in Table 21.2.
Decision 21.1: To approve the minimum service standards for the
fifth regulatory period, as per Table 21.2 of this Report.
The Economic Regulator will implement the approved:

change process, for a 1 July 2026 commencement; and

❑

service standard definitions by way of the Tasmanian Water and
Sewerage Industry Performance and Information Reporting Guideline,
for a 1 July 2026 commencement (Details in Appendix A5).

22.1 Customer contract
TasWater has indicated that its proposed Customer Contract for the fifth
regulatory period is based on the approved Customer Contract for the fourth
regulatory period, with updates to:

❑

improve clarity through simplified language;

❑

removal of duplication;

❑

reorder certain clauses; and

❑

to reflect all relevant changes outlined in its proposed PSP5.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

The Economic Regulator has noted that TasWater did not provide details of
its customer consultation regarding the proposed Customer Contract. While
TasWater described consultation activities it had undertaken on other
aspects of its proposed PSP5, it did not provide any evidence that customers
had been consulted specifically on the terms or content of the proposed
Customer Contract. This omission made it difficult for the
Economic Regulator to assess whether the Customer Contract reflected
customer views and met the consultation requirements set out in the
PSP Guideline.
The Economic Regulator also identified a small number of inconsistencies
with the Customer Service Code, the Industry Act, relevant Regulations, and
TasWater’s other proposed PSP5 policy documents. TasWater submitted an
updated draft Customer Contract which addresses these inconsistencies.
That updated version was circulated for consultation as part of the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report.
The Economic Regulator’s review does not extend to the legality or
enforceability of TasWater’s proposed Customer Contract, which remains
TasWater’s responsibility.

22.1.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
The Treasurer’s submission raises concerns about TasWater’s customer
contract and connection pricing approach, noting that a market‑based “price
on application” model reduces transparency and consistency for customers.
The submission highlights customer frustration in understanding and
challenging quoted prices and encourages the Economic Regulator to require
transparent, standardised costing methodologies that better protect
customers and reflect TasWater’s responsibilities as a monopoly service
provider.121
Sorell Council’s submission states: Council supports an easy-to-understand
Customer Contract that reflects household and developer needs. We
recommend including examples of common scenarios, such as new
connections or temporary service, to improve comprehension.

22.1.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator notes the concerns raised in the Treasurer’s
submission regarding TasWater’s connection pricing approach, including the
use of price on application and the transparency of quoted connection costs.
The Economic Regulator also notes Sorell Council’s support for a clear and
accessible Customer Contract and appreciates the suggestion to include

121 Submission from the Hon, Eric Abetz MP, Treasurer, page 2.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

211

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

illustrative examples, such as new connections or temporary services, to
enhance customer understanding.
However, the Economic Regulator does not propose amendments to the
version of the Customer Contract as circulated for consultation as part of the
Draft Report in response to these submissions.
The Customer Contract is not the primary regulatory instrument through
which connection classifications, costing methodologies or pricing structures
are established. Under the current regulatory framework, these matters are
primarily addressed through TasWater’s broader connection and pricing
instruments, including its connection-related policies and approved Price and
Service Plan. Those instruments establish the relevant charging
arrangements, including the application of standard charges and, where
relevant, price on application charges.
Accordingly, the Economic Regulator does not consider the Customer
Contract to be the appropriate vehicle for prescribing detailed connection
pricing methodologies, cost structures or charging approaches.
While the Customer Contract remains an important customer-facing
document, the matters raised in submissions are more appropriately
considered through TasWater’s broader pricing and policy framework, rather
than through amendments to the Customer Contract.
The Economic Regulator’s final decision is to approve the Customer Contract
provided at Appendix A6.1 to this Final Report, for submission as part of
TasWater’s final PSP5. The Customer Contract provided at Appendix A6.1 is
unchanged from the version circulated for consultation as part of the
Economic Regulator’s Draft Report.
Decision 22.1: To approve the Customer Contract, as provided in
Appendix A6.1 to this Final Report, for submission as part of
TasWater’s final PSP5.

22.2 Connections Policy
The Economic Regulator’s review of TasWater’s draft Connections Policy
identified several notable changes in the Policy’s drafting, including:

212

❑

the removal of sections on connection costs, non-standard
connections, land development, and connections outside serviced
land;

❑

the omission of definitions distinguishing standard and non-standard
connections;

❑

reduced detail on cost structures; and

❑

the exclusion of information on additional connection types.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater subsequently explained that its PSP4 initially supported fixed
pricing across various connection types, but that the entity adopted a market
based “Price on Application” approach, which rendered the previous
distinctions redundant. Furthermore, timing constraints prevented PSP4 from
fully reflecting these changes, but PSP5 would now incorporate them. Under
the Price on Application approach, TasWater prices all connection types
individually through contractor quotes, which is why the draft Connections
Policy for the fifth regulatory period no longer includes detailed
classifications, cost structures, or additional connection types from PSP4.
The Economic Regulator has considered TasWater’s explanations and is
satisfied that the revisions in the draft Connections Policy for the fifth
regulatory period are appropriately justified and reflect a consistent,
transparent approach to pricing and policy structure.
No matters were raised in submissions in relation to the draft Connections
Policy. The Connections Policy provided at Appendix A6.2 to this Final
Report is unchanged from the version circulated for consultation as part of
the Economic Regulator’s Draft Report.
Decision 22.2: To approve the Connections Policy, as provided in
Appendix A6.2 to this Final Report, for submission as part of
TasWater’s final PSP5.

22.3 Unconnected Property Service Charges Policy
(formerly Service Charges Policy)
A water service charge or a sewerage service charge is a charge levied on a
property owner where there is an ability to access a service even if there is
not yet a physical connection from the property to TasWater’s water and
sewerage infrastructure.
The Industry Act allows, but does not require, a regulated entity to impose
service charges for water services and sewerage services on owners of
property within serviced land. TasWater has decided to impose services
charges for both water and sewerage services and those liable to pay service
charges fall within the definition of customers under the Industry Act and are
therefore covered by TasWater’s Customer Contract.
Service charges on unconnected properties have historically applied in most
parts of Tasmania, having been imposed by the majority of local councils
until 2008, the three regional water corporations from 2009, and then
TasWater from 1 July 2013.
In its proposed PSP5, TasWater noted its intention to maintain its current
approach to the imposition of service charges. That is, that vacant land within
TasWater’s serviced land boundaries continues to be levied a service
charge. TasWater proposes that the service charge for water continue to be
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

213

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

equal to the fixed water charge for 20 mm connections and the service
charge for sewerage continue to be 60 per cent of the fixed sewerage charge
for a standard residential property. TasWater stated that its retention of this
charge is consistent with the findings of the Economic Regulator’s Inquiry into
the level of TasWater’s service charges, Final Report, December 2023.
TasWater also noted its intention to rename the term ‘service charges’ to
‘access charge for unconnected properties within serviced land’ as a means
of enhancing clarity for its customers. TasWater also proposed renaming its
fixed charges for water and sewerage as ‘water access charges’ and
‘sewerage access charges’.
To reduce potential confusion between ‘access charges’ for connected
properties and unconnected properties, in its Draft Report the Economic
Regulator recommended replacing the term ‘service charges’ with
‘unconnected property access charges’. However, as already discussed in
Chapter 11 of this Final Report, after considering the feedback received from
TasCOSS during consultation, the Economic Regulator requires that
‘service charges’ be renamed to ‘unconnected property service charges’ from
1 July 2026. This terminology is now reflected in TasWater’s Customer
Contract and associated policies. Other minor revisions have also been
made to the Unconnected Property Service Charges Policy, including
updated definitions and aligning terminology with other proposed policies.
TasWater agreed to these minor revisions.
Decision 22.3: To approve the Unconnected Property Service
Charges Policy, as provided in Appendix A6.3 to this Final Report,
for submission as part of TasWater’s final PSP5.

22.4 Sub-metering Policy
The Industry Act defines a customer and connection point to include a lot
owner and their lot, even if the lot is not directly connected to TasWater’s
infrastructure and instead receives water through pipes on a strata property.
As a result, strata title lot owners are liable for water and sewerage charges.
Multi-unit properties generally have a single connection point with a master
meter but contain areas with discrete water use. Unlike strata schemes, the
individual areas of water use are not deemed to be connection points to
TasWater’s infrastructure. On that basis, the multi-unit property owner is the
customer and is liable to pay for the water and sewerage services, rather
than the occupiers of multi-unit properties.
There are a number of options available to determine the charge that applies
to individual lots within a strata scheme, including the installation of
sub-meters and water measuring manifolds. To clarify how TasWater will
meter and bill strata schemes and multi-unit properties, TasWater is required

214

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

to have a Sub-metering Policy, which was required to be submitted to the
Economic Regulator as part of TasWater’s proposed PSP5.

22.4.1 Fourth regulatory period
The default position with regards to new strata schemes in the Sub-metering
Policy approved for the fourth regulatory period was to:
❑

Install a master meter only, with fixed and variable costs apportioned
on unit entitlements, if available from the Land Information System,
and billed to directly to lot owners or, if no unit entitlements are
available, billed to the body corporate.

❑

Install a master meter and provide sub-meters for installation at the
owners’ expense. The sub-meters will continue to be TasWater assets
and TasWater reads the meters and issues bills to the lot owner with
variable charges based on water supplied through the sub-meter.
TasWater also imposes a fixed charge for each sub-meter which is
deemed to be at least a 20 mm connection. Sub-metering common
property is optional. Where common property is sub-metered, the fixed
and variable costs are either apportioned on unit entitlements if these
are available from the Land Information System and billed directly to
lot owners or billed to the body corporate.

❑

Individual lots could also be connected to TasWater’s water main. This
option was available at TasWater’s discretion where there was no
common property, no interposing pipework or requirement for a master
meter.

❑

With regards to new multi-unit properties, the default position was to
install a master meter at the connection point, with the property owner
liable for both fixed and variable charges. The owner could sub-meter
the property at their own cost with TasWater supplying the sub-meters.
The property owner would then be liable for all the sub-meter fixed
and variable charges. The owner could also sub-meter the property
using meters provided by a third party. However, TasWater does not
read these meters, and they are not used for billing purposes.

22.4.2 TasWater’s proposal for the fifth regulatory period
TasWater proposed mandatory sub-metering for all new strata schemes and
multi-unit properties.
TasWater has stated that the change of policy supports customers being
charged on a ‘user-pays’ basis and reflects TasWater’s proposal to increase
the variable component of customer bills.122 TasWater contends that the
proposed changes require accurate water use data for all customers, which

122 TasWater Supporting Information S, page 7.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

is only possible if every site is individually metered rather than having usage
apportioned.
TasWater is also proposing that legacy strata schemes, that do not fit the
configurations specified in the Policy, are billed on a ‘fair and equitable’ basis
after consultation with property owners and body corporate(s).
TasWater has stated that it did not consult on the proposed changes to its
Sub-metering Policy. 123
The Economic Regulator considered that TasWater has not provided
evidence that it has the power to require new multi-unit properties to be
sub-metered and also considered that the Policy lacks clarity as to charging
methods for some of the connection configurations. Additionally, the Policy
does not include criteria by which to assess the fairness of billing
arrangements for legacy strata schemes which do not fit the configurations
specified in the Policy.
The proposed changes are significantly different to the previous
Sub-metering Policy. Therefore, to enable stakeholder consultation on the
proposed changes, the Economic Regulator prepared an amended draft
Sub-metering Policy that is consistent with legislative and regulatory
requirements. This amended Policy does not include TasWater’s proposed
mandatory sub-metering of new multi-unit properties. The amended Policy
also excludes the application of the Policy to legacy strata schemes, which
the Economic Regulator proposed are reviewed for billing fairness on a
case-by-case basis.

22.4.3 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater does not accept the draft amended Sub‑metering Policy included
in the Draft Report, submitting that the amendments weaken fair and
cost‑reflective charging outcomes and do not align with its PSP5 proposal.
TasWater maintains that mandatory sub‑metering for all new multi‑unit
properties and strata schemes is fundamental to equitable, usage‑based
charging, customer transparency and efficient water use.
TasWater objects to a number of specific changes made in the draft
amended Policy. These include changes to the structure and presentation of
the Policy; the removal of information relating to responsibility for, and
damage to, meters; the omission of a requirement for the Economic
Regulator to approve billing arrangements for legacy developments that are
excluded from the Policy due to specific site or configuration issues; and the
removal of an explicit statement that TasWater will not replace privately
owned sub‑meters. TasWater submits that these changes reduce clarity,

123 TasWater Supporting Information S, page 7.

216

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

increase legal and operational risk, and weaken customer and regulatory
oversight, particularly in relation to legacy arrangements.
TasWater also submits that the draft amended Policy’s removal of mandatory
sub‑metering for new multi‑unit developments undermines fair and
cost‑reflective pricing by permitting reliance on master metering
arrangements that can result in cross‑subsidisation between occupants.
TasWater argues that shared metering is a frequent source of customer
dissatisfaction, whereas sub‑metering enables individual units to be billed
transparently on the basis of actual water use.
In support of its original proposal, TasWater asserts that sections 56P
and 56TC of the Industry Act provide it with the power to require
sub‑metering at both the planning and certification stages for new multi‑unit
properties and strata schemes. TasWater further submits that this approach
is consistent with section 68(1)(b)(i) of the Industry Act, which contemplates
mandatory metering to support efficient pricing and recovery of variable
costs. TasWater argues that mandatory sub‑metering for new developments
aligns with established practice in other Australian jurisdictions, where
individual metering is routinely required for new multi‑unit and strata
developments, subject to defined practical exceptions.
In relation to legacy strata and multi‑unit developments, TasWater
acknowledges that historical arrangements may present complexity and
therefore require case‑by‑case assessment. TasWater submits that its
proposed Policy provides flexibility to achieve equitable charging outcomes in
these circumstances, with oversight by the Economic Regulator where
developments are excluded from the Policy due to specific issues. For these
reasons, TasWater re‑proposes its original PSP5 Sub-metering Policy.

22.4.4 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator reviewed metering requirements in other Australian
jurisdictions and found that only Queensland and the ACT mandate
sub-metering for multi-unit developments, and only where units have
separate titles (for example, strata schemes). In Queensland, this
requirement arises under the Plumbing and Wastewater Code made under
the Plumbing and Drainage Act 2018 (Qld), while the Water Supply (Safety
and Reliability) Act 2008 (Qld) allows water utilities to treat each lot as a
separate customer and bill on measured consumption.
In other jurisdictions, sub-metering is permitted and supported but is not
mandatory. For example, Sydney Water requires plumbing to be capable of
individual metering but does not require meters to be installed, and this
currently applies only to certain vertical strata developments. No jurisdiction
mandates sub-metering for multiple units on a single freehold title.
The Economic Regulator does not agree with TasWater’s interpretation of the
Industry Act and considers that the Industry Act does not confer a power to
mandate sub-metering. In particular:
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

217

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

Section 56P relates to planning permit requirements addressing
matters that may affect TasWater’s ability to provide services, and any
conditions imposed must be within the scope of planning legislation.
The requirements are part of a planning permit issued under The Land
Use Planning and Approvals Act 1993 and as such, must be within the
scope of that Act.

❑

Section 56TC allows conditions to be imposed on water and sewerage
related building or plumbing work to protect TasWater’s infrastructure.
Sub-metering relates to consumption allocation, which is an economic
matter, and is not necessary to enable connection or protect
infrastructure.

❑

Section 68 refers to determining the prices for regulated services, with
section 68(1)(b)(i) referring to the mechanisms that may be used
determine the volume of water consumed for calculating the variable
component of the mandatory two-part pricing for water services.

❑

Further, under section 3A, strata lot owners are customers and
therefore liable for fixed and variable water and sewerage charges. By
contrast, multi-unit property tenants do not meet the definition of a
customer under the Industry Act.

Under the current framework, TasWater measures the total volume of water
supplied to a strata scheme using a master meter at the connection point.
Where no sub-meters exist, water consumption is apportioned in accordance
with unit entitlements, as allowed for under regulation 17(1) of the
Pricing Regulations. Where a strata scheme is sub-metered, the volume
measured by each lot’s sub-meter is used for billing purposes. Regulation 16
of the Pricing Regulations provides for the water used in common property
areas to be billed to the lot owners.
For multi-unit properties on a single freehold title, the property owner is the
sole customer and is responsible for all fixed and variable charges based on
the master meter. While the owner may choose to sub-meter internally,
occupiers are not TasWater customers and TasWater does not bill on the
basis of those sub-meters.
For new multi-unit developments, TasWater may offer developers the option
of installing either a master meter only, or a master meter with sub-meters.
Where a master meter only is installed, developers may be advised to allow
for future sub-metering through private plumbing arrangements.
The Economic Regulator has redrafted the policy to remove any requirement
for mandatory sub-metering of strata schemes or multi-unit properties, while
allowing sub-metering at the discretion of property owners. In strata
schemes, this would require approval of the lot owners. Under this approach,
strata lots may be billed on actual consumption where sub-metered, together
with a share of common property water.

218

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

For multi-unit properties, any sub-metering arrangements remain private.
TasWater will not use private sub-meters for billing and is not responsible for
their maintenance or replacement.
The redrafted Policy also includes information regarding damage to
TasWater owned sub-meters and the Economic Regular approving the billing
arrangement of metering arrangement that are excluded from the Policy.
Decision 22.4: To approve the Sub-metering Policy, as provided in
Appendix A6.4 to this Final Report, for submission as part of
TasWater’s final PSP5.

22.5 Service Extension and Expansion Policy
The Economic Regulator’s review identified several notable changes
compared with the Service Extension and Expansion Policy approved for the
fourth regulatory period. TasWater has indicated that these changes are
intended to improve transparency, equity and clarity. Rather than relying on
Growth and Capacity Plans / master plans, which are not statutory or
regulatory documents, the Policy now relies primarily on the Capital Works
Program, using objective prioritisation criteria to determine when and where
infrastructure investment occurs. TasWater considers this approach to be
more transparent and equitable, as allowing individual customers to require
projects to be brought forward could disrupt the program and disadvantage
other customers who schedule developments around the published program.
TasWater has also clarified responsibilities for delivering works and
incorporated mandatory Infrastructure Agreements for extensions or
expansions outside the Capital Works Program. TasWater stated that these
agreements allocate scope, cost, timing, and responsibility, reducing
uncertainty, delays, and disputes for both TasWater and customers, and
aligning TasWater’s approach with mainland utilities. TasWater considers
that its updated draft Policy streamlines planning approvals, removes
unnecessary processes, and clearly distinguishes scenarios outside the
Policy, such as non-standard historical connections and service introductions
which continue to be managed under separate policies.
The Economic Regulator has accepted the information provided by TasWater
with minor drafting amendments to ensure consistency with the relevant
regulations. TasWater agreed to implement these changes.
No matters were raised in submissions in relation to the draft Service
Extension and Expansion Policy. The Service Extension and Expansion
Policy provided at Appendix A6.5 to this Final Report is unchanged from the
version circulated for consultation as part of the Economic Regulator’s
Draft Report.

submission as part of TasWater’s final PSP5.

22.8 Trade Waste Policy
Trade waste refers to the liquid waste generated by any industry, business,

Chapter 15 of this Final Report discusses TasWater’s current and proposed
trade waste arrangements in detail. As noted, TasWater has proposed a
major overhaul of its current risk-based charging system for commercial and
industrial customers. However, the Economic Regulator has stated that

change. Consequently, the current trade waste charging structure will remain
unchanged for the fifth regulatory period.

23

TasWater’s financial sustainability

TasWater has included a dedicated section on financial sustainability in its
proposed PSP5 for the first time, reflecting the ongoing importance of
ensuring its long-term resilience. TasWater noted that economic regulators in
other jurisdictions undertake financeability assessments to consider whether
their regulatory decisions provide sufficient revenue for an efficiently run
business to fund its operations over a pricing period.
On this basis, TasWater’s proposed PSP5 includes financeability testing,
based on IPART’s framework, using three metrics:
❑

Interest coverage ratio, which measures a business’ ability to service
its debt from cash flows;

❑

Funds from operations to net debt, which measures a business’ ability
to generate cash flows to service and repay debt; and

❑

Gearing, which measures a business’ leverage.

TasWater applied these tests to both its actual financial position under the
proposed PSP5 assumptions, and to a benchmark regulated business with
gearing of 60 per cent, consistent with the historical WACC allowance.
In the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator noted that TasWater’s actual
results presented in its proposed PSP5 indicated financial sustainability
across all metrics throughout the fifth regulatory period. This demonstrates
that TasWater is financially sustainable under its existing gearing and
financial arrangements. TasWater has indicated that when the benchmark
test is applied, it did not appear to meet the interest cover or funds from
operations to net debt metrics in any year. TasWater has attributed this
outcome to structural elements of the regulatory framework, including the
statutory return on equity for existing assets and the interaction between
inflation adjustments and regulatory depreciation.
The Draft Report noted the Economic Regulator has not sought to verify the
outcomes of TasWater’s financeability tests, nor has it conducted updated
tests based on its draft decisions.
Instead, the Economic Regulator noted that while the benchmark results
provide useful diagnostic insights, they are not automatic triggers for changes
to revenue allowances. As IPART found, when regulated prices are
considered sufficient for a prudent and efficient business to remain
financeable, the business should not be granted a price increase simply
because it has failed to act prudently or efficiently.124 In these circumstances,
IPART suggests businesses explore other options including the business

124 IPART, Final Report - Review of our financeability test, November 2018, page 65.

224

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

injecting equity or accepting lower returns, or seeking approval for net
present value neutral adjustments.
In its proposed PSP5, TasWater also referred to international experience,
particularly in the UK water sector, to highlight the risks associated with weak
financial resilience. TasWater proposed that financeability testing be formally
integrated into the determination process and called for a broader review of
the regulatory framework. The Draft Report acknowledged the importance of
financial sustainability, and the Economic Regulator indicated its willingness
to consider undertaking a review of the framework.

23.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
23.1.1 TasWater’s submission
TasWater submits that the Draft Report materially increases risks to its
financial sustainability due to the combined effect of changes to the regulated
asset base, regulatory depreciation, inflationary adjustments and returns on
capital. TasWater argues that these settings move financeability from a
theoretical concern to a practical constraint, limiting its borrowing capacity,
reducing resilience to risk, and constraining its ability to deliver the capital
program during PSP5.
TasWater states that financial sustainability is essential to customers’
long‑term interests, as it underpins efficient investment, service delivery and
affordability over time. While the Draft Report recognises the need for
significant capital investment, TasWater considers that the associated
revenue settings do not provide sufficient cash flow to deliver even the
reduced PSP5 capital program in a financially sustainable way.
Using benchmark financeability testing consistent with IPART’s framework,
TasWater submits that regulated revenue is insufficient for an efficient
business operating at benchmark gearing to remain financeable. TasWater
attributes this outcome primarily to the legislated cap on returns for existing
assets and insufficient regulatory depreciation once inflationary gain
adjustments are applied. TasWater further submits that under the Draft
Report these benchmark failures translate into real financing constraints, with
forecast financial metrics deteriorating across key credit indicators and
leaving minimal financial headroom, despite TasWater operating at gearing
levels well below those assumed in the regulatory framework.
TasWater identifies the treatment of inflation under the nominal framework as
a key driver of the financeability issue. It argues that while the framework
may be net present value‑neutral in theory, the combination of reduced
depreciation allowances and large inflationary gain deductions in a
high‑inflation environment creates material cash flow shortfalls during PSP5.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

225

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

TasWater submits that the Draft Report’s revenue settings do not support
borrowing sufficient to deliver the accepted capital program without
increasing financial risk or deferring necessary investment. TasWater states
it is not seeking excess returns, but a Final Determination that appropriately
recognises financeability impacts, ensures depreciation and regulated asset
base settings support cash flow, and avoids shifting costs and risks to future
customers. While acknowledging some issues are structural, TasWater asks
the Economic Regulator to reconsider aspects of the Draft Report that
materially exacerbate these risks.

23.1.2 Other submissions
Submissions from Infrastructure Partnerships Australia and the Water
Services Association of Australia raised similar concerns regarding
TasWater’s financeability and long‑term financial sustainability. Infrastructure
Partnerships Australia submits that TasWater’s proposed step‑change in
capital investment reflects sector‑wide pressures and that deferring
investment risks undermining long‑term financial sustainability. It cautions
that regulatory decisions overly focused on short‑term bill impacts can
weaken financeability, leading to inefficient delivery, higher future costs and
increased reliance on debt, and supports a review of the regulatory
framework, including the use of financeability testing.
The Water Services Association of Australia similarly submits that changes in
the Draft Report reduce TasWater’s revenue and borrowing capacity and
place its financial sustainability at risk, arguing that TasWater’s low measured
gearing is misleading given weak underlying cash flows and that the
cumulative effect of the Draft Report settings risks driving debt to
unsustainable levels and shifting costs to future customers, outcomes it
considers contrary to customers’ long‑term interests.
Simplot has indicated its concerns with the proposed expansion of
TasWater’s debt across the fifth regulatory period, and the lack of detail on
the intention and capability to pay down this debt in future periods.
Despite receiving submission from a number of TasWater’s owners, the
Economic Regulator notes that the Treasurer was the only owner to
comment on TasWater’s financial sustainability.
“The Government acknowledges that TasWater’s own modelling shows
pressure on some ratios under its preferred assumptions. However, these
assumptions reflect different price paths and regulatory settings from those
applied in the draft decision. In this context, the Government consider it
appropriate that the Regulator continues to examine financeability carefully as it
finalises its determination, to ensure that the regulatory framework supports a
prudently managed and efficiently operated business.” 125

125 Submission by the Hon Eric Abetz MP, Treasurer, page 3.

226

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

23.2 Economic Regulator’s considerations
The Economic Regulator acknowledges TasWater’s submission that its
financeability analysis indicates increasing pressure on a number of financial
metrics under the Draft Report settings. The Economic Regulator notes that
these results raise questions relevant to TasWater’s long‑term financial
sustainability. However, the Economic Regulator has not been provided with
sufficient information on the business decisions and planning assumptions
underpinning TasWater’s corporate financial model to independently verify
the results or to recalculate the outcomes of TasWater’s financeability tests
using the decisions in this Final Report.
The Economic Regulator further notes that TasWater advised it had not
sought a formal advice from the Tasmanian Public Finance Corporation
regarding the implications of the Draft Report on its borrowing capacity or
debt facilities.
In considering TasWater’s submission, the Economic Regulator has given
regard to the appropriateness of applying financeability tests developed and
used in other jurisdictions, including IPART’s framework. The Economic
Regulator notes that TasWater operates within a unique historical and
legislative context that differs materially from the utilities for which these tests
were designed. TasWater’s regulated asset base was transferred at no cost
when the entity was established, and the regulatory framework includes a
legislated return on capital for those legacy assets. TasWater’s unique
features and framework may mean that benchmark financeability tests may
not be directly transferable to the Tasmanian context without adaptation.
Given the scale and complexity of changes proposed as part of PSP5, the
Economic Regulator has not had sufficient time within this price investigation
to fully assess the appropriateness of applying such tests to TasWater.
Further, the Economic Regulator’s role is to assess the prudency and
efficiency of TasWater’s costs, including its capital program, and to determine
an expected level of expenditure to be reflected in TasWater’s notional
allowable revenue. The framework as it currently stands does not provide for
discretionary adjustments to regulatory depreciation, returns on capital or
other building blocks.
TasWater’s submission states that a major driver of the financeability
problem under the Draft Report is the treatment of inflation within the
regulatory framework. The Economic Regulator acknowledges these
concerns but considers that the inflation adjustment plays an important role in
preventing inflation from being double‑counted through both the nominal rate
of return and indexation of the regulated asset base. To assist stakeholders’
understanding, the Economic Regulator has included Chapter 10 in this Final
Report explaining the operation of the nominal framework and the role of the
inflation adjustment in more detail.
Furthermore, the Economic Regulator notes that the use of a nominal return
on capital and the subsequent need for the implementation of the inflationary
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

227

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

gain adjustment were introduced during the third regulatory period at
TasWater’s request. While the ongoing use of this element of the framework
will be considered during the review of TasWater’s regulatory framework, the
Economic Regulator considers that it is not good regulatory practice to
change elements of the framework every few periods to suit prevailing
conditions.
The Economic Regulator further notes that, following a price determination, it
is reasonable for a regulated business to revisit its internal budgeting,
financing arrangements and capital delivery plans, particularly where the final
determination differs materially from its initial proposal. As with other
commercial entities, responsibility for managing financial performance within
regulatory and market constraints rests with TasWater’s Board.
This is consistent with the approach taken by other utility regulators, such as
the Essential Service Commission, which considers that businesses are
primarily responsible for managing their financial performance and that
adjustments to their maximum prices should be a last resort.
“Businesses are primarily responsible for managing their financial performance
and our expectation is they will explore avenues to address any concerns prior
to seeking a financial viability adjustment to their maximum prices, which is a
last resort safeguard available in the pricing framework.” 126

In this context, the Economic Regulator notes that TasWater has access to
funding options beyond regulated revenue should its Board consider it
appropriate to accelerate capital delivery. These may include reinvestment of
equity with the agreement of TasWater’s owners to reduce or forego
dividends, targeted Tasmanian or Australian Government funding to support
policy objectives or economic development, and private funding contributions
where third‑party beneficiaries derive direct benefits from specific projects.
The Economic Regulator acknowledges that TasWater has raised broader
concerns regarding financeability and long‑term financial sustainability, which
are important matters warranting careful consideration. While these issues
cannot be resolved within the scope of the current price determination, the
Economic Regulator will consider the matters raised by TasWater, and by
other stakeholders, as part of a broader review of TasWater’s regulatory
framework beyond this determination.

126 Essential Services Commission, 2024 Water Price Review: Greater Western Water draft decision,

page 65.

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

24

Review of TasWater’s regulatory
framework

Australia’s water sector is facing increasing pressure from climate change,
population growth, ageing infrastructure and rising costs. Utilities are
responding by investing in climate-resilient infrastructure, diversifying water
supplies, renewing assets and strengthening operational resilience, while
also managing the growing complexity associated with digital systems and
cybersecurity.
Balancing affordability with long-term sustainability is a core challenge.
Rising costs from environmental compliance and infrastructure renewal need
to be balanced with cost of living pressures. Regulators are shifting towards
“lowest sustainable pricing” to fund critical upgrades without compromising
service quality or customer protection.
Australia is not alone in facing these challenges. The United Kingdom is
undergoing a fundamental reset of its water sector regulation in response
rising water bills, environmental pollution levels and high dividend
payments.127 The final report of the Independent Water Commission
identified the need for a stronger and integrated regulatory framework, with a
new mechanism for dedicated funding for asset maintenance, and the
adoption of a ‘supervisory approach’ in the way the regulator engages with
water utilities.128 Ireland implemented full economic regulation in 2023 in an
approach that links allowed revenue to performance obligations.
As previously noted, the Economic Regulator is responsible for the economic
regulation of the Tasmanian water and sewerage industry. The regulatory
framework covers licensing, customer service framework, price regulation,
compliance and performance monitoring, complaints handling and other
regulatory matters. In fulfilling its role, the Economic Regulator aims to
protect customers’ long-term interests and ensure water and sewerage
services are safe, environmentally responsible, efficient and sustainable. To
avoid duplication with other regulatory areas, it consults regularly with the
EPA, Director of Public Health and NRE Tas.
The role of the Economic Regulator in determining the maximum revenue
that TasWater is permitted to recover for customers for its regulated water
and sewerage services is to find that balance between smoothing price
changes, protecting the long-term interest of customers and enabling
TasWater to recover the efficient costs of delivering regulated services and
complying with its regulatory obligations.
The first regulatory price determination for TasWater was completed by the
Economic Regulator in 2012. The Final Report from that investigation
127 Water sector regulation, UK Parliament Committee of Public Accounts. 18 July 2025.
128 Independent Water Commission Final Report, 21 July 2025

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

229

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

highlighted that the scale of the challenges, such as aging infrastructure,
regulatory non-compliance and customer concerns could not be fully
resolved during the first regulatory period and may take several decades to
address.129
In its proposed PSP5, TasWater requested that the Economic Regulator
undertake a comprehensive review of its regulatory framework and approach
following the PSP5 price determination, with a view to implementing changes
from the sixth regulatory period, commencing in 2030. This timing would
allow the review to be conducted outside the constraints of the price
determination process and enable thorough consultation with stakeholders.
TasWater’s submission proposes a number of specific amendments to the
regulatory framework including:
❑

transitioning to a ‘real’ price framework, where annual prices are
indexed to actual inflation rather than fixed forecasts;

❑

updating the WACC methodology through annual adjustments to the
cost of debt;

❑

broader benchmarking of equity beta estimates;

❑

improving inflation forecasting by adopting practices used by other
regulators;

❑

extending the regulatory period from four to five years to improve
planning certainty and reduce regulatory costs; and

❑

regulating Category 3 and 4 trade waste prices to ensure fairness and
transparency.

As noted in the Draft Report, the Economic Regulator is willing to undertake
a comprehensive review of TasWater’s regulatory framework following the
PSP5 price determination, within the bounds of the current legislation. This
review will consider TasWater’s proposals, while ensuring that the framework
continues to meet the needs of customers.

24.1 Issues raised during consultation on the Economic
Regulator’s Draft Report
TasWater’s submission identifies a range of systemic issues that it considers
materially affect its long‑term financial sustainability, including the current
revenue‑setting approach, the treatment of regulatory depreciation, the
regulated asset base and the return on capital. TasWater submits that, taken
together, the current application of the framework presents a risk to its ability
to remain financially sustainable over time.
Similar concerns were raised by Infrastructure Partnerships Australia, which
supports TasWater’s call for a review of the regulatory framework ahead of
129 2012 Water and Sewerage Final Report.

230

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

the sixth regulatory period. Its submission emphasises the role of
financeability testing, noting that such tests are used in other jurisdictions to
help utilities manage financial risk. Infrastructure Partnerships Australia
considers that any financeability test adopted should be clear, robust and
transparent, and focused on protecting customers’ long‑term interests.
Several submissions emphasise the importance of considering long‑term
costs and intergenerational impacts. The Huon Valley Council submits that
constraining investment in the current period risks transferring higher costs to
future customers and the broader community. It recommends that the
Economic Regulator give greater weight to long‑run cost considerations in its
efficiency assessments and consider mechanisms, such as a capital
expenditure pass‑through arrangement or formal review triggers, to address
unforeseen compliance‑driven expenditure between regulatory resets.
The Tasmanian Chamber of Commerce and Industry similarly argues for a
more stable and ongoing approach to asset renewal. It recommends moving
toward a consistent renewal allowance that treats legacy asset replacement
as a core and recurring cost of service provision rather than a series of
episodic “catch‑up” programs. In TCCI’s view, this would smooth price
impacts over time, strengthen investment discipline and support more
efficient prioritisation of works.
Submissions from local government and regional stakeholders highlight the
need for a regulatory framework that can respond to growth pressures.
Sorell Council supports a comprehensive review of the framework to ensure
it can accommodate population growth, economic development and the
timely delivery of infrastructure. It expresses concern that demand and
capital expenditure may be underestimated in high‑growth areas and that
deferral of infrastructure investment could delay economic and community
benefits. The Council recommends greater flexibility to enable efficient
infrastructure delivery while maintaining affordability.
The Committee for Greater Hobart emphasises the broader economic and
social value of water, including its role in public health, community wellbeing
and key industries such as agriculture and hydro‑electric generation. Its
submission highlights the role of water pricing in signalling scarcity and
supporting sustainable infrastructure investment, and calls for continued
recognition of water’s long‑term value as Tasmania balances growth,
sustainability and climate‑related pressures.
The Treasurer’s submission acknowledges TasWater’s request for a
comprehensive review of the regulatory framework but considers that the
existing framework has delivered significant benefits, including statewide
price harmonisation, improved water quality and strong environmental
outcomes. While not opposing reform in principle, the Treasurer supports
change only where it is clearly necessary and evidence‑based. It considers
targeted reviews, such as the current trade waste consultation, to be an
appropriate mechanism for informing proportionate reform rather than
wholesale changes to the framework.
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

231

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

24.2 Economic Regulator’s decision
The Economic Regulator acknowledges the feedback received from
stakeholders regarding the operation and effectiveness of TasWater’s current
regulatory framework. Stakeholders raised a range of matters, including the
framework’s ability to support transparency, cost reflectivity and equitable
outcomes for customers over time. The Economic Regulator notes these
views and recognises the importance of ensuring that the regulatory
framework remains fit for purpose and responsive to changing
circumstances.
The Economic Regulator confirms that a review of TasWater’s regulatory
framework will be commenced following the completion of the current price
determination investigation. This sequencing will ensure that the review can
be undertaken independently of the immediate price setting process and
informed by the outcomes of the fifth regulatory period. Consultation with
stakeholders and customers will be a central element of the review, with
opportunities provided for meaningful engagement to inform the scope,
analysis and potential reforms arising from that process.
Decision 24.1: To undertake a comprehensive review of TasWater’s
regulatory framework, within the bounds of the current legislation.

A1 Glossary
Term

Meaning in this Final Report

access charge

Has the same meaning as “fixed charge” in the Pricing
Regulations and refers to a recurrent charge for the provision of a
regulated service to a customer but does not include a variable
charge.

AER

Australian Energy Regulator

asset life

The expected useful life of an asset, used to calculate regulatory
depreciation.

building block
approach

A method for setting regulated prices by combining key cost
components: operating expenditure, regulatory depreciation,
return on capital, and inflation adjustment.

capital expenditure
(capex)

Money a regulated entity invests in purchasing, constructing and
installing assets to deliver regulated water and sewerage services.

customer

As defined in the Industry Act.

customer contract

A contract between a regulated entity and a customer for the
provision of regulated services to the customer, which includes
standard terms and conditions of service.

Customer Service
Code

Refers to the Tasmanian Water and Sewerage Industry Customer
Service Code issued by the Economic Regulator. The Customer
Service Code sets the minimum service standards, conditions,
and customer protections that a regulated entity must comply with
when providing regulated water and sewerage services.

Customer Service
Regulations

Water and Sewerage Industry (Customer Service Standards)
Regulations 2019.

developer charges

Cost recovery fees imposed on developers to fund the expansion
or upgrade of water and sewerage infrastructure required to
service new developments. Includes headworks charges, assets
gifted by developers and cash payments made by developers to
the regulated entity for the construction of new reticulation works.

domestic sewage

Wastewater generated from everyday household activities (such
as bathing, cooking, cleaning, and toilet use) that contains organic
matter, nutrients, and other contaminants requiring treatment
before discharge into a regulated entity’s infrastructure.

double-dipping

The risk that a customer is charged twice for the same service,
particularly where both sewerage and trade waste discharge
factors are applied to the same volume of waste. Recognised as a
pricing principle in the regulations.

Economic Regulator

The Tasmanian Economic Regulator as appointed under the
Economic Regulator Act 2009.

234

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
Equivalent Tenement
(ET)

A measurement to estimate how much demand a property places
on the sewerage network, relative to an average residential
household under normal dry weather conditions.

existing assets

All assets transferred to a regulated entity before 1 July 2011
under section 41 of the Water and Sewerage Corporations
Act 2008.

fourth regulatory
period

1 July 2022 to 30 June 2026.

Industry Act

Water and Sewerage Industry Act 2008.

inflationary
adjustment

An adjustment applied to costs and asset values to account for
changes in the Consumer Price Index (CPI), ensuring costs are
recovered in real terms.

kilolitre (kL)

A metric unit of volume or capacity equal to 1 000 litres.

kPa

A metric measure of pressure.

limited water quality
customer

A customer receiving water from a supply which has a permanent
boil water alert in place or a customer receiving water from a
supply that the regulated entity has declared to not be of drinking
water quality.

limited water supply
customer

a customer:
❑

connected to a water main that periodically does not contain
water under positive pressure; or

❑

with a connection designed to provide low or intermittent
flow, such as where the customer has been required to
install, operate and maintain an individual tank or pump; or

❑

connected to a non-reticulation water main that is subject to
significant pressure variations due to either:
-

a pumped supply where the low pressure is below 50 kPa
and the high pressure is above 500 kPa; or

-

an inlet supply to a trunk reservoir such that when the
reservoir inlet valve is open the pressure is below 50 kPa;
or

receiving a supply that the regulated entity determines to be
inadequate.
long-run marginal
cost (LRMC)

The additional cost of supplying one more unit of a service when
all inputs can be adjusted over time, including infrastructure
expansion. Used as a basis for setting variable water charges.

miscellaneous fees
and charges

Charges for services other than standard water and sewerage
provision, such as connection applications, meter testing, and
development-related services.

net present value
(NPV)

Is a way to work out what future costs or benefits are worth in
today’s terms. It does this by applying a discount rate, which
adjusts for things like inflation, risk and opportunity cost.

new assets

All assets purchased or constructed by a regulated entity since
1 July 2009.

The actual price of an item during a specific year, meaning,

The ongoing costs a regulated entity incurs to deliver day-to-day

Means “Price on Application” and refers to a charge that is not

Spending that is necessary for providing regulated services, as

The value of assets used to provide regulated services, on which

A person holding a licence under the Industry Act [currently, the

regulatory
depreciation

The amount that a regulated entity is allowed to recover each year

The additional cost of supplying one more unit of a service when

Changes to the structure of water and sewerage charges, such as

Cost of Capital
(WACC)

The WACC is used to set a rate of return on capital, with the value
of capital determined by the value, for regulatory purposes, of the
assets (ie the regulated asset base) the regulated entity uses to
provide regulated services. The WACC comprises the cost of
equity plus the cost of debt, weighted by the contribution of each
to the capital the business uses to fund its operations.

A2 Regulatory framework
Since 1 July 2013, TasWater has owned, controlled and operated water
supply and sewerage systems in Tasmania. TasWater manages all aspects
of the water supply chain from dams and reservoirs to customers’ property
connections. TasWater also manages sewerage systems from customer
sewer connections to wastewater treatment and disposal.
Economic regulation of the Tasmanian water and sewerage industry, and the
conduct of the price determination in particular, are governed by the:
❑

Water and Sewerage Corporation Act 2012;

❑

Water and Sewerage Industry Act 2008;

❑

Water and Sewerage Industry (Pricing and Related Matters)
Regulations 2021 (Pricing Regulations);

❑

Water and Sewerage Industry (Customer Service Standards)
Regulations 2019 (Customer Service Regulations);

❑

Customer Service Code; and

❑

Price and Service Plan Guideline (PSP Guideline).

Currently TasWater is the only licensed entity in Tasmania. The licence
places a number of regulatory obligations on TasWater through reference to
various regulatory instruments, such as codes and guidelines, as well as
requiring the preparation of management plans.

Water and Sewerage Corporation Act
The Water and Sewerage Corporation Act established TasWater by
transferring the liabilities, employees and water and sewerage assets from
the previous regulated entities to TasWater. The Water and Sewerage
Corporation Act also ensured the continued application of the Economic
Regulator’s 2012 Price Determinations and Price and Service Plans to
TasWater until the end of the first regulatory period on 30 June 2015.

Water and Sewerage Industry Act
The Industry Act is the primary legislative instrument governing the economic
regulation of the water and sewerage industry in Tasmania.
In relation to pricing, the Industry Act provides for:
❑

an independent regulator (the Economic Regulator) for the industry
with clear accountabilities and responsibilities to ensure effective and
efficient outcomes for the sector and the protection of customers;
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

239

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

independent pricing regulation with a regulated entity (now TasWater
only) required to submit a proposed Price and Service Plan to the
Economic Regulator which outlines the services, revenue
requirements and operational requirements of the regulated entity. The
Economic Regulator bases its price determination on an assessment
of the proposed Price and Service Plan submitted by the regulated
entity; and

❑

the Economic Regulator to be guided by legislated pricing principles
when making a price determination.

Pricing Regulations
In addition to the pricing principles set out in the Industry Act, the Pricing
Regulations contain additional pricing principles in relation to:
❑

pricing zones (nodal pricing);

❑

the structure of service introduction charges;

❑

the calculation of developer charges;

❑

the treatment of contributed assets; and

❑

the basis for setting fixed and variable charges (including the removal
of free water allowances).

Part two of the Pricing Regulations outline matters to which the Economic
Regulator is to have regard in making a price determination (for the purposes
of section 66(2)(f) of the Industry Act). Specifically:
❑

maximum allowable revenue;

❑

pricing zones;

❑

estimates of changes of prices over life of plan;

❑

developer charges policy; and

❑

service introduction charges policy.

Part three of the Pricing Regulations set out the following:

240

❑

additional pricing principles;

❑

full cost recovery from certain contracts;

❑

no double-dipping;

❑

contributed assets;

❑

service introduction charges;

❑

fixed charges;

❑

imposition of variable charges;

❑

when variable charges may not be imposed;

❑

meter of amounts of variable charges;
Tasmanian Economic Regulator

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report
❑

developer charges;

❑

amount of developer charges; and

❑

miscellaneous fees and charges.

The Pricing Regulations set out the criteria that TasWater’s fixed and variable
charges must comply with:
❑

TasWater’s fixed charges must reflect costs that are reasonably
attributable to providing the service to the property to which the charge
relates (Regulation 14);

❑

TasWater must charge a variable charge for a water service and may,
but is not required to, charge a variable charge for a sewerage service
(Regulation 15(2)); and

❑

If a variable charge is imposed for a regulated service, it must be
charged in respect of each unit of water delivered to, or sewage
removed from, the property to which the charge relates
(Regulation 15(3)).

Customer Service Regulations
The Customer Service Regulations set out minimum service standards for
the water and sewerage industry. TasWater is required to comply with the
Customer Service Code, which was last updated in July 2025.
Under the Customer Service Code, TasWater is required to:
❑

meet the customer-related standards, procedures, practices and
conditions for regulated services as set out in this Code; and

❑

develop, issue and comply with a customer charter which meets the
procedural and substantive requirements of this Code and sets out the
minimum service standards.

Industry regulators comprise of the Tasmanian Economic Regulator, Director
of the Environment Protection Authority (EPA), Director of Public Health and
the Secretary of the Department of Natural Resources and Environment
Tasmania (NRE Tas).
Additional legislation, including the Tasmanian Environmental Management
and Pollution Control Act 1994, Public Health Act 1997, Fluoridation
Act 1968, Water Management Act 1999, and the Water Management (Safety
of Dams) Regulations 2025 impose a range of compliance and regulatory
obligations on TasWater.

Pricing Principles
The Industry Act and Pricing Regulations contain pricing principles that set
out a range of criteria for water and sewerage pricing, including:

Tasmanian Economic Regulator

241

2026 Water and Sewerage Price Determination Investigation – Final Report

242

❑

TasWater should be allowed to recover the efficient costs of providing
regulated water and sewerage services and comply with its regulatory
requirements.

❑

Where reasonable, prices charged to specific customers or customer
classes should reflect the direct cost of servicing them.

❑

A customer must not be charged twice for the same cost – if the cost
is recovered through one charge it cannot be included in another.

❑

Prices should encourage TasWater to operate efficiently, reduce costs
and improve productivity.

❑

Prices for water services must include separate fixed and variable
prices to recover the fixed and variable costs of delivering water
services.

❑

TasWater is entitled to earn a return on the assets it uses to provide
regulated services.
